sentimos-perseguidos [accessed 30 November 2011].
34 ‘Esto se esta transformando en una guerra que no tendria que haber existido’ (my transla-
tion). See Perfil, 1 April 2010, http://www.perfil.com/contenidos/2010/04/01/noticia 0022.html
[accessed 4 May 2010]. 7
35 *Yo no entendia nada. Uno sabia qué era ser adoptado pero no hijo de desaparecidos. Es-
taba totalmente perdido’ (my translation). The testimony comes from the 21 September 2010
edition of the television show 678. An excerpt can be found here http://www.youtube.com/
watch?v=YtUPBFOhCs8 [accessed 18 November 2010].

PARADOXES OF BLOOD ZS)
teeth of his dog, knowing that the brush was one oft he elements that the judge
would order confiscated as part of the genetic examination. If for a long time
Sandoval could not deal with his conflicted background, eventually he learned
how to cope with it. From his personal website, he now proudly parades the
condition of recovered grandson:
My parents left me the biggest family a human being can possible have.
Despite the pain, I have a hundred siblings [....] with some of them it works
as if we had known each other since birth. I have thousands of aunts and
uncles. They are the ones who were with my mum and dad. With some of
them they shared their activism. With others, they shared captivity. Today, I
have many grannies and mums who are willing to embrace me as soon as
they see my face [...] I wouldn’t change this family for anything.*°
As a gesture of commitment to his brand new affiliations, Sandoval signed the
passage Nieto 84 (‘Grandson 84’). It could be argued that his testimony is just
an expression of the ‘happy narrative’ supported by the relatives, a mere itera-
tion of the script that underwrites the illusion that the recovery of biological
backgrounds is enough guarantee of fulfilment. Nonetheless, it also becomes
clear that the ties that Sandoval refers to do not correspond to any traditional
family. In fact, his testimony shows how someone who first grew up within a
family structure forged through violence was able to recreate alternative engage-
ments to cope with the ongoing effects of trauma.
Eventually, Sandoval managed to work through his traumatic story by means
of an intense political activism. As with many others, he found a new ‘place in
the world’ under the sponsorship of the Kirchnerist administrations, which
promoted the militancy of the recovered children as part of their own political
legitimacy. Sandoval’s experience can also be read alongside the idea of ‘mourn-
ing activism’, developed by Carrie Hamilton in her scholarship on trauma,
memory and political pleasure where she argues that mourning can lead to
merriment when processed through activism.*’ In this vein, Sandoval’s case
reveals how certain forms of public memory, usually associated with trauma,
36 ‘Mis viejos me dejaron la familia mas grande que un ser humano puede tener. A pesar del
dolor, yo tengo 100 hermanos [...] con algunos de ellos es como si nos conociéramos de cuna a
cuna. Tengo miles de tias y miles de tios. Son los cumpas que estuvieron con papa y mama. Algu-
nos muy allegados, porque compartieron con mis viejos la militancia y otros compartieron el cau-
tiverio. Hoy tengo un monton de abuelas y madres que con tan solo verme, me llenan de abrazos
y besos [...]. Yo a esta familia, no la cambio por nada’ (my translation). http://alejandropedrosan-
dovalfontana.blogspot.com/ [accessed 7 November 2010].
37 Carrie Hamilton, ‘Activist Memories: The Politics of Trauma and the Pleasures for Politics’,
in The Future of Memory, ed. Richard Crownshaw, Jane Kilby and Antony Rowland (New York:
Berghahn Books, 2010), pp. 267-80 (p. 272). I am also indebted to Hamilton for accompanying
me in the subsequent exploration of the parallel between recovered grandchildren and transgendered
individuals in the following pages.

26 CECILIA SOSA
can be reversed through political involvement. A process of ‘conversion’ enabled
him to reinvent his future while endorsing the official lineage ofl oss.
Working against simple closure, Sandoval’s public presentations do not stage
a ‘happy script’ but a precarious mixture of vulnerability and empowerment.
His trajectory shows how a recovered ‘grandson’ could manage to work through
his traumatic past to find himself fully immersed within an extended ‘family of
choice’. Ultimately, Sandoval’s story points towards an extended conception of
the family in which a broad array of uncles, aunts, grannies and siblings are
embraced. His case also draws attention to how, in the wake of violence, the
language of kinship is by no means exhausted by the mandates of blood.
Rather, traditional forms of tie might be re-inhabited through non-normative
forms of intimacy, support and care. Sandoval’s dramatic reinvention as public
persona invites us to read the stories of the recovered children alongside the
experience of transgendered individuals who also adopt new names, solidarities,
affections and a whole collection of expectations in relation to the past and
future. While considering Felix Bruzzone’s novella Los topos in Chapter 6, |
will explore further a fictional variant of this transitioning. For now I suggest
that these processes of ‘conversion’ can be read as a ‘coming out’ within an
expanded lineage of loss. In this process, Sandoval’s political militancy worked
as a form of physical and affective reparation that encompassed his ‘transition-
ing’ as recovered grandson.
As Horacio Gonzalez suggests, politics should be conceived as the opportu-
nity to create ties that go beyond primary bonds, which he defines as non-nor-
mative ‘families of choice’.* * Drawing upon this, the main question that under-
pins this book is how to recreate a creative politics of memory, which does not
compromise the need to establish the biological identity of the abducted children
(named by Abuelas as the ‘right to the identity’), and which gives room to the
expanded affiliations that have emerged in the wake of violence. With these
ideas in mind, this book aims at exploring the unconventional, queer bonds that
have emerged from loss. As already shown, these alternative ties do not admit
simple solutions. Each chapter of this book examines different expressions of
these hushed non-biological attachments that refuse to be hosted by existent
frameworks. Current times demand novel critical interventions. Only by think-
ing about the failure of traditional narratives of kinship will it be possible to
address the forcible but still hopeful changes brought about in the realm of the
family by dictatorship violence. This is the invitation that I shall respond to in
the following pages.
38 Gonzalez, ‘Filiaciones Argentinas’.

N
Black Humour and the Children of the Disappeared
In December 2010 H.I.J.O.S., the organisation created by the children of the
disappeared, celebrated its 15th anniversary.' A big party took place at a pub in
San Telmo, a historic and bohemian area of Buenos Aires. ‘Our only revenge is
to be happy’, ran the invitation flyer.? Since the group was established in 1995
many fundamental aspects have changed in Argentina. While in the early 1990s
those responsible for the dictatorship’s crimes were free or had been ‘pardoned’,
by 2010 huge trials were taking place. In 2005 the Supreme Court nullified the
laws of impunity, and prosecutions were allowed once again. Today, 1,424
military personnel are involved in cases across all the country.’ In this context,
H.1I.J.O.S. became a prosecutor in court.’ Although these testimonies had most-
ly symbolic value — the descendants were children at the time of their parents’
kidnapping — the courts became a major stage for witnessing the younger gen-
eration’s accounts of the ongoing effects of trauma.
While attending these trials in December 2009 and January 2010, I was
impressed by the affective investment of the descendants’ testimonies. All those
individual performances were strongly embedded in a collective spirit. | was
especially moved by the words of Paula Maroni, a founder member of H.I.J.O.S.,
whose father was kidnapped and murdered and whose mother survived after
three months of captivity and torture. She declared:
My childhood was sad and grey. I wasn’t able to put the situation into words.
In 1995 I joined H.I.J.0.S. We still had no answers but we were happy: we
were together; we could share feelings, stories, everything. The organisation
saved my life.°
1 The name of the group, which means children in Spanish, is an acronym that combines the
generational position with a strategy: ‘H.I.J.O.S. por la Igualdad y la Justicia contra el Olvido y
el Silencio’ (Children for Equality and Justice, against Forgetting and Silence).
2 ‘La tinica venganza es ser felices’ (my translation).
3 This information was provided by Argentina’s Centro de Estudios Legales y Sociales (CELS).
4 Unlike the 1980s trials that condemned the military junta, the new series includes the role
of the querellantes (the plaintiffs). Hence, the accusations are brought forward not only by the
state, but also by individuals and civil organisations, including H.I.J.O.S.
5 Maroni’s testimony was given on 15 December 2009 at the Comodoro Py Tribunal. She
testified as part of the so-called ABO circuit that investigates human rights violations that took

28 CECILIA SOSA
In this chapter, I wish to focus on this unusual form of ‘happiness’. | am in-
terested in exploring the journey that goes from ‘grey’ childhoods to the sense
of empowerment that the descendants acquired in H.1.J.O.S. Maroni’s testi-
mony already addresses a crucial concern of my research: the possibility that
the transmission of trauma could not only imply sorrow and pain, but also
delight and joy. I seek to explore how and why the fact of being part of a col-
lective might have allowed the pnt of non-normative pleasures from
the experience of loss.
My focus on pleasure contests adifionsl approaches, in particular those
which visualise the effects of trauma as ‘paralysing’ for the descendants. For
instance, in the early 1990s Elizabeth Jelin argued that ‘the experience ofl oss,
and the veneration oft he victims and their memory could become a frightening
and paralyzing mechanism for the young, who have not personally lived through
that experience’.® By critically engaging with this perspective, | would like to
show how the sense of humour that circulated within H.I.J.O.S. provided the
members with a new language to cope with loss. In the first section, I shall
explore the continuities between the Madres’ performance and the escraches,
the new form of activism developed by the descendants. I will contest Diana
Taylor’s idea of the ‘DNA of performance’ by showing how H.1.J.O.S. ex-
panded the experience of mourning beyond the margins of those considered to
be the ‘true’ victims. In the second section, | will examine how the sense of
humour cultivated by the descendants sheds light on a non-normative culture
of mourning. Through glimpses into the rituals and ceremonies of the group,
tracked via interviews and archival research, I will argue that humour provided
H.1.J.O.S. with a new platform for survival. I will discuss these arguments
through a collection of episodes and scenes witnessed during field trips to Ar-
gentina, conducted in April and December 2009, and January and October 2010.
From a historic perspective, I will focus particularly upon the period from the
constitution of the group in 1995 until the late 1990s when H.I.J.O.S.’ escraches
were at the heart of this history. I will then consider the challenges that emerged
during the Kirchner governments, when the organisation of the descendants
experienced a new turn.
Emotions and activism: just a DNA performance?
The activism developed by the Children of the Disappeared in Argentina has changed
the scene — and the mood — of human rights interventions worldwide. Hugo Vez-
place at the detention centres of Club Atlético, El Banco and Olimpo. Although no official record-
ings are available, attendees are able to take notes. Apart from ABO, H.I.J.O.S. was also the
prosecutor at the ESMA case, which tried 18 military figures accused of the disappearance, torture
and execution of 88 people in the former detention camp.
6 See Jelin, ‘The Politics of Memory’, p. 52.

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 29
zetti, Susana Kaiser, Diana Taylor and Diego Venegas are only some of the na-
tional and international scholars who have presented various theoretical frameworks
for approaching H.1.J.0.S.’ demonstrations in many productive ways.’ Still, most
of these analyses have focused on the ways in which the escraches embodied a
new flair for performance. The descendants’ loud and confrontational occupations
of the public sphere, which denounced unpunished crimes, already disturbed the
emphatic but relatively quiet interventions of other groups of relatives. Drawing
upon these perspectives, | am interested in exploring further the broader combina-
tion of affects that animated the children’s parades. In particular, it has always
intrigued me that the escraches, far from offering a spectacle of sorrow and pain,
displayed the intensity and the energy of a celebration.
I will first take a small detour to address Taylor’s accounts of H.I.J.O.S.’
activism, which constitutes the point of departure of my own analysis. In her
famous scholarship on cultural memory in the Americas, she argues that H.I.J.O.S.
developed a kind of performance that revisited their parents’ struggle.* Taylor’s
conviction appears to be related to the way in which the new generation used
visual evidence in continuation with other relatives’ associations. While the
Madres carry the ID pictures of their missing children around their necks ‘like
a second skin’, the Abuelas rely on DNA testing to reconstruct the lineage bro-
ken by the military. Drawing from this repertoire, Taylor argues that H.I.J.O.S.’
escraches inverted the logic of the evidence: in using archival photos to target
the perpetrators who have not been punished, the children ‘quote’ the Madres,
representing themselves as the conduit of memory. Thus, they exposed the
perpetrators to heightened public awareness at a time when legal justice was
not available.’ As much as Madres and Abuelas politicised affiliative bonds, the
new generation also emphasised their identity as based on biological kinship."°
In this vein, Taylor argues that the distinctive ways in which the victims have
sought to establish ‘truth and lineage’ were based on a chain of ‘presentation
and representation’ in which the scientific and the performative became entan-
gled.!' Ultimately, Taylor contends that the post-dictatorship period was embed-
ded in a biological and repetitive paradigm of public presentation based on
blood: ‘This representational practice of linking the scientific and performative
claim is what I call the DNA of performance,’ she writes.”
7 See Susana Kaiser, ‘Escraches: Demonstrations, Communication and Political Memory in
Post-dictatorial Argentina’, Media, Culture and Society, 24 (2002), 499-516; Hugo Vezzetti ‘Ac-
tivismos de la memoria: el escrache’, Punto de Vista, 62 (1998), 1-7; and Diego Benegas “If There’s
No Justice...”: Trauma and Identity in Post-dictatorship Argentina’, Performance Research, 16.1
(2011), 20-30.
8 Taylor, The Archive and the Repertoire, p. 164.
9 Taylor, The Archive and the Repertoire, p. 178.
10 Taylor, The Archive and the Repertoire, p. 180.
11 Taylor, The Archive and the Repertoire, p. 175.
12. Taylor, The Archive and the Repertoire, p. 171.

30 CECILIA SOSA
Taylor’s perspective is indeed provocative and theoretically challenging.
Nevertheless, as already suggested while considering the Madres’ case, her
analysis tends to stage a privatising mode*of conceiving the transmission of
trauma, which ultimately champions the ownership of memory in the hands
of a ‘wounded family’ of victims. In order to contest this framework, I wish
to raise different questions. For instance, I aim to explore whether this en-
tanglement between the scientific and the performative could have also man-
aged to build affiliations beyond familial settings. I seek to examine how the
performance of blood queries traditional forms of kinship, liberating ex-
ploratory territories that have not yet been fully addressed. By engaging with
H.1.J.O.S.’ public and private routines, | want show how humour emerged as
a strategy for displacing familial settings, a possibility that has been over-
looked by Taylor’s approach.
A collective ‘coming out’
In 1997 a group of young people, almost all adolescents, stood on one of the
busiest corners in the centre of Buenos Aires in front of the Mitre Hospital. It
was raining heavily but the group kept on singing and jumping for hours. The
target was Jorge Magnacco, the obstetrician who had been in charge of the
clandestine maternity ward that operated at ESMA during the dictatorship. As
had been recently proved, Magnacco assisted at the birth of at least 15 babies
who were stolen by the military or their associates. In July 2012 the obstetrician
was judged to have been one of the main heads of a ‘systematic plan of robbery
of newborn babies’ and condemned to ten years’ imprisonment, consolidated to
fifteen because of previous trials.'* In 1997 this criminal structure was still
unexposed and Magnacco was a respected obstetrician working at the main city
hospital. After the controversial pardon granted to those already imprisoned by
President Carlos Menem in 1990, the country was living through a time of
impunity. In this context, H.I.J.O.S. made the first public intervention. Every
Friday the group paraded from Magnacco’s house to the hospital. ‘No bajes las
escaleras, no tomes el ascensor al ladito de tu casa, esta viviendo un represor’
(‘Don’t walk down the stairs, do not take the lift, a repressor is living next to
you’), they sang. Eventually, Magnacco resigned. The episode is now known
as H.I.J.O.S.’ first escrache. Afterwards, the group’s techniques became more
belligerent and coordinated. The demonstrations incorporated loudspeakers,
music, gigantic puppets, posters, massive signals and yellow graffiti to denounce
the unpunished repressors and expose their flagrant crimes in the face of the
entire society. Yet the slogan remained the same: ‘If there is no justice..., there
'3 See http://www. lanacion.com.ar/1488272-videla-fue-condenado-a-50-anos-de-prision-por-
el-robo-de-bebes [accessed 7 July 2012].

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED ol
is escrache!’'* These parades became massive, festive and mobile spectacles of
public shaming, which highlighted the absence of legal justice.
The queer scholar Eve Kosofsky Sedgwick suggests a transformative link
between shame and performativity by arguing that shame can broach fresh grounds
for thinking about politics: ‘Part of the interest of shame is that it is an affect that
delineates identity — but delineates it without defining it or giving content. Shame,
as opposed to guilt, is a bad feeling that does not attach to what one does, but to
what one is.’'* Sedgwick’s perspective offers a fruitful displacement from an
essential definition of identity (“what one is’) to a more fluid notion of the self
as being with others (‘what one does’). This framework is illuminating in con-
sidering the descendants’ activism in Argentina. Drawing on this contagious and
ultimately bonding character of shame, I would like to suggest that H.I.J.O.S.’
escraches can be perceived of s theatrical displays of shame, which enabled the
descendants to progress from the private experiences of rejection that they ex-
perienced during their childhoods to a collective action of public shaming redi-
rected at the military repressors. More than that, H.I.J.O.S.’ activism also provides
us with a good case to explore how forms of injurious or hate speech can some-
times be reversed.'* I suggest that this reworking of shame was crucial in spread-
ing the militant energy beyond those directly affected by violence.
By the late 1990s the escraches had achieved a mythical status in the coun-
try. Not only were the young activists involved but also neighbours who often
adopted a leading role in the initiative. ‘The neighbours would realise that the
old man with the nice smile who lived in the same building used to torture, rape
and steal babies from the detention camps,’ said Carlos Pisoni, a pioneer activ-
ist of the group.'’ Andrés Centrone, another member of the group, laughed when
recalling the first reactions of ‘older’ associations to the new wave of activists:
‘The Madres complained that our escraches were a cocoliche [pastiche].’ Al-
though Centrone has no missing relatives, he gained extra status by acting as
H.I.J.O.S.’ DJ. For many years he escorted the parades from the top of a truck,
making people dance in the streets of the city. For the anarchist musician with
no missing relatives, H.I.J.O.S.’ celebratory spirit was crucial: ‘It showed our
way of doing things,’ he said."*
14 ‘Si no hay Justicia .... jhay escrache!’
1S Eve Kosofsky Sedgwick, ‘Queer Performativity: Henry James’s The Art of the Novel’,
GLO: A Journal of Lesbian and Gay Studies, \.1 (1993), 1-16 (p. 12).
16 Judith Butler, Excitable Speech (London and New York: Routledge, 1997), p. 65.
17 ‘Los vecinos se daban cuenta de que el sefior de sonrisa agradable que vivia en el mismo
edificio torturaba, violaba y robaba bebés de los centros de dentencién’ (my translation). My in-
terview with Carlos Pisoni took place on 17 April 2009 at a coffee shop near Plaza de Mayo in
Buenos Aires. All the quotations included in this chapter come from this interview.
18 ‘Mostraban nuestra manera de hacer cosas’ (my translation). My interview with Andrés
Centrone took place on 23 December 2009 at the former ESMA, where he works as a public tour
guide. All the quotations included in this chapter come from this interview.

LoN o CECILIA SOSA
This festive form of public condemnation helped to create audiences that
exceeded those demonstrating in the streets. Widely covered by the media,
the escraches not only confronted the lack ofj ustice but also the individual-
ist logic that characterised the long decade of Menem’s administrations
(1989-99). This particularly authoritarian brand of neoliberalism ‘generated
unprecedented social exclusion, reflected in the emergence of the newly poor,
the disenfranchised middle class, and growing popular opposition during the
nineties’, as Brenda Werth asserts in her rich exploration of theatre and per-
formance in Argentina.'? Increasing levels of marginalisation accompanied
extravagant consumption by a minority wealthy sector. While the belief in
traditional political parties was also undermined, traditional leaders tended
to be perceived as ineffective and corrupt. The escraches became a centre of
attraction for many youngsters with no political involvement. By embracing
a broad sector of social disadvantage, H.I.J.O.S. prepared the ground for the
popular reaction that exploded during 2001’s massive political, economic
and social crisis. Almost unexpectedly, this energy provided a more inclusive
language for loss. To some extent, the escraches worked as a form of ‘com-
ing out’, in which an expanded society was invited to play a part. While
transforming the act of shaming into a public celebration, the escraches
worked as a confrontational and still joyful enactment of a brand-new form
of popular justice.
Partners in loss
During my series of interviews with members of H.I.J.0.S., | was surprised to
notice that most of the descendants employed the language of the closet to ar-
ticulate the feelings of shame that they had experienced during their childhood.
Once inside the group, those abject feelings of injury regarding their conflicted
backgrounds were reversed. The moment of joining the group emerged as a
turning point within individual biographies, a significant life-event that changed
the ways in which the descendants conceived their own stories and those of the
others. In close contact with their peers, the descendants also learned to rebuild
and reframe their relations with their missing parents in a sort of reverberation
from one to the other. Most of the activists agreed in regarding their first en-
counters with the group as a moment of revelation and expansion. This is how
Maroni addressed this moment during our interview:
Joining H.I.J.O.S. was a total ecstasy, one of the happiest moments of my
life. It was euphoria. Once you were inside you got a feeling of plenitude,
of having found your place in the world. Before that, I used to feel guilty
19 Brenda Werth, Theatre, Performance, and Memory Politics in Argentina (New York:
Palgrave, 2010), p. 8.

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 33
and I didn’t know how to deal with my own need for punishment. H.1.J.O.S.
changed everything.”°
Within the group the descendants could deal with the exacerbated anxieties of
being stigmatised as part of the shameful legacy of the military violence. Dur-
ing our interviews, most oft he activists would stress that ‘just a gaze or a smile’
was enough to build a sense of companionship and solidarity between them.
In tune, the first newsletter delivered by H.I.J.0.S. was entitled La mirada (‘The
Gaze’), clearly acknowledging the sense of mutual recognition that circulated
‘beyond words’. The organisation provided its members with a strong sense of
belonging that was eventually articulated as ‘the place in the world’, as Maro-
ni described. Drawing upon their similar stories, the descendants found in
H.1.J.O.S. a common groun.d4 o pass from isolated victims to partners in loss.
Pisoni, still one of the voices of the group, made a provocative association to
illustrate this process:
Although it cannot be strictly compared, being a son of the disappeared
worked a little bit like being an alcoholic. For someone who had to lie and
hide his origin, H.I.J.O.S. initiated a new process. At the beginning you are
ashamed of telling it, then when you talk about it in the group, you find
someone who has experienced the same and you feel relieved. Then you can
start telling others your story, until you can finally say ‘Hey, I am a son of
the disappeared!’.*!
The comparison was indeed provocative. It vividly portrayed how the group
‘ultimately sponsored a transition from rejection, guilt and denial — and various
forms of public repudiation also experienced by other neglected communities
—toa cherished state of public pride. This environment enabled its members to
rework earlier disgraceful identities as ‘sons’ and ‘daughters’, while collec-
tively embracing the brand-new subjectivity of ‘children of the disappeared’.
The seminal feelings of self-importance and even arrogance that eventually
emerged within the group could be compared with the expansion depicted by
queer biographies after the ‘coming out’. As is well known, coming out is
20 ‘Para mi ingresar a H.I.J.O.S. fue un éxtasis total, lo recuerdo como uno de los momentos
mas felices de mi vida. Era euforia. Una vez que estabas adentro tenias una sensaci6n de plenitud,
de haber encontrado tu lugar en el mundo. Antes me sentia culpable y nos sabia como lidiar con
esa necesidad de castigo. H.I.J.O.S. cambié todo’ (my translation). My interview with Paula
Maroni took place on 30 December 2009 in Buenos Aires. All the quotations included in this
chapter come from this interview.
21 ‘No se puede comparar estrictamente pero ser hijo de desaparecidos es un poco como el
caso de un alcoholico, hay algo de seso. Para alguien que tuvo que mentir y ocultar sus origenes,
llegar a HIJOS es-el comienzo de otra historia. Al principio te da verguenza decirle, pero hay otro
que dice lo mismo y sentis alivio. Después empezas a hacer publica esa historia, y al final podes
decir: jEy, soy hijo de desaparecidos!’ (my translation).

34 CECILIA SOSA
never a ‘transparent revelation’; even so, H.I.J.O.S. provided its members with
a ‘safe environment’ in which to revise their own biographies. In this transition,
the group ultimately emerged for each individual member as a new ‘addiction’.
The empowering of the figure of the descendant propagated inside the group
can be conceived of as a counter token of the collective ‘coming out’ that the
escraches signified to the wider society. Both processes nurtured and enhanced
each other. In parallel, the social recognition of H.I.J.O.S.’ activism worked
inside the organisation as a mechanism of self-assurance for each individual
member. However, despite these expansive energies, the group was also haunt-
ed by tensions and contradictions.
A question of ‘pedigree’
H.I.J.O.S. was always proud of defining itself as a horizontal group with no
leaders, in which all decisions were voted in assembly. Nonetheless, there
were many distinctions. Initially, members had to be either the son or daugh-
ter of a murdered, disappeared, formerly arrested or exiled person, as was
established during H.I.J.O.S.’ first national meeting, which took place in 1996
in Cordoba city. Thus, to be part of the group each candidate had to bear the
condition of the ‘victim’. As I noticed during my fieldwork, unspoken distinc-
tions and status cut across its members. These hierarchies were mostly re-
lated to the extent to which each member had been affected by state violence.
Disappeared or exiled parents did not qualify as the same. Similarly, a mem-
ber who had one parent disappeared had a differential status from another
who had lost both. The differential levels of infliction produced a regime of
ranks inside the group. Basically, the more one was affected by violence, the
more ‘privileged’ one’s status was within the organisation. Personal status
tended to increase in the case of well-known disappeared parents. In addition,
those who had lost many relatives were known as having sangre azul (‘blue
blood’). They were ‘VIP’ members of the group. These surreptitious hierar-
chies were acknowledged in the organisation’s slang: they called it ‘a question
of pedigree’.
The discovery of this internal slang acted as a revealing moment in my
fieldwork. It enabled me to grasp the ubiquitous code, which dramatically
defined blood in connection to loss. It also made me aware that the reverbera-
tions of the traumatic past had created a hierarchy of lineage inside the group,
which was mischievously presented as a version of royal ‘blue’ stock. This is
how Pisoni, who lost both parents during the dictatorship, addressed this issue
during our talk:
When we were kids we were ashamed to say that we were children of the
disappeared. Now we are not afraid any more. We are not ashamed to say
who we are. Among us, we call each other ‘orphans’. I like the idea of the

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED $i)
‘orphans’ but only if it is one of us speaking. I don’t like people who make
jokes who are not real descendants. There is the ‘pedigree’.
If there was a time when the descendants were afraid of confessing their condi-
tion, under the protection of the group the status of the victim qualified as a plus.
As Pisoni argued, the ‘pedigree’ primarily worked as a contestation of the ex-
perience ofi ndividual shame on the part oft he descendants. Thus, while assum-
ing their condition of ‘orphans’, the activists contested the repression and disa-
vowal that marked the first years of democracy when the entire society was
obliged to face the effects of violence. In reversing that experience of shame,
the organisation established an inverse hierarchy. This vernacular slang can be
formulated in a straightforward way: ‘the more tragic, the better’. Quite surpris-
ingly, the group’s reinvestment of the experience of loss was processed through
a dark sense of humour.
Still, the question of pedigree raised a controversial ambivalence in relation
to one of my main preoccupations, that of expanding the possibilities for shar-
ing trauma beyond bloodline configurations. If at a public level H.I.J.O.S.’
escraches contested the bloodline and self-contained forms of activism devel-
oped by other relatives, in a subtle manner the internal slang that circulated
inside the group tended to iterate the ‘monopoly of memory, loss and pain’ that,
as Jelin addressed, was constituted on the part of the victims.**? However, to
make things more intricate, these distinctions were also processed through an
ineffable sense of humour. In fact, Pisoni’s description of the ‘pedigree’ seems
to point towards an unwritten rule that says that it is neither sensitive nor desir-
able to make jokes in relation to delicate issues, unless one has experienced a
similar situation. If this tacit norm had the putative aim of preventing sensitive
issues from being trivialised, it also established limits and rights, which ulti-
mately defined those who were granted the privilege of laughter. In these terms,
those entitled to make jokes about the injured condition of the members were
those who had pedigree: the true ‘orphans’. As becomes clear from Pisoni’s
words, the descendants were somehow aware that a mischievous performance
of blood was taking place within the group. In fact, the pedigree also worked
as a self-reflective logic of auto-promotion, which reflected the feeling of su-
periority that circulated among those who shared the ‘blue’ blood. In fact, the
DJ Centrone, with no ‘disappeared’ in his family, perceived these tensions when
he first came to the group:
22 ‘Cuando éramos chicos nos daba verguenza decir que eramos hijos de desaparecidos.
Ahora no tenemos miedo de decirlo, no tenemos verguenza de quién somos. Entre nosotros nos
llamamos los ‘hdérfanos’. Me gusta la idea, pero solo si es alguno de nosotros. No me gustan lo
que hacen chistes y no son hijos. Ahi esta el pedigree ’(my translation).
23 Jelin, ‘The Politics of Memory’, p. 53.

36 CECILIA SOSA
The one who received me was Rodolfo Walsh’s grandson, one of my best
friends now.** He asked me who my parents were and when I told him he
started looking at me with distrust. ‘Which party are you from?’, he asked.
I said that I was an anarchist and they still laugh at me because of that. But
what was the point? Did you need to have your old man killed to have the
right to take part?”
For many years, Centrone was the exception, the only member of H.1.J.O.S.
who had no direct victims within his family. Nonetheless, from 1999 onwards,
H.I.J.O.S. acknowledged that wider forms of support were required in order to
legitimise their claims. After heated debates, the organisation decided to accept
the ‘open population’, as they called those who had not been directly affected
by violence. A new period began.
A non-normative family in mourning
The decision to welcome the ‘open population’ was crucial. For the first time,
an relatives’ organisation accepted the possibility of ‘sharing’ in the trauma. In
H.I.J.O.S.’ case, the construction of a new lineage of non-kin activists contrib-
uted to displacing the idea that the dictatorship only resulted in direct victims.
In welcoming the ‘open population’ into their ranks, H.I.J.O.S. contributed to
a conception of loss as collective. This form of non-kin activism brought a new
political impulse in the aftermath of violence. Nonetheless, the decision also
led to an internal split. While some members left the organisation to create a
new section, within the original group tensions became visible. A non-norma-
tive form of membership was born: there were members who had pedigree,
and those who lacked it.
This desire to belong contributes to make sense of the particular atmosphere
that circulated within the group. H.I.J.O.S. provided its members with an affec-
tive life-world that could be conceived in terms of a political family. In fact, the
group brought together spaces of friendship and kinship that are usually con-
sidered separately. Being a member not only implied entering into a new circuit
of political activities, including demonstrations and public assemblies, but also
parties, camping, journeys and a whole network in which friendship, sex and
political discussion were usually knotted. Ultimately, these affective ties drew
upon an alternative pattern of social belonging that may be read in tune with
24 Rodolfo Walsh was a renowned journalist killed by the military on 25 March 1977. He
was ambushed one day after denouncing the criminal acts of the military through his famous
Carta abierta a la Junta Militar (‘Open Letter to the Junta’).
25 ‘El que me recibid fue el nieto de Rodolfo Walsh, uno de mis mejores amigos ahora. Me
pregunt6 quienes eran mis viejos y cuando le dije me empez6 a mirar con sospecha. “De qué
partido venis”, me pregunt6. Le dije que era anarquista y todavia se rien por eso. Pero cual es el
punto? {Te tienen que haber matado a mi viejo para poder entrar?’ (my translation).

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 30
Eng’s expanded notion of kinship, which addresses not only ‘collective, com-
munal, and consensual affiliations’ but also ‘the physic, affective, and visceral
bonds’, which emerge within non-kin micro-communities.° In fact, those ‘vis-
ceral bonds’ depict well the tensions and also the strong sense of communality
involved in H.I.J.O.S.’ practices. This was especially clear during the com-
memoration of the 20th anniversary of the military siege in 1996 when the
descendants had the feeling that the whole society was looking at them. This is
how Maroni evoked that period:
We went to all the events together. We were 18 and the only priority was
being together. We could be all day at someone’s place, laughing, eating,
or sleeping. The idea was to have all the time for us. That was what it was
all about.?’ o
Those early times came to be known as H.I.J.0.S.’ ‘golden years’. Expelled
from conventional structures of kinship, the descendants managed to recreate
alternative ties of social belonging, which surpassed conventional nuclear ar-
rangements. ‘We became a sort of family,’ said Nina Ruiz, who had lost neither
parent and who is now in charge of group communications.”* The sense of inti-
macy was so intense that those who were not part of the organisation were called
chongo/chonga (‘outsiders’). Remarkably, the term chongo has traditionally
been used within the Argentine gay-lesbian-trans communities to refer to sex-
ual partners from outside who commonly do not identify themselves as gay. In
this way, the incorporation of queer sexual slang into H.I.J.O.S.’ jargon reveals
a productive wide-ranging imbrication of spheres in which the political rever-
berations of the term chongo, derived from its origins in sexual minorities’
struggles, is transferred to an up-coming form of memory activism.”
Inside H.I.J.O.S. a new form of support and care was constituted. To some
extent, kinship was remade into a ‘family of choice’, one that tended to be
perceived as progressively exclusive. In fact, within the group dating people
from the ‘outside’ was considered as a betrayal. ‘Mixed couples hardly survived’
was the prevailing rumour. ‘I was dating a guy who had no relatives disappeared
and eventually I left him. An outside boyfriend simply didn’t fit,’ confirmed
Maroni. This logic of inside/outside could be read alongside the particular sense
of humour that circulated within the group.
26 See Eng, The Feeling of Kinship, p. 2.
27 ‘Ibamos a todos lados juntos. Tenfamos 18 y la unica prioridad era estar juntos. Podiamos
estar todo el dia en la casa de alguien, riéndonos, comiendo 0 durmiendo. La idea era tener todo
el tiempo para nosostros. De eso se trataba’ (my translation).
28 ‘Empezamos a ser como una familia’ (my translation). My interview with Nina Ruiz took
place on 23 April 2009 at Familiares, the office that H.IJ.O.S. shared by that time with the as-
sociation of the relatives, in Buenos Aires.
29 | am grateful to Jens Andermann for pointing out this productive connection.

38 CECILIA SOSA
A dark sense of the comical
While interviewing H.I.J.O.S.’ members, I was surprised by the use of humour,
especially a dark sense of humour that the descendants applied to painful expe-
riences. It was not regular humour but a particular spirit of the comical, a mix
of affects always flirting with death. Eventually, I discovered that this appeal to
the tragic worked as a sort of viscous substance through which non-normative
feelings of kinship resonated among the members. Something that could not be
perceived as comical taken in isolation functioned within the group as a form
of recovering memory and also as the reversal of previous experiences of in-
jury. This is how Maroni justified the mood of the group:
I cannot laugh about the same things with friends who did not experience the
same. They could not understand it. Even for myself it was uncomfortable,
a sort of internal doubt ‘you cannot be laughing at this’. But with someone
who suffered the same, there is a feeling of complicity that you cannot have
with anyone else.*°
As becomes clear, this non-normative sense of humour was animated by a re-
strictive idea of ‘us’, which encouraged certain feelings of possession and ex-
clusivity among those who shared in trauma. In this sense, humour created an
internal logic of privilege. This profound sense of complicity was nevertheless
accompanied by guilt. ‘We shouldn’t be laughing at this’ was the remorseful
thought. To some extent, humour emerged as a confirmation of the pedigree.
Once again, the comical spirit addressed a key tension within the descendants
concerning the feelings of ownership in relation to loss. While activism ex-
panded the experience of trauma throughout wider sectors of civic society, at a
more intimate level the ‘injury card’ was reinforced. ‘Because we suffered, we
are entitled to laugh’ was the unspoken code that circulated within the group.
Long ago, Sigmund Freud noticed that there is a sense of superiority and invin-
cibility attached to humour: ‘If it is really the super-ego which, in humour, speaks
such kindly words of comfort to the intimidated ego, this will teach us that we
have still a great deal to learn about the nature of the super-ego.’*! Drawing on
this, I suggest that humour provided the descendants’ ‘intimidated egos’ with a
form of comfort. Humour enabled them to mourn their missing parents through
30 ‘No me puedo reir de las mismas cosas con amigos que no pasaron por lo mismo. No lo
entenderian. Incluso para mi es un poco incomodo, como una duda interna ‘no podés estar riendote
de esto’. Pero con alguien que sufrié lo mismo que vos, hay una complicidad que no tenés con
nadie mas’ (my translation).
31 Sigmund Freud, ‘Humour’, in The Standard Edition of the Complete Psychological Works
of Sigmund Freud, Vol. XX\: The Future of an Illusion, Civilization and its Discontents, and
Other Works (1927-1931), trans. James Strachey, in collaboration with Anna Freud, assisted by
Alix Strachey and Alan Tyson (London: Hogarth Press, 1974), pp. 159-66 (p. 166).

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 3
the reverberating properties of joy, which they associated with activist prac-
tices. It is worth exploring this argument further.
At some point in my fieldwork, I became especially curious about the par-
ticular kind of jokes that made the descendants laugh. Yet the resistance of
my interviewees to going into detail was palpable. ‘It is difficult to remember
those jokes now. During these first years this humour was there all the time.
It was a mood ofb eing in the group,’ argued Lucila Quieto, a former activist.
Nonetheless, I noticed that most of the jokes that circulated within the group
drew from the same kind of impulse. A member oft he group gave me a price-
less clue to explore this: ‘We always joke about death. Our jokes are always
related to the experience of not having your loved ones next to us, and we
play with this all the time,’ Pisoni told me.** Simply and powerfully, humour
coped with death. ~
It was revealing to find out that jokes were especially popular during birthdays
or family celebrations at which the parental absence became unavoidable. For
instance, it was an internal agreement that the best place to celebrate Fathers’
or Mothers’ days was next to the rio de la Plata, into which most of the disap-
peared were thrown alive. Moreover, the activists used to play with the idea that
a green Falcon, the emblematic car that was used to conduct the kidnappings
during the dictatorship, might kidnap them. More than just flirting with death,
what was finally at stake in such dark jokes was the fantasy of repeating their
parents’ stories. Indeed, both Jelin and Hirsch refer to this as a problem involved
in the transmission of traumatic memory, the fear that the descendants may have
their ‘own stories and experiences displaced and even evacuated by those of a
previous generation’.** As I will document later, among some members this
fantasy reached difficult extremes. Nonetheless, in most cases, the descendants
appeared to reverse these fears through laughter.
In his important work on laughter, Henri Bergson reminds us that we should
not imprison the comic spirit with a definition but rather try to understand it in
its natural environment, the specific community in which it arises.*> Humour
worked in H.I.J.O.S. as a way of coping with the everyday effects of trauma,
enabling the descendants to make sense of the difficulties involved in the re-
construction of parental figures. During our interview, Maroni offered a gripping
example to illustrate this. She told me the story of an activist whose entire fam-
ily, including his father, mother and siblings, had been kidnapped. “He used to
32 ‘Bs dificil de acordarse de esos chistes ahora. Durante los primeros afios ese humour es-
taba presente todo el tiempo. Era el modo de estar en el grupo’ (my translation).
33 “Siempre hacemos chistes en relaci6n a la muerte. Los chistes estan siempre relacionados
ano tener a nuestros seres queridos con nosotros, jugamos con eso todo el tiempo’ (my translation).
34 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 107; Jelin, “The Politics of Memory’, p. 52.
35 Henri Bergson, Laughter: An Essay on the Meaning of the Comic (London: Manor,
2008), p. 10.

40 CECILIA SOSA
say that even his dog was made to disappear. We could not stop laughing because
of the pathetic tone that he used in that phrase. This guy was a VIP member of
the group.’ This impish idea of ‘VIP member’ powerfully captures the appeal
to the tragic that permeated the organisation. The self-deprecating gesture of
the activist can also be read alongside Freud’s valuable insight. The psycho-
analyst observes that one of the most common forms of humour takes place
when an individual adopts a humorous attitude to make himself the object of
amusement for others. In those cases, the non-participant or listener is also af-
fected.’ As this episode illustrates, this form of self-reflective humour played
a fundamental role in H.I.J.O.S. It produced an atmosphere in which the yield
of pleasure fell back on the entire group. The humorous attitude did not belong
to a single person but to the group itself. Bearing this in mind, | would like to
consider further the sense of commonality that Bergson relates to humour: ‘Our
laughter is always the laughter of ag roup,’ he writes.** Indeed, H.1.J.0.S. offers
a particularly eloquent case for exploring this. Since most of the members of
the group had gone though similar experiences, to some extent their experi-
ences became interchangeable. On these grounds, pleasure became expansive
and expandable. The effects were intensified. This closed and enclosing mech-
anism not only reinforced the high levels of self-reference in the group but also
infused its feelings of superiority.
Drawing upon this, I would like to go back to the case of the member of the
group who used to joke about his dismembered genealogical tree. The young
activist performed a self-deprecating act of victimisation through which he
deliberately assumed the injury card to amuse the others. He showed off his
condition as victim. This liturgy was already known among other associations
of relatives; nonetheless, in H.I.J.O.S. this script was displaced and re-empow-
ered by laughter. To some extent, it could be argued that the descendants revis-
ited the figure of the ‘wretch’, as Sara Ahmed describes those miserable or
unfortunate individuals who are perceived ‘as lacking what causes happiness’.*?
Nonetheless, H.I.J.O.S. drew from the rhetoric of this unfortunate lineage to
transform it into a source of collective amusement. In so doing, the group pro-
posed a subversive parody of traditional forms of unhappiness. The descendants
managed to convert those who seem to lack everything to become the ‘kings’
of the victims, and thereby VIP members of the group. As Bergson reminds us,
humour always requires a collective environment capable of reading a particu-
lar tone as a joke. In H.1.J.O.S. this collective environment drew upon a common
36 ‘Solia decir que se le habian Ilevado hasta el perro. No podiamos parar de reirnos del pa-
tetismo de esa frase. El pibe era un activista VIP del grupo’ (my translation).
37 Freud, ‘Humour’, p. 161.
38 Bergson, Laughter, p. 11.
39 Sara Ahmed, ‘Happiness and Queer Politics’, World Picture, 3 (2009), p. 12, http://www.
worldpicturejournal.com/ [accessed 1 December 2011].

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 4]
loss. It provided the common atmosphere capable of identifying the depreca-
tory style as amusing, and even transformative. In some oblique way, humour
also worked as a peculiar form of community elitism among the descendants.
It pivoted on loss and blood. Only in this manner could the deprived condition
of the ‘wretch’ be promoted as a plus. Ultimately, this fooling, bitter style became
the frame through which the group perceived the world and developed their
ability to laugh about it.
The long laughter of the wretch
During H.I.J.O.S.’ fifteen-year trajectory, humour worked as a collective tool
to deal with various political contexts. From 1994 the democratic governments
began offering ‘financial compensation’ to the relatives of the missing. In this
context, the relatives’ associations responded differently. While one faction of
the Madres rejected the official funds, most of the other groups accepted them,
including H.I.J.O.S.*° Pisoni explained:
Even in the cases in which this money went to support an anarchist fanzine,
we thought that it was better to take it. It was a sign of responsibility from the
state. It wouldn’t change our fight.*!
Even so, the situation of being the recipients of these ‘reparative funds’ engen-
dered certain feelings of embarrassment and discomfort among the descendants,
which eventually became the source of new jokes.” For instance, while making
a flag for a demonstration, an activist could say: ‘Dad, please don’t appear now
because | will not get the reimbursement.’** Or in the case of members whose
parents had both disappeared, it was possible to hear: ‘Oh dude, you are so lucky,
you are going to get double.’ Other jokes also addressed the uses of the official
money. If someone turned up with a new car, aspersions would be cast: ‘Look!
He is driving his dad’s arm.’* Conversely, if a member started dressing more
expensively, he or she would be intimidated without compassion: “There you

between US$90 and US$250,000 per disappeared and complained against setting prices on their
children’s lives. See http://www.youtube.com/watch?v=38fXK Y QXB9I&feature=share [accessed
5 June 2010]. See Chapter 4 for further analysis.
41 ‘Incluso si la plata se usaba para pagar un fanzine anarquista nos parecié mejor aceptarla.
Era una sefial de responsabilidad de parte del estado. No cambiaba nuestra lucha’ (my translation).
42 Ag I will explore in Chapter 6, the uneasy feeling in relation to reparative funds is also
addressed by the wild humour that informs Félix Bruzzone’s novella, Los topos.
43 ‘Viejo, nd aparezcas ahora qué no cobro la indemnizacién’ (my translation).
44 ‘Chabon, qué culo, vos cobras doble’ (my translation).
45 ‘Miren, esta manejando el brazo del viejo’ (my translation).

42 CECILIA SOSA
have your mum’s nail.’*° Although this dark-humorous attitude became part of
the logic of the group, some activists felt uncomfortable talking about it a dec-
ade later. In some cases, I had the feeling that they were protecting something
private, only verbalised within the slang of the group; a guilty pleasure that only
those with pedigree could share. Even so, other activists eventually dared to
explicitly advocate this kind of humour. Pisoni argued:
I know that many people didn’t like this kind of joke. But for me it was clear:
one can cry for the dead or remember them in a playful way. We couldn’t
spend our whole life crying.*’
One can either cry or laugh: the activist’s response was certainly provocative.
It reminded me of the kind of argument that Jacques Derrida raises in The Work
of Mourning, in which he writes: ‘the affirmation of life is nothing other than a
certain thought of death; it is neither opposition nor indifference to death’.** For
Derrida, the moment of laughter often occurs in the most difficult moments of
mourning; hilarity comes as an affirmation, a protestation in the name of life,
almost as a triumph over death.” I suggest that similarly contested affects cir-
culated inside H.I.J.O.S. A further example can give a better sense oft his.
Daniel Shapira was born at ESMA. After his parents’ kidnapping, he was
returned to his grandmother, who raised him. During the Kirchnerist period,
a memorial dedicated to his father was placed near Daniel’s workplace. Since
he had almost no pictures of his father, he likes to joke that at least he now
has a memorial to pose with. When it rains, he teases: ‘I am going to hang out
a little bit with my dad. The poor guy is getting wet outside.’*° The case is
illustrative of a classic operation that Freud identifies in humour. When the
activist assumes this humorous attitude in relation to his father’s death, he
refuses ‘to be distressed by the provocation of reality, and presents himself
beyond vulnerability’.*' In so doing, he not only denies being affected by the
external world, but he also pretends that such trauma can be a source of pleas-
ure. For Freud, this displacement is an essential element of humour, and a
46 ‘Bsa es la ufia de tu mama’ (my translation).
47 *A mucha gente no le gustaba este tipo de chistes. Para mi era claro: o lloras por los muer-
tos 0 los recordas con alegria. No podiamos pasarnos toda la vida Ilorando’ (my translation).
48 Jacques Derrida, The Work of Mourning (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2001), p. 175.
49 Derrida, The Work of Mourning, p. 183.
50 ‘Me voy a ver un rato a mi viejo. El pobre tipo se esta empapando afuera’ (my translation).
The case was addressed in an article entitled ‘La risa y la libertad’ (‘Laughter and Freedom’)
published in the youth supplement Ni a palos, 2 August 2009, http://www.niapalos.
org/?s=Daniel+Shapira&submit.x=0&submit.y=0 [accessed 10 December 2011]. As I came to
know later, some of my interviewees mentioned the main arguments of this chapter to the editors
of the magazine, inspiring the above-mentioned production.
51 Freud, ‘Humour’, p. 162.

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 43
subtle form of ‘dignity’.** Moreover, he argues that ‘humour is not resigned;
it is rebellious. It signifies not only the triumph of the ego but also of the
pleasure principle.’** At the same time, Freud suggests that the unstable borders
of humour may also address the loss of a certain sense of reality, something
that in his reading emphasises the invincibility of the ego over the real world.*!
It seems to me that H.I.J.O.S. introduced an enhancement within this mecha-
nism. It was not an individual sense ofr eality that was at stake but a collective
spirit from which the source of pleasure eventually emerged. Precisely this
amalgam ofv ibrations explains how the descendants managed to work through
the process of grief as a group.
Thinking this process from Derrida’s perspective, humour appeared as an
operation of ‘countersignature’, a way of reversing the parental absence while
adding something new; in otter words, making room for a new space in the
present. Thus, humour did not work for H.I.J.O.S. as a simple distraction, but
rather as ‘a movement to survive, to live on’, to borrow Derrida’s words again.*
This appeal to laughter enabled the descendants to move into an experience of
survival in which the very idea of vulnerability and loss turned out to be em-
powered. H.I.J.O.S.’ flirtation with death also inaugurated another stage in the
process of mourning. This differential capacity to produce joy from loss counts
as H.I.J.O.S.’ main triumph. Dark humour was the gift, the rebellious attitude
that finally ‘saved’ its members from casting as merely vistims.
The dark side of laughter
This collective sense of humour, however, also compromised a certain sense
of reality. This process was mostly related to the over-identification with the
parental figures. To address this, I need to go back to the atmosphere that cir-
culated within the group in those early years. As already suggested, H.I.J.O.S.
marked a turning point regarding how to recall the missing parents. While the
relatives’ associations had mostly ignored or denied the political activism of
the ‘disappeared’, the descendants vindicated the parental militancy and at
some point they even tried to re-enact it. These ritualistic practices reached
such a passionate extreme that they brought about new anxieties and contradic-
tions in the group.
Given that many youngsters arrived at H.I.J.O.S. without knowing about
their parents’ militancy, the group became a space in which to lose inhibitions.
In the weekly assemblies, the candidates learned to link their names to those
of their parents, identifying the places and circumstances of their kidnapping.
52 Freud, ‘Humour’, p. 162.
53. Freud, ‘Humour’, p. 163.
54 Freud, ‘Humour’, p. 162.
55 Derrida, The Work of Mourning, p. 176.

44 CECILIA SOSA
The organisation sponsored what was acknowledged as a ‘critical revision’ of
the parental activism. Ironically, the process led most of the descendants to
adopt their parents’ affiliations. In fact, by the end oft he 1990s, two main fac-
tions could be recognised within the group: the Montoneros, who identified
with Peronism, and the left-wing Ejército Revolucionario del Pueblo (ERP),
who identified with a communist background. In some extemporaneous way,
H.1.J.0.S. tended to reproduce the main divisions that informed the guerrilla
movement during the 1970s.
In fact, during the national meeting in 1996, it was possible to witness two
bands of young activists engaging for hours in a ‘war of songs’ against each
other. Ostensibly, the songs were the same as those chanted by their parents
during the clandestine period. To some extent, this over-identification with the
parental stories could be read in tune with Taylor’s argument that H.I.J.O.S.
was irremediably captured by the past. Nonetheless, the scene was embedded
in a strange temporality that exceeded the mere logic of the ‘quote’. Although
there was something anachronistic, and maybe also childish, in the portrait of
the descendants fiercely chanting those militant songs, the old lyrics also
emerged as the affective surface through which the new generation made con-
tact with the missing. The rehearsal of the parental ‘script’ embodied by those
songs also worked as a form of affective reparation in the present. Patrick
Duggan’s and Mick Wallis’s analysis of trauma and performance offers a pro-
ductive framework to consider how the rehearsal of traumatic events may also
have restorative efficacy. Thus, the repetition of those old lyrics enabled the
descendants to connect with the intensities that were part of their parents’ lives.
In so doing, they found a way to mourn their absence through a common in-
vestment in activism. For the descendants, grief became possible through the
sense of friendship and complicity gained through activism. Despite internal
differences, the feeling of being part of a collective also had the capacity to
liberate a surplus of pleasure, mostly associated with certain practices in which
the descendants felt closely identified with their parents’ lives. The excess
involved in those songs was less related to their revolutionary slogans than to
the possibility of making them work as a surface of enjoyment. This process
also reminds me of the importance of pleasure as a political factor as addressed
by Carrie Hamilton, and also how non-normative forms of grieving can draw
upon the memories of happiness associated with activism.*’ While reworking
a common loss, the group created a new space of enchantment. During the
Menemist period in which oblivion or denial were the ordinary, the ‘war of
songs’ staged a renewed possibility of communality. It finally built a sense of
being together across times.
56 Patrick Duggan and Mick Wallis, ‘Trauma and Performance: Maps, Narratives and Folds’,
Performance Research, 16.1 (2011), 4-16 (p. 6).
57 Hamilton, ‘Activist Memories’, p. 271.

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 45
At the same time, the vindication of parental activism was also a source of
new anxieties. By the late 1990s the descendants were convinced that they had
achieved a successful model of activism. For this reason, they feared that tra-
ditional left-wing parties might try to ‘infiltrate’ the organisation. The perception
of this ‘external threat’ influenced discussions around the opening of the group
to the ‘open population’. It also mobilised distinctive modes of security, which
to some extent tended to mimic the strategies that were undertaken by the guer-
rilla groups during the 1970s. For instance, some ‘hard-core’ members started
using ‘war names’ and keeping diaries with fake contacts to protect the secu-
rity of the group. H.I.J.0.S.’ assemblies, which were considered as the supreme
stage of decision-making of the group, began to have limited access. As many
of my interviewees commented, all candidates who wanted to be part of these
meetings (even those who bore the ‘pedigree’) had to go through a series of
mandatory ‘reception dinners’, which included food and wine, plus specific
‘training’ and formal declarations about their reasons for joining the group. As
most of the activists now agree, those security measures were probably exces-
sive. The displacement between the younger generation’s activism and previous
clandestine forms of militancy also contributed to set an outdated agenda
within H.I.J.O.S.’ political horizons. Eventually, the over-identification with the
parental figures also brought desertions. Ricardo Dios, who spent a short period
with the group in 1996, referred to this:
By that time, there was an idealisation of the guerrilla groups and their
commitments, loves and passions. There was a lot of confusion about what it
meant to be an activist during the 1970s and what we were doing during the
1990s. The fantasy of becoming our parents pushed me away.”
In a minority of cases, the ubiquitous sense of identification and transference
that circulated in the group achieved the extreme fantasy of descendants taking
the place of the missing, and eventually becoming those vanished figures. This
is how one of my interviewees described these tortuous feelings:
I couldn’t sleep at night. Each time I went to bed, I thought that they would
come to take me. I was terrorised. I started acting a story that was not mine.”
In the literature that addresses the emotional intensities associated with street
activism and direct action, obscure preoccupations such as the fantasies of
58 ‘Por esa época habia una idealizacién de los grupos armados, de los compromisos que
tenian, sus amores y pasiones. Habia mucha confusion sobre lo que significaba ser activista en los
1970s y lo que nosotros estabamos haciendo. La fantasia de pensar que ramos nuestros viejos me
termin6 alejando” (my translation).
59 ‘No podia dormir de noche. Cada vez que me iba a la cama, sentia que me iban a venir a
buscar. Estaba aterrada. Empecé a actuar una historia que no era mia’ (my translation).

46 CECILIA SOSA
persecution described by this activist have not been sufficiently explored.” My
analysis is far from being exhaustive; however, the woman’s over-identification
with her mother’s story appeared as the dark’side of the vindication of parental
activism, and also as a form of ‘evacuation’ of the younger generation’s own
experiences. As the testimony oft he activist reveals, these disturbing fantasies
risked a faulty appraisal of reality. Her case also sheds light on disturbing con-
tradictions inscribed in H.I.J.O.S.’ activism, which remained mostly silenced
even within the members oft he group. These conflicts have not been exhausted.
They could be grasped as the counter-effect of the humorous mood developed
in the group during those mythical early years.
Communities across time
Even in its darkest moods, the strong ‘coalition’ constituted by the descendants
with their missing parents seemed to suggest a strange form of conviviality across
time. This conviviality was made palpable by an artistic initiative that was hosted
by H.I.J.O.S. in the late 1990s. Between 1998 and 2000 the photographer and
group member Lucila Quieto developed a photographic project in the organisation.
She invited the activists to pose next to their missing parents. “You can have the
picture you always wanted’ was the pitch posted on the walls of the office that the
group had in the area of Constitucion in Buenos Aires. Responding to Quieto’s
instruction, the volunteers brought their favourite picture of their parents to the
shooting session. There, the mostly old black-and-white images were amplified
and transformed into gigantic slides, which were projected on to a big screen.
Using these slides as tantalising curtains, Quieto portrayed both generations to-
gether. The effect is as spectral as it is uncanny: they are scenes of impossible
reunion in which parents and offspring inhabit the edgy temporality of the would-
have-been. Sometimes the youngsters are the ones who hug, toast or dance with
their progenitors (Figure 1, p. 75). Sometimes it feels as if it were the other way
around: it is the missing who come back to comfort their already grown-up off-
spring, to hold them or even to propose a toast across time. In some poignant
cases, the youngsters look older than their parents. They even glance suspicious-
ly at each other, as if they cannot finally arrive at an agreement.
Quieto’s photographic series stands as the testimony of a struggle, another
ghostly war in which the young generations tried to find their own space in the
present. Still, the playful mood that permeated that fight was difficult to grasp
for those who were not familiar with the spirit of the group. In this respect,
Quieto argued during our interview:
60 Ann Cvetkovich’s analysis of lesbian activism and Carrie Hamilton’s reflections on po-
litical excitement and merriment as a hidden form of mourning open productive grounds to explore
this question further. See Cvetkovich, An Archive of Feelings, p. 187; Hamilton, ‘Activist Memo-
HeSia paaluilp

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 47
Many people felt sorry for me and usually said, ‘I can imagine how painful
and terrible this process must have been for you’. But for me it was exactly
the opposite; I had a lot of fun!*!
During the early period of the group, that those kinds of phantasmagorical scenes
were part of the descendants’ everyday lives suggests a strange sense of con-
viviality between the dead and the living. This sense of temporality can be ad-
dressed further through the work of the medieval feminist scholar Carolyn
Dinshaw. In her research on pre- and postmodern archives and sexualities,
Dinshaw noticed that ‘a concept of queer relations across time recognizes the
past, including the distant past, as a vibrant and heterogeneous resource for
self-fashioning as well as community building’.® In a parallel way, for the de-
scendants of Argentina’s military violence, the recent past also emerged as a
vibrant source of encounter. In this expanded sense of time, as Dinshaw well
puts it, ‘the dead may indeed come alive — some part, some aspect of them — as
we touch and animate them in our own lives’.® The impossible scenes of reun-
ion portrayed by Quieto speak about this possibility: they manage to give ac-
tual shape to and materialise a self-created community across time. More than
this, in Quieto’s photographic essay the shared feelings in relation to the miss-
ing parents generate a common sense of belonging in the here and now.
In 2001 Quieto’s images were part of an art exhibition in Italy and a cata-
logue.“ More recently, they have been published as a book, Arqueologia de la
ausencia (‘Archaeology of Absence’).® More than a decade later, Quieto’s series
of impossible encounters haunts the present with a pervasive demand for justice,
which is not satisfied by the ongoing trials. To some extent, those unattainable
ceremonies of reunion show how the missing figures reject being archived. They
come back time and again, inviting us to explore ways in which communities
are constituted through affective touches with the past.

place on 23 April 2009 at the former ESMA.

22 December 2011].

48 CECILIA SOSA
The legacies of humour
In this chapter I have attempted to explore a key issue: whether the humorous
mood that accompanied H.1.J.0.S. worked as an effective tool to move on from
feelings of victimisation. The answer is not simple. Without doubt, H.1.J.O.S.’
flirtation with death inaugurated a new phase in the national scene of mourning.
The group worked as a laboratory for the emergence of slang and rituals that
challenged the codes of other relatives’ associations. The participatory and
expansive mood inaugurated by the escraches helped to establish a public cul-
ture of mourning in which the whole society was invited to take part. At an
internal level, H.I.J.O.S. worked as a space of encounter for injured peers, which
eventually became a ‘family of choice’. The descendants found in the group a
common ground of complicity to share in pain but also in fun and love. This
singular mix of death and laughter delineated a new experience of survival in
which vulnerability and loss turned out to be empowering. To some extent,
humour was H.1.J.O.S.’ biggest rebellion. It built a non-normative position to
resist violence while reinventing a politics of grieving for a whole generation.
Indeed, this was a collective triumph, which secured the dignity of the members.
While making fun of their wretched condition, H.I.J.O.S. fought for a new space
in the present. Humour worked as a tool for social transformation, providing its
members with ‘a site of both individual and collective repair’, to use Eng’s
words.*’ Perhaps the main achievement was to adopt this attitude as a collective,
rendering the yield of pleasure an object of exchange for all the members. More
broadly, humour also provided H.1.J.0.S. with a form of collective consolation
to deal with parental absences. It empowered a generation of victims to defy the
legacy and reinterpret ‘the transmitted experiences in terms of their own his-
torical circumstances’.** Humour worked as a mode of survival, a way of life.
Nonetheless, as has also been addressed here, paradoxes and tensions among
the group remained. To some extent, humour contributed to cause stress. The
fooling dark style also brought a sense of superiority among the members of the
group, who mostly refused to be defeated by their traumas. Oblique forms of
arrogance also addressed the aspiration of mastering the ownership of trauma.
Expressions such as ‘pedigree’ and ‘blue blood’, despite being embedded in a
playful mood, also maintained the victim status. If this humorous attitude suc-
ceeded in introducing a novel shift in the spectrum of affects that governed the
relatives’ discourse, the style also showed its limits. In fact, H.I.J.O.S. never
stopped oscillating from one pole to another. The generational complicity also
contributed to give the group the character of a sect, which might not have helped
its members to move forward. After all, the very category of H.I.J.O.S. tended
67 Eng, The Feeling of Kinship, p. 186.
68 Jelin, ‘The Politics of Memory’, p. 52.

BLACK HUMOUR AND THE CHILDREN OF THE DISAPPEARED 49
to position the group in an endless childhood. Not only does it point towards a
kinship tie and a highlighted performance ofb lood, but also towards an impos-
sible emotional state, which stages the fantasy of being forever young. While
in 1995 the group’s average age was between the late teens and early twenties,
by 2011 most of the members were in their mid-thirties. If one of the main issues
for the organisation was how to deal with the question of legacy, the problem
(and the privilege) of staying alive, to some extent the group failed to work
against the condition of not being children, but adults. In some respects, the
group became a cocoon from which to remember their glorious adolescent days
during the 1990s.
The period initiated in 2003 brought new pressures for the organisation. For
almost ten years, the demands for justice performed by the group stuck to a
well-known tagline: Otro gobierno, misma impunidad (‘Another government,
the same impunity’). When Néstor Kirchner took office in 2003 a different
period began. Seeking a heroic narrative, the current administration adopted the
position of the victims as their main banner. This shift also created an awkward
situation among the relatives’ associations. ‘When a government transforms
your principles into public policies, two different things might occur: you can
get totally dislocated, as happened to a lot of us, or you can give your support.
This is what I did,’ said Pisoni, who in 2013 was appointed as the government’s
sub-secretary of Human Rights Promotion.” It is important to bear in mind that
2003 was also foundational year of La Campora, the youngest activist branch
of the official party, which was created by Maximo Kirchner, son of the presi-
dential couple. Some of its main leaders began their activism in H.I.J.O.S. As I
will address later, especially after Kirchner’s death, La Campora gained new
political relevance.”
The end of the ‘culture of impunity’ and the effective reopening of the trials
in 2006 imposed new challenges and dilemmas on the association of the de-
scendants. If for almost fifteen years the claim had been ‘If there is no justice,
moral condemnation will come’, from then on a major adjustment was required.”
Also, the legendary slogan Juicio y Castigo (‘Justice and Punishment’) had to
be revised. Eventually, a new pitch emerged, maybe not as powerful as the
original one: ‘The genocides are judged by a trial but condemned by everybody.’
The new position also involved a change of outfit; the group invited actors and

50 CECILIA SOSA
media people to pose with their brand new T-shirt. ‘I put on the T-shirt of the
trials and punishment.’ Truthful to its activist style, H.I.J.O.S. made the courts
the occasion for participatory art: ‘Come to draw in the trials’ is one of their
ongoing campaigns.” On 27 June 2011 H.I.J.O.S. inaugurated a new office at
ESMA former detention camp, La casa de la militancia (‘The House of Activ-
ism’). The headquarters are located in a vast space that used to be the military
gym during the dictatorship.
Nowadays, H.I.J.O.S.’ constitution has also changed. Not only has the num-
ber of activists dropped, but most of them are no longer children oft he disap-
peared: they do not have pedigree. The transformation of the group’s background
also speaks about a thriving experience oft ransmission. It shows how loss does
not belong entirely to a ‘wounded family’ but was, at least partially, transferred
to the wider society. While tempting new generations into activism, H.I.J.O.S.
also contributed to expand the ‘vicinity’ of trauma. When the discourses of
memory have been rendered official, new voices promote alternative combina-
tions of loss and laughter. These new experiments will be the focus of the fol-
lowing chapters. They offer fresh landscapes and textures to rework the ongoing
tensions explored in H.I.J.O.S. They bring on stage non-normative perfor-
mances of blood and alternative narratives of grief. They are, in many ways,
H.1.J.0.S.’ heirs. The legacy is strong. Despite all the contradictions, the de-
scendants indicate how a loud laugh can always emerge from loss.

Undoing the Cult of the Victim: Los Rubios,
Mand La mujer sin cabeza
In the context of a restored democracy, a substantial number of film directors
began to respond to Argentina’s violent past.! This new cinematic repertoire
corresponded to an incipient democratic culture in need of forging a new
pedagogical conscience. In this context, these typically testimonial pieces
have mostly monumentalised the past while adhering to the cult of the victim.
However, a new genre emerged in the 1990s when the descendants of those
who went missing introduced their own narratives to re-engage with the past.”
Still, the unwritten rule of the early post-dictatorship period stipulated that
relatives and survivors must honour the name of the vanished through force-
ful comments on truth and justice. Although the descendants’ testimonial ef-
fervescence remained mostly attached to the legitimacy of the ‘wounded
family’, there are two films that have contested the dominant tradition in re-
markable ways: Los Rubios (‘The Blondes’, 2003), the provocative film di-
rected by Albertina Carri, and M (2007), an extended documentary by the
debut filmmaker Nicolas Prividera.* As Jens Andermann suggests, both films
could be thought of as remarkable examples of a series of ‘post-memory
documentaries’, in which the descendants stage their own family albums,
‘autobiographical or “autofictional” docu-essays about orphaned selves inquir-
| La historia oficial (‘The Official Story’, Luis Puenzo, 1985) was precisely the ‘official’
and earnest point of departure for this series of films, and also the first Latin American film to
receive the Academy Award for Best Foreign Language Feature in 1986. E/ exilio de Gardel:
Tangos (‘Tangos, The Exile of Gardel’, Pino Solanas, 1985), Muro de Silencio (‘A Wall of Silence’,
Lila Stantic, 1993), Buenos Aires ViceVersa (Alejandro Agresti, 2004) and Kamchatka (Marcelo
Pifieyro, 2002) are only a small number of the films that have informed this series.
2 This series includes Papd Ivan (Maria Inés Roqué, 2004), (h) Historias cotidianas (Andrés
