the Madres could do there.'°
From the start, I was interested in Bonafini’s relating the former centre to a form
of doing; it already suggested the possibility of reversing ESMA’s charged at-
mosphere through a form of practice. This perspective contrasted with that of
former political prisoners and their relatives who resisted any kind of institu-
tional activity on the premises and wanted to preserve the place as the ‘mate-
rial testimony of genocide’.'' Bonafini went further:
When the organisations,started arguing about transforming ESMA into a
museum, | was against it. I have been to Auschwitz and all I saw was a
huge horror: it is a place where you can go once but you will never return. I
didn’t want to turn ESMA into a place where people just wanted to go once.
I thought that we had to make a place where people could stay, a place where
life wins over death. We had to transform ESMA into a space for life.'”
I was surprised. In opposition to the reification of horror that she claimed to
have witnessed in Auschwitz, Bonafini argued that ESMA should not be reduced
to the mere landscape of horror. While prestigious scholars spoke of the ultimate
‘irrecoverability’ of the former centre, the 81-year-old mother proposed to
transform those ‘bad feelings’ into a source of affirmative power, something
that in her words appeared articulated as ‘life’.'* I suggest that Bonafini’s dis-
course introduced an alternative path for conceiving mourning, one that can be
understood as a ‘politics of conversion’.
Some contemporary theorists have resisted the idea of conversion to define
the task of politics. For instance, while discussing the feelings of melan-
cholic immigrants, Sara Ahmed argues against those who think that painful
10 ‘No sé si a mi me parecié, 0 realmente era un lugar helado. Era verano pero te corre el
hielo y el frio por el cuerpo. Se habian Ilevado todo, de basuras que son para robar. Después yo
ya me imagine todas las cosas que podiamos hacer las Madres ahi adentro’ (my translation). My
interview with Hebe de Bonafini took place on 19 April 2009 at the Madres’ house in Buenos
Aires. All the quotations included in this chapter come from this interview.
11 ‘testimonio material del genocidio’ (my translation). I am referring again to the project of
the Asociacién de Ex-Detenidos Desaparecidos.
12 ‘Cuando las organizaciones empezaron a discutir que querian hacer un museo, yo estuve
en contra. Estuve en Auschwitz y lo que vi fue el horror mas terrible. No quiero hacer un lugar
donde la gente quiera ir una sola vez. Tenemos que hacer un lugar donde la vida le gane a la
muerte. Tenemos que transformar ESMA en un espacio para la vida’ (my translation).
13 This is, for instance, the position of the Argentine philosopher Alejandro Kaufman, which
Andermann defines as a ‘radical testimonialism’ and finally accounts for the ‘irrecoverability’ of
ESMA. See Andermann, ‘Returning to the Site of Horror’, p. 87.

86 CECILIA SOSA
experiences can be simply overcome. She contends that this position envisions
the bodies of these individuals who cannot forget as a ‘conversion point’."’
Politics, for her, should not be conceived of as a conversion. Although Ahmed’s
perspective builds a fascinating critique of neoliberal discourses of happiness,
a politics of conversion does not necessarily imply reconciliation or leaving
the past behind. Rather, it can be conceived as a form of rehearsing trauma in
the present. In fact, Mick Duggan and Patrick Wallis address this possibility
of considering performance as a way to ‘work through’ painful resonances of
the past.'* Drawing on a productive encounter between trauma and performance
studies, I shall consider Bonafini’s proposal for converting ESMA alongside
her Catholic background.
Faithful to her defiant style, at the height of the disputes with other relatives’
associations and survivors Bonafini eventually stopped attending the official
meetings hosted by the national government:
I told President Kirchner, ‘Look, I don’t want to come to these meetings any
more. Everybody says what they want to do, which are the places they want
to have, and how big their offices should be, but I am not interested in that.
I want the Liceo Naval Militar: this was the place where the military were
taught how to kill, rape and torture. We have to show that in this place we
can do something different. We have to show that we can transform ESMA
into a centre ofl ife.’!°
With the official blessing of the president, Bonafini proceeded to occupy the
targeted building: the Liceo Naval, where the ideology of the navy was repro-
duced among the military candidates. When the rest of the premises were still
in ruins, the cultural centre began to offer concerts, art exhibitions and a vari-
ety of workshops. To some extent, Bonafini envisioned her strategy as peda-
gogical, even contagious. This is particularly provocative when considering a
basic activity that takes place in most domestic spaces: cooking.
Cooking to convert
As far as I could witness at the Tuesday gatherings, part of the Madre’s inten-
tions were realised. The cooking sessions managed to transform the former
military school into an open space where ordinary people gathered to combine
14 Ahmed, The Promise of Happiness, p. 148.
1S Wallis and Duggan, ‘On Trauma’, p. 2.
16 ‘Asi se lo pedi a Kirchner. Yo le dije “mire, no quiero venir mas a las reunions. Todo el
mundo discute lo que quiere, que lugar quieren, cunto va a ser de grande, no me interesa. Yo
quiero el Liceo Naval Militar, porque es ahi donde se dieron las clases, donde se ensefié a matar,
a violar, a torturar. Tenemos que demostrar que en ese mismo lugar podemos hacer otra cosa.
Tenemos que transformar la Esma en un centro de vida’” (my translation).

THE COOKING MOTHER 87
political discussion with practical food recipes. The presence ofa brand-new
kitchen at ESMA suggested an alternative religion, almost a secular Eucharist.
As I will suggest, the process of sharing food proposed a non-biological mode
for the transmission oft rauma: an experience of food-conversion. That Tues-
day Bonafini stood in the middle of the classroom. She mixed the ingredients
in a big saucepan. Her preaching did not stop: ‘Today we are preparing 30
portions of Mexican food for 37 pesos [around 6 pounds]. See how many
healthy meals can be prepared with so little money!’!” Everybody in the room
called her Madre (‘Mother’). As with the filigrees in church, Bonafini asked
the audience to contribute | peso for the handouts (£0.16). The tithe was worth
paying. Less traditional than bread and wine, the handouts included low-
budget recipes for guacamole, tabule, empanadas, pizza and lentil bread. At
the bottom, all the pages bore a name: ‘Hebe’. To some extent, Bonafini’s
signature appeared to endorse certain feelings of ownership over the ingredi-
ents ofa healthy digestion of grief.
During the hourly session, Bonafini could be cheerful, authoritarian or proud
of her Catholic background, but was always pleased to grab the attention of
the audience. While the food was prepared and the political chat continued,
she indulged the audience with anecdotes of the Madres touring conferences
abroad, or with gossip about Evo Morales, Bolivia’s president, one ofh er clos-
est friends. The cooking session also included a session of close reading. Far
from the Bible or even any cookery book, one of Bonafini’s favourite psalms
is Eva Peron’s autobiography La razon de mi vida (‘The Reason of My Life’,
1952). Although Peron’s first wife has been a mythical figure within subsequent
Peronist governments, Fernandez de Kirchner’s administration transformed
her public persona into a new idiosyncratic icon of female power.'® Still, |w as
surprised to notice the passage that Bonafini selected to share with her audience.
She read aloud: ‘Fanaticism is the only way to conquer death. I am a fanatic
of my people. I will die for them, in front of the capitalist imperialism, with or
without weapons.’!’ Like a minister in church, Bonafini interpreted Evita’s
sacred word in line with her own activism: ‘They have said that the Madres
are fanatics. And yes, I am a fanatic in what I do.’ The vindication of a certain
form of rhetorical violence was at least disturbing, especially considering that
17 ‘Hoy vamos a preparar 30 platos de comida mejicana por 37 pesos. Miren cuanta comida
sana se puede cocinar por tan poca plata’ (my translation).
18 To commemorate the 59th anniversary of Eva Perén’s death, on 26 July 2011 two gigantic
metallic portraits were placed on both sides of the building that hosts the Ministry of Social De-
velopment, a key landmark in Buenos Aires’ landscape. The artists, Daniel Santoro and Alejandro
Marmo, worked under the direct supervision of President Fernandez de Kirchner.
19 “‘B] fanatismo es la Gnica manera de vencer a la muerte. Yo soy fanatica de mi pueblo.
Moriria por él, frénte al capitalismo o al imperialismo, con o sin armas.’
20 *‘Algunos dicen que las Madres son fanaticas. Y si, yo soy fanatica de lo que hago’ (my
translation).

88 CECILIA SOSA
it took place inside a kitchen. To some extent, Bonafini seemed to be echoing
H.1.J.0.S.’ re-editing of the radical liturgies that used to dominate their parents’
activism during the 1970s. Watching Bonafini dominating ESMA’s kitchen, I
had the feeling that her performance created the collective scene for a ritual-
istic incorporation and digestion of mourning. First, | would like to show how
the Madre’s attempts to convert the former detention centre could be read in
parallel with her transformation as public persona.
New outfits for a rebel
Even during the dictatorship, Bonafini built her political career by demanding
the impossible, the ‘alive appearance’ of the 30,000 disappeared. As was stated
earlier, when the first mass graves with the remains of the missing were found
in the early 1980s, Bonafini remained faithful to her original demand. In her
stubborn political discourse, the appraisal of reality was not a priority. For dec-
ades, Bonafini has hampered military and democratic administrations with a
pervasive practice of disobedience. In the mid-1990s when the government
offered reparations to the relatives, she complained against the associations that
accepted the money: ‘It’s disgusting that anyone can think of setting a price on
the lives of our 30,000 disappeared.’?! Her tone, or maybe the ‘taste’ of her
political interventions was always harsh, always unpredictable. Within the
global arena, Bonafini celebrated the 9/11 attack arguing that for the first time
the USA had received an ‘invoice’ for the actions performed during its history.”
In the human rights field, Bonafini’s presence has been publicly tolerated but
always difficult to digest.
It was only after Nestor Kirchner took power in 2003 that Bonafini began
her process of conversion. After years of fighting against the state, she gained
a special shield during the Kirchnerist period. These administrations, which
adopted the banner of the victims, also transformed the defiant Madre into a
protected figure. In return, she became one of the main ambassadors of the new
era of memory, at least for a period. During the cooking sessions at ESMA,
Bonafini swore, joked and poked fun at most of the political figures, but she
always referred to the president as ‘Cristina’. Before her eyes, the presidential
couple became the unquestionable popes of her controversial lineage. From their
21 ‘Nos da asco que alguien piense en cobrar y le ponga precio a la vida de nuestros 30,000
desaparecidos.’ At that time, she argued that the reparative funds offered between US$90 and
US$250,000 per disappeared son. See http://www.youtube.com/watch?v=38fXK YQXB9I1&featu
re=share [accessed 5 June 2011].
22 ‘Por primera vez le pasaron la boleta a Estados Unidos por lo que hizo durante toda su
vida.’ See “Polémicas declaraciones de Hebe de Bonafini’, La Nacién, 10 October 2001, http://
www.lanacion.com.ar/341895-polemicas-declaraciones-de-bonafini [accessed 11 November
2011).

THE COOKING MOTHER 89
perspective, Bonafini’s ‘rehabilitation’ proved their solid commitment to human
rights. Even so, Bonafini’s food-performance at ESMA could not easily be as-
similated within normative scripts of mourning. To some extent, she became
the “housekeeper’ of ESMA — and in so doing, she offered an alternative way
of dealing with the incorporation of trauma.
ESMA’s housekeeper
When I first heard a colleague referring to Bonafini as ESMA’s housekeeper,
I was instantly curious. The expression not only made ironic reference to the
institutionalisation of the Madre’s role during the Kirchner era but it also
acknowledged the peculiar rehearsal of gender roles that took place at her
brand-new kitchen. The figute of ‘“ESMA’s housekeeper’ also raises fruitful
resonances with the idea of the ‘bad script’ addressed by Diana Taylor while
analysing the Madres’ circuits of the central square. In Taylor’s perspective,
although the group of women contested the military regime, ‘they left a re-
strictive patriarchal system basically unchallenged’.?? Although I have re-
sponded to Taylor’s analysis here and elsewhere, I would like to explore how
this seemingly ‘bad script’ was displaced at the cooking sessions.** While
considering the kitchen as her new space for activism during the interview,
Bonafini’s response was direct:
I don’t have any academic background. I just needed to find a place where
I could feel comfortable, that was close to me, and this was the kitchen. I
always thought that the kitchen is pure life.*°
Despite Bonafini’s seemingly ‘patriarchal’ script, the way in which she brought
the ‘women’s world’ into play during the cooking lessons at ESMA introduced
a crucial disruption into the skills usually associated with the housewife. In
fact, her intervention seemed to mock common understandings of human
rights’ activism. Her response to attempts at the reification of horror came
from a terrain unexplored by memory scholars: the kitchen. During the inter-
view, I also explored whether this initiative could be read in tune with her
experience at her own family table.
Bonafini comes from a very traditional background. She was born in 1928
in El Dique, a little village next to an oil refinery in the province of Buenos
Aires. The biggest industry of the village was a modest factory where her father
was employed. In her working-class Catholic family, there were few doubts
23 Taylor, Disappearing Acts, p. 191.
24 For an expanded critique of Taylor’s argument, see Sosa, “On Mothers and Spiders’.
25 ‘No tengo ninguna preparacion académica, tenia que buscar un lugar que yo pudiera estar,
que tuviera que ver conmigo y ese lugar es la cocina’ (my translation).

90 CECILIA SOSA
about who should be in the kitchen: ‘The one who cooked at home was always
my mother. She taught me how to do it since I was a child.’* In fact, the first
apron that Bonafini used at ESMA had her inother’s name, Pepa, printed on it.
This is how she recalled her childhood times:
My mother cooked a lot of puchero [a cheap stew] and also soups; simple and
humble food [...] and the snacks! All morning I chopped fennel to feed the
rabbits. We also chopped spinach, and chard that is very nice. We sometimes
had milanesa [schnitzels], but not so much meat. We bred rabbits, chickens,
and my grandmother built a pond, and we had ducks. We had eggs of all
sorts. We ate all this.”’
Bonafini did not recall having a favourite meal. ‘I liked eating a lot. I ate my
food and also what my brother left. I ate everything,’ she told me with a smile.*
I could not avoid picturing the Madre transformed into a girl enthusiastically
eating, storing energy for future fights. Nonetheless, back then her family did
not discuss politics at the table. Bonafini only recalled modest and peaceful
evenings until the Peronist movement began to develop in the early 1940s. Then
the first political quarrels began:
My father was Radical [from the Radical party] and my brother was Peronist
and they used to argue a lot. For many years, we couldn’t talk about politics
at all. But I knew that my dad painted the walls of the neighbourhood for
electoral propaganda. In our backyard there was a container where he
prepared the paste. Kids always know.”
Maybe those hushed divisions in her family prepared Bonafini for dealing
with disagreement. They might also have made her aware of the strange ‘secrets’
that circulate around family tables. Bonafini married young. She was only 14
when her husband built their first house at the back of her mother’s place. She
gave birth to three children; Jorge, Raul and Alejandra; the first two were to
‘disappear’. Years before the military regime, the family moved to La Plata, a
university city in the province of Buenos Aires: ‘This was precisely because I
26 ‘En mi casa cocinaba mi mama. Desde muy chiquita me ensefié a cocinar a mi’ (my translation).
27 ‘Mi mami hacia mucho puchero, mucha sopa, comida humilde. Todas las mafianas me iba
a cortaba inojo para los conejos. Y de paso cortabamos acelga, cardo, que es muy rico. Algunas
milanesas, carne poco. Cridbamos conejos, pollos, gallinas y mi abuela habia hecho un estanque
con patos. huevos de todo tipo, comiamos eso’ (my translation)
28 ‘Me gustaba mucho comer, me comia lo mio y lo que dejaba mi hermano. No tenia prob-
lemas con nada’ (my translation).
29 ‘Mi papa era radical y mi hermano peronista asi que se discutia mucho. En realidad, por
muchos afios no se habl6 de politica en la cocina para nada. Yo sabia que mi papa iba a pintar las
paredes del barrio para hacer propaganda. En el fondo de la casa habia un tacho donde se prepa-
raba el engrudo. Uno era chico y sabia’ (my translation).

THE COOKING MOTHER 9]
wanted to preserve the upbringing of my children. | wanted them to study and
to bring their friends home.’*° Bonafini also recalled those times in that kitchen:
Raul, my youngest son, was very cheerful. He made me dance a chamamé
[a folk dance] while I was frying chips or milanesa and made me ruin all the
food. I never got upset. I have such good memories at the kitchen. Kitchen
always has to do with love.*!
She smiled, recalling how her children used to make fun of her ability to save
and cook for less:
I never threw anything away. I learned this when we were poor, and I kept it
even when my husband worked for YPF [Yacimientos Petroliferos Fiscales,
a large oil company that belonged to the state]. Raul used to tell me: ‘I can
eat whatever you prepare, but let me first do the autopsy.’ If I served a pie, he
would open it to see what was inside.*?
Maybe exorcising the dark insinuations of the ‘pie-autopsy’ in relation to her
son’s never buried body, Bonafini laughed. Her moments of humour reminded
me of H.I.J.O.S.’ spirit, a similar affirmation of life in the face of loss. A similar
bittersweet style was in play when recalling Jorge, her other disappeared son:
Jorge was already married but he came home regularly. ‘Four days a week we
eat chicken, but it is never real chicken,’ he used to complain. He was right.
I used to split the chicken in multiple parts to prepare schnitzels, giblets,
stews. He had a little income, since he handed everything to his activism, and
one day he told me: ‘Mum, would you socialise this chicken for me?’ Last
year at ESMA I showed how to ‘socialise’ a chicken.*?
We laughed together about the episode. The ‘socialisation of the chicken’ was also
prophetic. Years later, at the height of the military period, during the endless circuits
30 ‘Justamente por querer preservar la crianza de mis hijos, que estudiaran, que vinieran con
los amigos a casa’ (my translation).
31 ‘Mi hijo menor Ratl era muy jugueton. Ponia un chamamé y me hacia bailar mientras yo
fritaba papas fritas, o milanesa, hasta que se quemaba la comida. Yo nunca me enojaba. Tengo tan
gratos recuerdos en la cocina. Siempre tiene que ver con cosas de amor’ (my translation).
32 ‘Yo toda la vida guardé todo, nunca tiré nada. Lo aprendi cuando éramos pobres aprendi
eso y cuando estuvimos un poco mejor, cuando mi marido trabajaba en YPF y yo también traba-
jaba. Mi hijo menor, me decia “yo como todo lo que vos hagas, pero dejame primero que le haga
la autopsia”. Si era una tarta la abria para ver qué tenia adentro’ (my translation).
33 ‘Mi hijo mayor Jorge ya estaba casado pero venia siempre. Mi hijo me decia comemos
cuatro dias pollo y nunca comemos pollo. Y claro yo hacia la milanesita, el menudito, los guisitos,
las sopas. Y claré, mi hijo también empezé a tener poco sueldo porque todo lo entregaba a la
militancia. Vino un dia y me dijo: ‘mama socializame este pollo’. El afio pasado ensefié a sociali-
zar un pollo en la ESMA’ (my translation).

92 CECILIA SOSA
of the Plaza de Mayo, the Madres also ‘socialised’ motherhood: they acted for all
the missing, not just for their children. This collective spirit was also present at
ESMA. In fact, Bonafini re-emerged as a housekeeper dressed up with radical
aprons. She mimicked a devoted cook only to jump into the contentious debates
about memory and loss. In those sessions, an intricate combination of political
and gender transgressions worked together. The patriarchal script that confines
women to the kitchen was iterated, slightly subverted. Moreover, the ‘bad script’
was reinhabited as a key source of power. Armed with a big tablespoon, Bonafini
reappeared ready to convert her audience into supporters of an alternative form
of mourning. Once again, she not only cooked for the victims but for all the guests
who came to see what was going on at her socialised kitchen.
The digestion of grief
When considering Bonafini’s intervention as a performance, I am using the
category not merely to highlight its spectacular component. Rather, I wish to
address a political intervention that was strongly attached to the everyday expres-
sion of affects, mainly those related to food. But there is also an extra benefit in
considering the cooking lessons as a performance. These gatherings helped to
make visible something that is still in the process of emergence in contemporary
Argentina, a non-normative narrative of grief. |w ould like to locate this analysis
in the context of Barbara Kirshenblatt-Gimblett’s exhaustive review of the artists
who have used food as a medium of performance. She writes:
Food, and all that is associated with it, is already larger than life. It is already
highly charged with meaning and affect. It is already performative and
theatrical. An art of the concrete, food like performance, is alive, fugitive
and sensory.**
Drawing on this, Bonafini’s insistence on ‘bringing life into a place of death’
not only reads as a personal manifesto that illustrates her ‘anti-museal’ ethos. It
can also be thought of as an opportunity to convert a site of mourning by means
of the sensory affects involved in the act of cooking. In fact, for Kirshenblatt-
Gimblett there is an intimate bond between performance and food: ‘To perform
in this sense is to make food, to serve food. It is about materials, tools, techniques,
procedures, actions. It is about getting something done.’* This perspective sheds
light on the ‘active’ components involved in Bonafini’s initiative. Still, the labour
undertaken by the Madre at the ESMA kitchen was not mainly related to the
production of goods, but also to another ‘performative’ way of dealing with
34 Barbara Kirshenblatt-Gimblett, ‘Playing to the Senses: Food as a Performance Medium’,
Performance Research, 4.1 (1999), 1-30 (p. 1).
35 Kirshenblatt-Gimblett, ‘Playing to the Senses’, p. 1.

THE COOKING MOTHER 93
trauma as a ritual intimately connected to the presence of an audience. ‘Food
events move towards the theatrical and, more specifically, towards the spec-
tacular,” argues Kirshenblatt-Gimblett.*° Apart from the participatory spirit, there
was indeed something ‘spectacular’ involved in the cooking sessions at ESMA.
Bonafini not only did the work for the audience in terms of producing food, but
also in staging a theatrical scene for the collective digestion of mourning. In this
sense, the Madre proposed a secular version of the Eucharist liturgy in which
the possibility of sharing grief became attached to the ritual of eating together.
At ESMA, the very act of sharing bread — or lentils or Mexican food — can be
conceived as a practice of ‘trans-substantiation’. Within the Catholic tradition,
bread and wine are the Eucharist species that change in substance to become
the Body and Blood ofC hrist. In parallel, during the food performances at ESMA
the ingredients of a non-normative grief were somehow created and even ‘trans-
substantiated’ in the bodies of the audience by the ingestion of ‘activist food’
— ultimately an alternative form of sacrament.
To expand further on this argument, I would like to read the cooking sessions
in tune with some considerations raised by Ridout in relation to how contem-
porary artists manage to bring the ‘real’ on stage.” During a symposium at the
Chelsea Theatre, the scholar argued that some forms of experimental theatre
and live art shed light on a set of relations of production which are not neces-
sarily visible or representable, including those in which the performance itself
is produced. Thus, these kind of experimental performances develop a form of
immanent critique of the conditions that made them possible.** Ridout read this
capacity of performance in tune with the theories of ‘inmaterial labour’ devel-
oped by Italian scholars, such as Antonio Negri, Maurizio Lazzarato and
Paolo Virno. In particular, he drew upon the work of Lazzarato to explore the
connection between the service economy and the work that some performers
deploy on stage. While suggesting a parallel between the aesthetics of post-
Fordist capitalism and contemporary performance, Ridout argued that current
forms of service economy are not mainly concerned with the production of
goods but of emotions. Contemporary performers also manage to generate
‘affective labour’ on stage. Ridout’s reading fits well with the way in which
Negri and Michael Hardt have addressed ‘affective labour’ as producing social
relations, communication and ‘forms of community’. Yet not only the kind
36 Kirshenblatt-Gimblett, ‘Playing to the Senses’, p. 2.
37 | am discussing here a paper that Nicholas Ridout presented at the Chelsea Theatre on 3
November 2009 during the Sacred Festival. The paper is entitled ‘Performing the Real: Econom-
ics and Aesthetics’ (unpublished). I am grateful to Ridout for giving me the permission to quote
from his notes.
38 Ridout, ‘Performing the Real’, n.p.
39 Michael Hardt and Antonio Negri, Empire (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press,
2000), p. 293.

94 CECILIA SOSA
of expertise and skills required by immaterial labor could be compared to the
virtuosity of performing artists. Moreover, as Ridout argued, ‘today’s experi-
mental performance might have some structural relation to the practices of the
service economy’.*° In his perspective, while constantly looking for new ways
of communication, certain immersive theatrical experiences manage to recon-
figure the relation between production and consumption through a practice of
‘activation’ of the audience.
Drawing from this insight, it might be appealing to explore what was the
‘service’ produced in the ESMA kitchen. Indeed, Bonafini’s cooking sessions
can be considered as a way of performing the real on stage. Like some contem-
porary performers, Bonafini’s intervention at ESMA contributed to produce an
alternative discourse of mourning in the aftermath of violence. Her performance
made ‘visible’ something that had not been articulated in the wider society. Her
sessions solicited an alternative form of conviviality in the wake of loss, one
which could be conceived as a secular communion. Thus, Bonafini’s way of
engaging and rehearsing trauma through the experience of sharing food made
grief available for expanded audiences. This could be read in tune with Ander-
mann’s reassessment of Marianne Hirsch’s notion of ‘post-memory’ as an em-
pathic form of transmission of trauma.*! Under this light, the Madre’s gatherings
generated an alternative public in ways that exceed the ‘direct victims’ of violence,
who have mostly endorsed the legitimacy of remembering in the post-dictato-
rial period. Bonafini’s performance brought to light a new form of community
in a former space of death. This is the form of sociality that Bonafini has produced
at ESMA, one that, in tune with contemporary performance and the service
economy, can also be conceived as ‘affective labour’. Moreover, in a period
during which discourses of mourning tended to be officialised, Bonafini’s in-
tervention reminded her audience that non-conventional forms of engaging with
loss were still emerging and being intensively negotiated. In the small unit where
the Madre operates, the ad hoc kitchen of a former detention centre, life became
inseparable from work, mainly a non-normative work of mourning. The emo-
tions mobilised through the food performances facilitated a live digestion of the
resonances of trauma among less implicated audiences. In so doing, Bonafini
offered an antidote to official politics of remembering, still marked by the ‘bio-
logical’ normativity supported by the ‘wounded family’. I would suggest that
this has been the ‘virtuosity’ and also the ‘immanent critique’ acknowledged by
Bonafini as a contemporary performer of the real.
While researching on food performances in Australia, Edward Scheer argued
that “digestive theatre’ can be conceived of as a style of performance that is
easily consumed by the audience, which ‘leaves the theatre edified and re-
40 Ridout, ‘Performing the Real’, n.p.
41 Andermann, ‘Returning to the Site of Horror’, p.81.

THE COOKING MOTHER 95
warded by the re-affirmation of middle-class values’? Yet Bonafini provided
a different ‘service’ for her audience: she staged a new narrative of mourning
for those who might not feel ‘naturally’ enabled, non-kin fellows in grief. In
so doing, the right to grieve became expanded to those who came to ESMA
to share a common meal. Food emerged as an expanded way of partaking in
mourning. In this sense, Bonafini’s food-performances also managed to expand
the table of victims.
Queering the table of victims
I suggest that the cooking sessions at ESMA could be conceived as peculiar
gatherings in which the table of victims became queered. To frame this argu-
ment I would like to draw att€ntion to Ahmed’s reflections on tables. In ‘Ori-
entations. Towards a Queer Phenomenology’, Ahmed describes the table as a
kinship object that gives form to the family; the table is the tangible thing over
which the family gathers and becomes normatively socialised.** Moreover,
she suggests that a queer politics might offer a different angle on tables to
include those ‘whose lives and loves make them appear oblique, strange, and
out of place. The table becomes queer when it provides such support.’“* Draw-
ing upon this, it could be argued that Bonafini’s performance at ESMA also
queered traditional family tables, in particular that of the ‘wounded family’,
those who have been directly affected by violence. In fact, for the relatives’
associations, some ‘external supporters’, those who are not ‘real victims’,
might also look ‘strange’ or ‘oblique’. This form of exclusive and private grief
was contested in Bonafini’s kitchen. Without doubt, the dictatorship did not
affect all parts of Argentine society in the same way. Yet beyond differential
ways of being touched by violence, the traces of this multi-layered trauma
could be felt in the brand-new kitchen at ESMA. There, Bonafini proposed an
extension of the family of victims while opening up alternative modes of
partaking in grief.
In her work, Ahmed has been keen to describe queer gatherings as family
encounters. This perspective enables her ‘to have joy in the uncanny effect of
a familiar form becoming strange’.** In similar vein, I have also approached the
cooking sessions as family gatherings in which the biological foundation of the
‘wounded family’ becomes queered. In doing so, I also aim to stress the non-
normative pleasure that emerged at the former detention centre, where family
42 Edward Scheer, ‘Ritual of Incorporation: Food and Performance in Sydney, Australia’,
Performance Research, 4.1 (1999), 137-4] (p. 137).
43 Sara Ahmed, ‘Orientations. Towards a Queer Phenomenology’, GLQ: A Journal of Les-
bian and Gay Studies, 12.4 (2006), 543-74 (p. 559).
44 Ahmed, ‘Orientations’, p. 570.
45 Ahmed, ‘Orientations’, p. 569.

96 CECILIA SOSA
dinners were recreated in ways that, for some non-converted guests, might also
taste uncanny. At ESMA’s kitchen, the privileges of blood have been undermined.
In fact, the experience of conversion not only involved a former site of death,
but also the guests of an on-biological community, especially those who were
not directly touched by violence. While sharing common food, the guests were
able to participate in an experience of mourning that might not feel entirely
theirs. In this sense, Bonafini emerged as a political leader who has the ability
to create other families and tables, making space for audiences that might also
feel ‘strangers’ within traditional narratives of grief.
Hebe de Bonafini and Bobby Baker: a virtual encounter
While researching Bonafini’s sessions at ESMA, many scholars indicated to
me the strong resonance that these interventions had in relation to the work
developed by Bobby Baker, one of the most popular artists in Britain working
in live art and experimental theatre. Over the last twenty years, Baker has
staged a series of solo performances in which the kitchen, food and female
roles have been in the foreground. The titles of her interventions, Edible Fam-
ily (1976), Mobile Home (1976), Kitchen Show (1991) and Table Occasion
(1997), already invite a dialogue with Bonafini’s food experiments at ESMA.
Here, I propose reading both trajectories together in the hope that Baker’s
work can shed light on how Bonafini’s experience of conversion does not
imply a denial oft he past.
Baker’s and Bonafini’s work seem to iterate the traditional association between
emotions and the feminine. Their performances bear the traces of ‘excessive’
feelings that are usually relegated to the private sphere. They also highlight
collective emotions that are part of the public sphere. Besides, from different
perspectives, both activists have explored the figure of the mother-artist and the
artist-cook. In March 2010 I had the opportunity to have an extensive dialogue
with Baker at her home in north London. | was curious about how she would
respond to the ESMA experience. From the beginning, the artist was touched.
She told me that the cooking lessons reminded her of Irish funerals in which
food is left inside the coffin next to the body. ‘To bring death into conscience
you have to eat it,’ Baker said.*° Her insight was revealing: it contributed to shed
light on the strong ties between mourning and digestion that were also at the
foreground of the gatherings at ESMA. More personally, it also helped me to
articulate how audiences not directly affected by violence could adopt the ex-
perience of mourning by sharing a meal. During our meeting, I showed Baker
some of the pictures that I had taken at the cooking sessions. One particularly
46 Unless otherwise indicated, quotations from Bobby Baker are taken from an interview I
conducted on 10 March 2009 in London.

THE COOKING MOTHER 97
captivated her. The image portrayed Bonafini wearing a Che Guevara apron and
chopping ingredients in the ESMA kitchen. She told me:
It is impressive to see this woman standing with this apron and cooking. You
don’t need to be there to feel the power of this experience, the dignity of her
work. She is steady.
‘Steady’: the word kept resonating. Later, I realised that Baker’s description
made reference to both a physical constitution and a psychic character involved
in the Madre’s presence. It reminded me of that childhood anecdote recalled by
Bonafini in which she had commented on her vocation of ‘eating everything’,
even ‘what her brother left’. Somehow, I thought that the contingent maternal
assertion, ‘You need to eat to grow big and strong’, was also present at ESMA.
There as well, Bonafini performed as the mother of all, not only of her disap-
peared children, nor even of the relatives, but also of all those who might come
to join the brand-new table at the former navy school. Somehow, the discussion
with Baker returned to the issue of gender roles, which has also intensively
shaped the British artist’s performances. In this respect, Baker argued:
Cooking could be seen as the most essential aspect of family support, and
also the most subordinated. However, in this context it becomes a political
act. This woman transforms an everyday act into a big statement. Nothing
could be more empowering than going to this place to have a meal. It is an
act of resistance. Like Auschwitz, ESMA bears fragments of death and loss.
The act of cooking is life-giving, is simple, is profound, and also a quite
humble action.
Humility might not be one of Bonafini’s main attributes. Even so, while listening
to Baker, the connection between these two women became more significant, as
if they were responding to each other without knowing of each other’s existence.
For instance, in Drawing on a Mother ’s Experience (1988), Baker recited the
story of her life while wrapping herself in a white sheet made from the contents
of shopping bags. In a similar way, testimonial and biographical content was also
at the core of Bonafini’s cooking sessions. Whereas in Baker’s performance the
experience of the mother was framed by traditional housework, in Bonafini’s
case ‘motherhood’ was revealed through a scarf that was replaced by an apron.
While Baker usually staged her body as the self-effacing, malleable and tortuous
surface of female traumas, Bonafini has not stopped monumentalising herself.
As much as Baker embraced the role of housekeeper as a way of contesting
gender positioning, Bonafini has attached her gastronomic performance to cel-
ebrate her rising political trajectory as the cook of a converted grief.
In considering Table Occasion, Adrian Heathfield notices that what is most
striking about Baker’s work ‘is not its humorous use of the social formality and
restriction but the foundations of pain and fear upon which this playfulness is

98 CECILIA SOSA
seen to rest’.4”7 In an unexpected way, Heathfield’s description also helped me
to account for the atmosphere created at ESMA’s kitchen. Although these gath-
erings bore witness to Bonafini’s personal tragedy, her intervention has not
staged her as a victim but rather as the playful cook of another future. Despite
the affirmative mood of the venues, the ‘foundation of pain and fear’ has also
been their landscape. Moreover, loss is what made the food sessions possible.
In fact, this foundation of pain shows how Bonafini’s attempts at the “conver-
sion’ of ESMA do not rely on denying the past, but rather on showing how this
past could bring new affective encounters in the present. To grasp this, it is worth
considering the significant resonances that persist throughout Heathfield’s read-
ing of Baker’s work. He writes:
Standing in the centre of the table, Baker serves her fare to a gathering of
absent friends and family whose ghostly presence infiltrates her speech. As
her serving progresses, the formal conventions which sustain the order of
the meal are surpassed with increasing frequency by digressive anecdotes,
intrusions of memory and returns of the repressed.**
The echoes are engaging. In fact, the ghostly presence of ‘absent friends and
family’ that Heathfield detects at Baker’s performance has been also crucial
at Bonafini’s table. If the Argentine mother managed to transform the former
navy school into a dining space, the lives that were made to vanish at the il-
legal centre were not excluded from those gatherings; rather they became
guests at the ESMA table. Like Baker in Zable Occasion, the cooking sessions
proposed a spectacle of conversion that propitiated the encounter with ‘infil-
trated’ presences, ‘digressive anecdotes’ and ‘intrusions of memory’. In so
doing, the ESMA kitchen also welcomed a collection of anachronistic guests.
For instance, the ghost of a missing son could always return to make the
mother dance in the kitchen. Or the secrets of a ‘chicken socialisation’, once
shared at her family dinner, could be transmitted to a larger audience of non-
kin guests. Moreover, these ‘infiltrated presences’ showed how the expanded
table configured at the former centre also hosted the desire to create affective
communities across time. This sense of temporal multiplicity mobilised by
the food could be framed as a form of ‘queer temporality’, which the queer
theorist Carolyn Dinshaw associates with ‘an expanded range of temporal
experiences — experiences not regulated by “clock” time or by a conceptuali-
sation of the present as singular and fleeting; experiences not narrowed by the
idea that time moves steadily forward’. In this sense, I suggest, the ESMA
47 Adrian Heathfield, ‘Risk in Intimacy: An Interview with Bobby Baker’, Performance
Research, 4.1 (1999), 97-106 (p. 97).
48 Heathfield, ‘Risk in Intimacy’, p. 97.
49 Dinshaw, Edelman and Ferguson, ‘Theorizing Queer Temporalities’, p. 185.

THE COOKING MOTHER 99
kitchen not only invited contemporary guests but also those coming from the
past and also from the future.
As if persistently engaging in a secret dialogue with Argentina’s traumatic
past, in Edible Family Baker performed the scene of the family dissolution. This
is how the artist referred to this experience in Heathfield’s article:
With this specific piece I was thrilled at the prospect of the family
disappearing; that the work would be lost and that it would be absorbed into
other people’s bodies.°°
In conceiving eating as ‘absorbing’, Baker made a fundamental link between
food and loss. In a similar way, the recreation of a collective meal has been
what made grief edible at the-former detention centre. If at her family kitchen
Bonafini ‘socialised’ chicken, ESMA has been the occasion for ‘socialising’
mourning, and ultimately making it ‘absorbable’; not only among traditional
guests but also among expanded communities across time. While Baker’s food
events engaged with an audience that actually afe the artwork, Bonafini’s
intervention staged the conditions through which trauma could be incorpo-
rated within a non-biological family. I do not want to suggest that loss was
not visible in post-dictatorship Argentina, but rather that the food perfor-
mances offered an expanded stage in which loss could circulate and become
available for wider audiences.
Finally, Heathfield argues that Baker’s project explores the everyday rituals
of women’s practice ‘not simply to assert it as an unacknowledged creativity,
but to mine its hidden sensuality and joy’.*! In parallel, Bonafini’s intervention
also introduced glimpses of joy within the experience of mourning. Thirty years
after the end of the military regime, a temporal gap has been opened in relation
to the traumatic event., Now there are other audiences that bring new perspec-
tives to engage with that past. There are second-hand witnesses who bring al-
ternative stories to infuse with life the former site of fear and pain. Bonafini
acknowledged this displacement through a change of outfit:
Scarves are for political acts. If we [the Madres] are invited to stay for dinner,
we take the scarves off. The cooking lessons may have a political touch, but
the kitchen is not a demonstration. If I wore the scarf, people would feel
a distance, they would not look at me as a peer. Scarves are to intimidate
enemies, not those who are with us.”
50 Heathfield, ‘Risk in Intimacy’, p. 98.
51 Heathfield, ‘Risk in Intimacy’, p. 97.
52 ‘El pafiuelo se usa para actos politicos. Si nos invitan a una cena, nos los sacamos. La
cocina aunque yo le doy un tono politico, no es un acto politico. Si tuviera pafiuelo la gente me
veria mucho mas lejos. El pafiuelo es para que nos tengan miedo los enemigos, no la gente que
esta con nosotros’ (my translation).

100 CECILIA SOSA
Bonafini’s change of outfits suggested another stage in the digestion of grief.
Now is not the time for threats, but for more expanded forms of community. It
is time to build new ties and families in the face of loss. It is time to set up a
new table and feed new guests.
An ‘impure’ family romance
Bonafini’s capacity to recreate a new family at ESMA could be read in tune with
her own biography, which seems to be constitutively attached to loss. Bonafini
has built and lost many families in her life. Soon after her children were kid-
napped, she broke relations with her biological family: ‘They were all fachos
milicos [fascist military], or they hung out with them.’ she told me in relation
to her mother’s side of the family.* In fact, Bonafini’s suspicion has been that
her relatives were involved in the kidnapping of her children:
After my eldest son was taken, I asked if there could be a way to see him
but they refused to help me. That day I told my mother ‘Today, I buried my
family. I don’t have a family any longer.’ Sometimes your family is not the
one you are left by inheritance but the one you choose. One builds families.”
Bonafini was eventually forced to conceive alternative forms of support beyond
bloodline ties. This process could help to explain the relationship that she eventu-
ally built with Sergio Schoklender, an infamous man who had been condemned for
murdering his parents in concert with his youngest brother in 1981 when they were
both teenagers. ‘Sergio began to send us supportive letters from jail each time the
Madres organised a demonstration. He was always punished for that,’ Bonafini told
me during our interview.** While in jail, Schoklender received a university degree
in law. After he was released in 1995, his role became progressively more central
within the Madres. Eventually, he was Bonafini’s right hand, and also her ‘stepson’.
The relationship between the mother of two missing activists and a ‘parricide’ was
particularly difficult to digest within the orthodoxy of the relatives. Although the
general public never ceased to be suspicious, for many years this non-normative
bond became part of the landscape of the victims’ associations.
The Kirchner governments placed a new test on this peculiar relationship. A
corruption scandal emerged at the heart of the Madres’ Foundation, which tar-
53 ‘Mi familia de parte de mi madre son todos fachos, 0 eran milicos 0 se juntaron con los
milicos’ (my translation).
54 “Después de que se llevaran a mi hijo mayor, pregunté si no lo podia ver. Y me dijeron que
no. Yo le dije a mi mama: ‘Hoy enterré a toda la familia. Yo no tengo mas familia.’ A veces la
familia no es la que uno le queda de herencia, es la que uno elige. Uno se arma familias’ (my
translation).
55 ‘Sergio empezé a mandar adhesiones a las madres, cada vez que hacia una adhesion a una
marcha de las madres, lo encerraban en un altillo’ (my translation).

THE COOKING MOTHER 101]
geted Schoklender as the main figure responsible. In June 2011 the éommis-
sioner of the Foundation was accused of using public funds for personal enrich-
ment. By that time, Swefios compartidos had become an enterprise which received
more than 756 million pesos from the national budget for social housing.°°
During the scandal, Schoklender resigned and the long-term relationship with
Bonafini was suddenly and publicly broken. At the time of my interview, how-
ever, this episode had not yet been revealed, and for Bonafini ‘Sergio’ was still
‘like a son’.*’ Her office was covered with drawings by Schoklender’s 9-year-old
adopted son, Alejandro, who called her ‘grandmother’. Bonafini described these
paintings with delight:
These are the Madres holding hands; and this is me, the one with the red
lipstick. When he was five he asked me ‘why if you looked so long for your
children couldn’t you find them?’ It is still so hard to reply to that, it shakes
everything.*®
At the height of the scandal, the reaction of the relatives’ associations was
defensive. ‘They are trying to get the scarves dirty,’ read the collective state-
ment signed by Abuelas, Madres Linea Fundadora and H.I.J.0.S., among
other associations of the victims.’ Rather than the tortuous nuances of the
case, | am interested in highlighting how the reaction of the ‘wounded family’
was still constructed around the privileges of blood. The relatives organically
responded by evoking the ‘whiteness’ of the scarves. Certainly, the ‘con-
tamination’ that they were addressing was not only related to the administra-
tion of funding. After all, the bond between the Madre and her stepson had
never met the standards of ‘purity’ required by the relatives. In an attempt to
secure its own legitimacy, the Kirchnerist administration put all its efforts into
saving Bonafini from the public resonances of the scandal.® The price was
high: the Madre had to get rid of her deviant son. By the end of 2011, Schok-
lender, accused of crimes of fraud against the government and money launder-
56 From 2008, 4,788 flats and houses were to be built by the Foundation Suefios Compartidos
in seven provinces of the country, and 1,288 social homes had already been built in Buenos Aires.
See La Nacion, 24 July 2011, http://www.lanacion.com.ar/1391981-registran-junto-a-las-madres-
a-un-jefe-de-inteligencia [accessed 25 July 2011].
57 ‘Sergio es como un hijo’ (my translation).
58 ‘Me llama “abuela”. Estas somos las Madres de la mano, y ésta soy yo, con los labios
rojos pintados. A los 5 afios, me pregunté por qué si buscaste tanto a tus hijos no los encontraste?
Es tan dificil contestar eso, te sacude todo’ (my translation).
59 ‘Intentan ensuciar los panuelos’; see http://www.paginal2.com.ar/diario/el-
pais/1-169971-2011-06-12.html [accessed 15 November 2011].
60 Guido Braslavksy, ‘La Rosada reuneva su tactica de apuntalamiento politico de Hebe’,
Clarin, 16 July 2011, http://www.clarin.com/politica/Rosada-tactica-apuntalamiento-politico-
Hebe_0_518348427.html [accessed 19 July 2011].

102 CECILIA SOSA
ing, had to explain in court the destiny of 50 million pesos.®' Eventually, he
went back to prison for a brief period and was released again in July 2012.
Since then, the former commissioner has pursued a personal campaign to
implicate Bonafini. The case is still under investigation. As far as I know,
Bonafini has not seen her step-grandson again. Even so, the break in this
unusual family does not necessarily imply that all non-normative ties are
condemned to failure. After all, they are the inevitable space of love, politics
and dining tables.
A kitchen in a Human Rights City
Before returning to the UK after my 2009 field trip, I attended a last cooking
session. I invited Vivi Tellas to come with me. She is one of the most respected
theatre directors in Argentina and her work inspired me to consider Bonafini’s
cooking lessons as a performance.” That evening, sitting on school chairs, we
shared a Mexican meal: chicken, peas and chocolate sauce. The huge saucepan
was left empty. Standing at the back of the kitchen, Bonafini looked tired, per-
haps older. ‘It is the final moment when the actor stops, the adrenaline is still
circulating but the energy, the rhythm slows down. Then, the euphoric mood
will come,’ whispered Tellas. Before leaving, I approached Bonafini to say
goodbye. She was euphoric. ‘Cooking at ESMA is like having dinner at home.
It is like a party,’ she said, and laughed.
The uncertain projections of a tiny kitchen at the former navy school also
invite a consideration of an alternative form of ethics, one that is closer to fragil-
ity and contingency. In contrast to memorials, artworks or museums, Bonafini’s
cooking sessions seem to have no clear legacy. In the Madre’s kitchen there was
no enduring inheritance, other than the traces of chat and food shared in each
session. It was precisely the fugitive character of these gatherings that might
have helped the audience to glimpse a non-normative act of grief and another
relation to loss. Unlike more stable sites of remembrance, the theatricality of
the kitchen sited at ESMA points towards a collective digestion of grief during
an ephemeral supper that can always be the last one. The cooking sessions also
enhanced their fugitive condition within traditional repertoires of memory. In-
stead of the compulsory acknowledgement of trauma, the live, embodied and
61 ‘*Schoklender, a indagatoria’, Pagina 12, 27 December 2011, http://www.paginal2.com.
ar/diario/ultimas/20-184261-2011-12-27.html [accessed 28 April 2014].
62 For her ‘Bio-drama’ series, Tellas draws upon the autobiographical experiences of or-
dinary people who develop special connections with their audiences. Tellas has developed four
different pieces following these rules: Mi mama y mi tia, Fildsofos con bigotes, Cozarinsky y
su médico and Escuela de conduccion. \n each performance she worked with real people, tak-
ing their world as the point of departure and assuming that each person is a living archive in-
volving experiences, texts, knowledge and images. See http://www.archivotellas.com.ar/ [ac-
cessed 7 August 2011].

THE COOKING MOTHER 103
precarious character of these venues seems to be deeply connected With the
feelings of exposure and vulnerability that emerge from loss.
One of the main differences that distinguishes the food sessions from
other experiences currently in place at ESMA is that audiences have not come
‘already-made’ to Bonafini’s kitchen. As she told me, many of those who
regularly came to the meetings were not familiar with the group’s long-term
activism. By contrast, audiences were forged in the space of performance. The
experience of food-communion has brought a new public into being. The
cooking gatherings reconfigured the relation between stage and auditorium.
From the traces of trauma, a displaced ceremony of the Eucharist has been
evoked. This ceremony of conversion bore the traces of a ritual of metamor-
phosis that took place through food. Vestments have not necessarily been
pious. A Madre dressed in arf apron with Che Guevara’s face led them. Her
kitchen provided an experimental stage on which trauma could be ingested.
It offered refuge to non-conventional mourners, those who have been persis-
tently excluded from the rights of remembering.
The cooking sessions speak about a way of conjuring the past without forget-
ting it. They have staged a peculiar intersection of corporeality, space and time
in relation to loss. Stories of trauma have not been left behind. Rather, they work
as a reminder that ‘the social, political, and aesthetic relations of the present are
always already composed of, and haunted by loss’, as Gust A. Yep writes in a
different context.® The bodies that were made to disappear at ESMA still haunt
its premises. As much as non-kin guests bring fresh air to all family meetings,
the cooking sessions showed how loss could also bring spaces of joy and dia-
logue. In this respect, the prospect of the conversion of the former military school
acquired enhanced meaning. ESMA might eventually become a ‘Human Rights
City’ but it will be always inhabited by its past. The ‘newness’ of the former
detention centre is still to be discovered. But at least the ‘coming after’ could
be negotiated at the table.
Food combines biology and social life. It bridges the private and the public
through rituals of everyday life. The secrets and taboos that circulate during
family dinners have also been part of ESMA’s expanded table. If the family
meal is always a source of drama, Bonafini’s performance ultimately managed
to make this drama public. The food gatherings invited an exploration of trauma
away from individual symptoms and closer to a communal experience. They
also suggested a different temporality of political life. The flavours of the past
can always reinhabit the present. In this sense, the kitchen at ESMA showed
how inhabiting a place could be a way of transforming it, and also a way of
awakening it to new scenes and smells. The kitchen at ESMA showed how
63 Gust A. Yep, ‘The Politics of Loss and its Remains in Common Threads: Stories from the
Quilt’, Rhetoric and Public Affairs, 10.4 (2007), 681-70 (p. 682).

104 CECILIA SOSA
mourning not only destroys but also creates spaces, bodies and subjectivities.
In this re-occupation, new lineages are also being formed, maybe from something
as basic as ‘cooking and chatting’. The cooking gatherings invited new guests
to join the table. They explored alternative ways in which loss could be di-
gested in a common environment. In so doing, they also made loss palatable.

The Attire of (Post-)Memory: Mi vida después
A cascade of clothes falls on to an empty stage. A woman in her early twenties
emerges from the mountain of fabric and picks out a pair of jeans. She tries them
on and walks to the front of the stage approaching the audience with her hands
in her pockets: =
When I was seven, I used to get dressed up in my mum’s clothes and parade
around the house like a tiny queen [...] Twenty years later I find a pair of my
mum’s Lee jeans from the seventies, and they fit me just right. I put on the
jeans and start to walk towards the past."
Facing the audience, the woman plays a frantic guitar solo. Meanwhile, five
other youngsters cross the stage and start rummaging through the pile of clothes
(see Figure 4, p. 78). The performers throw the pieces of fabric into the air in an
uneasy fight with the costumes of the past. This is the opening scene of Mi vida
después. Lola Arias’s production was premiered in Buenos Aires in March 2009
at the Teatro Nacional Sarmiento.’ The first time I attended this performance was
on a warm Thursday in April 2009. The crowd that gathered at the front of the
venue looked diverse. Apart from youngsters slightly over-conscious of their
intellectual looks — classical habitués at Arias’s productions — there were also
middle-aged couples dressed up for a special night out, former activists, elders
and groups of students sporting alternative neo-hippie outfits. A promotional offer,
which included a 30 per cent discount on the standard tickets, contributed to the
mixed audience. The contrast was striking: at an official venue belonging to the
national network of theatres, we were about to witness a provocative hour-long
show that proposed a deviant deconstruction of official narratives of grief.
Mi vida después was supposed to be released as part of Vivi Tellas’s series
of ‘bio-drama’. The well-known director invited Arias to participate in her series
1 All quotations from the play in this chapter are taken from My Life After (2009), by Lola
Arias, translated by Daniel Tunnard (unpublished). Thanks to the director for permission to quote
from the text. I am not providing quotations in Spanish since this is the ‘official’ translation of the
play and recognised as such by the author.
2 The venue bears the name of one of Argentina’s national heroes, Domingo Faustino Sarm-
iento (1811-88), the seventh president of the country and member of the so-called “Generation of
1837’, a group of intellectuals deeply concerned with educational issues.

106 CECILIA SOSA
of documentary performances that she launched at the Sarmiento theatre. As
Tellas eventually resigned as artistic director of this venue, Arias’s production
was finally released as an autonomous piece. Faithful to the ‘bio-drama’ prin-
ciple of enacting real lives on stage, Mi vida despues presents the stories of six
professional actors who were born during the dictatorship. Their backgrounds
are very different. Alongside descendants of guerilla activists, exiled intellectu-
als and a police officer working for the military, the production also includes
testimonies from those who are not usually considered as victims, such as the
son of ab ank employee and the offspring of ac onverted priest. The play is based
on the personal experiences of these actors, their real stories. The actors show
pictures oft heir parents, they read their letters and in some cases they enact their
deaths. Childhood memories, nightmares and family gossip send the audience
back and forth, from the past to the present, and also to the future. As the first
scene shows, the premise is so simple that it is childish: putting on the parents’
clothes so as to re-enact their youth. The pile of clothes works as the perfect
medium to step into a time machine. As if it were a science-fiction film, the
actors alternate between motor racers, priests, guerrillas and bank employees in
performing the most spectacular stunts of their parents’ lives.
Looking at the young cast wearing those old clothes on stage, I got the feel-
ing that a strange dislocation of time was taking place. The actors did not
embody their parents’ lives — how could they? — but rather their own ambiva-
lent versions of them. Still, something profoundly uncanny emerged from this
particular combination of costumes and flesh. To some extent, it reminded me
of Aiofe Monks’s argument that costumes and actors become indistinguishable
in the experience of performance.’ ‘Costumes have a peculiar half-life: they
are not quite objects and not quite actors.’* More than ever, within Arias’s
production the ghostly character of these clothes is material of contestation. In
fact, through their ‘half-life’ quality, the descendants revisit and re-inhabit the
lives of their parents to revise their own heritage. Indeed, the costumes also
speak about different desires infused by the performers onstage. In this sense,
it is not that the past is represented but rather it becomes enacted and twisted
right in front of the audience. Strangely enough, those deferred impossible
scenes flash back, “to interrogate the present in a moment of danger’, as Walter
Benjamin once put it.°
In this chapter I would like to consider the impact of Marianne Hirsch’s idea
of ‘post-memory’ in the context of Argentina’s post-dictatorship. I will examine
whether this structure of transmission, specially crafted to address the experi-
ences of those who were not direct witnesses of traumatic events, could shed
3 Aoife Monks, The Actor in Costume (Basingstoke: Palgrave Macmillan, 2009), p. 7.
4 Monks, The Actor in Costume, p. 121.
5 Walter Benjamin, ‘Theses on the Philosophy of History’, in ///wminations, ed. H. Arendt,
trans. H. Zohn (London: Pimlico, 1968), pp. 245-55 (p. 247).

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 107
light on the debates that are currently taking place in the country.° Drawing on
Arias’s production and a series of interviews conducted with the director and
the actors in Buenos Aires during April 2009 and January 2010, I shall explore
the extent to which personal testimonies can travel off the stage to build new
affiliations in the present. In this sense, I want to show how Mi vida después
displays an uneasy machine of affects that helps to explore the resonances of
grief beyond those usually considered as victims. Ultimately, I shall explore the
possibilities of adopting trauma in the context of spectatorship.
A time machine
For the last ten years, Arias has pursued a programme that includes theatrical
plays, songs, poems, installatfons, live concerts and urban performances. This
repertoire acquired cult status among students, artists and filmmakers of her
generation, those who are now in their thirties.” Since her debut Escudlida
familia (translated for foreign audiences as 4 Kingdom, A Country or a Waste-
land in the Snow, 2001), Arias has contributed to the emergence of a circuit
of ‘post-memory’ directors, mostly born during or after the dictatorship and
largely based in Buenos Aires. Contrasting with the ostensible dry humour
and neo-realist stories introduced by the New Argentine Cinema discussed in
Chapter 3, this lineage of theatre-makers has offered slightly more stylish and
playful experimental proposals, which have been keen to include live music,
screens, surreal poetry and dance tandems on stage. Overall, these productions
have been mostly reluctant to deal with controversial issues from the past, as
if they were separating a playful tone from contamination by over-exposed
questions with respect to the politics of memory. The autonomy of subjects
and styles among this wave of directors became a sort of aesthetic platform
that furtively denied the demands for a political revision of the past guided
by the Kirchnerist period. Arias was no exception to this tradition, until Mi
vida después.
To ground the kind of problems that I am interested in exploring here, I need
to rewind to the first scene of the show. Once again, we see the actor dressed in
her mother’s jeans travelling to the past. On a bus route, she runs into her still
young parents. They all take a motorbike ride around Buenos Aires. This is how
the actor pictures that fantastic trip: ‘My dad’s at the front, then my mum, then
me at the back, with the wind hitting me so hard it’s as if it’s trying to erase my
face.’ Ostensibly, the wind that attempts to efface the actor embodies the force
that the first generation have on the experience of the descendants. In fact, this
problem has been of central interest in Hirsch’s work. As the daughter of a
6 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 103.
7 For an expanded analysis of Lola Arias’s work, see Cecilia Sosa, ‘Lola Arias: Expanding
the Real’, in No More Drama, ed. Peter Crowley (Dublin: Project Press, 2011), pp. 46-66.

108 CECILIA SOSA
Holocaust survivor, she has argued that second-generation witnesses connect
so deeply with the previous generation’s remembrances that these experiences
seem to constitute memories in their own right.’ Unlike contemporary wit-
nesses, Hirsch contends that for descendants oft he victims of traumatic events
the past is ‘not actually mediated by recall but by imaginative investment,
projection and creation’.’ In fact, the scholar warns that the main risk involved
in this affective structure of transmission.is that descendants may have ‘their
own stories and experiences displaced and even evacuated by those of a previ-
ous generation’.'° Indeed, this sort of displacement foregrounds Arias’s produc-
tion. Nonetheless, I wish to make the case that, from the first scene onwards,
Mi vida después develops a critical machine to fight against this occupation. In
doing so, it also provides me with an exemplary case to critically engage with
Hirsch’s model of post-memorial transmission.
The very idea of a time machine speaks about a new technology of memo-
ry taking place on stage, one that gives a sense of a tense negotiation between
descendants and parental figures. Two generations are back together; they face
each other, they are both young. In that fantastic encounter of costumes and
time, Mi vida después confronts the audience with two undercover assumptions.
Not only can past stories be revised, recreated and even transmitted from one
generation to another, but also the re-examination of the past can be a means of
discovering something different about the future. Far from being just mannequins
of their parents, the new generation literally act their own futures. In this sense,
Arias’s production can be read as a provocative attempt at cross-dressing the
parental figures that defies time. As Monks suggests, ‘theatre invokes and in-
vents bodies through the act of crossing, and changing clothes can mean chang-
ing bodies on the stage and in the audience’.'' To be precise, while proposing
a creative entanglement between actors and performers, costumes and charac-
ters, documentary and life, Mi vida después creates an empowered fiction for
the present, one in which the new generation have power on their side. Let us see
how it works on stage.
What we have left
Arias’s production operates through the objects that the actors have inherited
from their parents: family pictures, home videos, old tapes, letters, toys, books,
pictures, even a turtle. On the verge of being forgotten, these minor objects
become souvenirs. They are not only individual spoils, but also cultural treas-
ures. These objects are brought back on stage to test their resonance in the
8 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 106.
9 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 107.
10 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 107.
1l_ Monks, The Actor in Costume, p. 97.

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 109
present. At an early point, Mariano Speratti shows a toy car to the atidience:
‘This is the Bugatti Type 35C that my dad gave me when I was three.’ The toy
is projected on to a massive screen as if it were being dissected. The actor
explains that his father was a Peronist activist who repaired old cars at a
workshop which during the 1970s was used to hide weapons. In 1976 his
father was kidnapped during a military raid. One of the few things that Mari-
ano was left was a reel-to-reel recorder: ‘The tape I like the most is the one
where he calls my name.’ Mariano’s son, Moreno, is also on stage. He is four
years old, just a little older than Mariano was when his father was taken. They
both operate the old recorder and the cheerful voice of a missing father floods
the hall: ‘Mariano, Marianooooo00000’. The voice resonates as clear and
lively as in the 1970s. It is a voice that sounds beyond artifice, almost naked.
On stage, three generations afe bonded together. An old tape allows the past
to touch the present. It works as the material reminder of what Benjamin
describes as a ‘secret agreement between past generations and the present
one’.'* Moreno plays around.
In her turn, Vanina Falco brings a picture on stage. It shows two children
embracing each other. She explains:
My brother is the one I love most in my family. We have always been
inseparable even though five years ago we found out we are not related by
blood. My brother is the son of murdered militants, and my father abducted
him because my mother couldn’t have children any more.
On stage, Vanina wears a blue suit like her father used to wear when he said he
was out ‘to sell medicines’. When Vanina was 21 she left home with a black
eye: her father had discovered that she was in love with another girl. Seven years
later she found out that her alleged brother was the son of an activist couple.
Her father, who was in fact an intelligence officer working for the military, had
abducted him from ESMA. ‘My whole life became fiction. My mother isn’t my
brother’s mother, my brother isn’t my brother, and my dad has many faces,’ says
Vanina on stage. Although I will return to Vanina’s case later, it is important to
recognise that not all the stories presented on stage are so dramatic. For instance,
Pablo Lugones is the twin son of a couple who had no political involvement.
His name carries the traces of a tragic family saga, which includes Leopoldo
Lugones, a brilliant poet and controversial essayist who committed suicide in
1938; his son Polo, a police commissioner and torturer; and Pirie, the grand-
daughter, a Montonera activist who ‘disappeared’ in 1978." Pablo’s branch,
12 Benjamin, ‘Theses on the Philosophy of History’, p. 246.
13 Socorro Estrada has reviewed the many publications that explore the Lugones’ tragic
lineage. See S. Estrada, ‘La maldici6n de los Lugones’, Revista N, Clarin, 30 October 2004, http://
edant.clarin.com/suplementos/cultura/2004/10/30/u-859136.htm [accessed 23 December 2011].

110 CECILIA SOSA
however, did not seem to retain any sign of that uncertain glory. During the
dictatorship, his father was an employee at a bank that was occupied by the
military. The most harmful episode he recalls is that his father was obliged to
shave off his long hippie beard. Pablo shows a pair of boots he inherited from
his grandfather. When he puts them on, he performs a striking ‘gaucho’ dance,
as if those boots could recover the majestic power hidden in his tragic lineage.
In his turn, Blas Arrese Igor brings a turtle on stage. He inherited it from his
father who used to be a priest and left the Church when he met her mother ‘to
populate the world’. At each performance, the turtle is required to run a ‘YES/
NO’ contest to determine whether or not there will be a revolution in the coun-
try. In this way, the prophetic turtle recreates the links among heritage, supersti-
tion and theatre. The turtle-game also suggests that the series of repetitions
performed on stage are ultimately unpredictable, perhaps as much as what the
new generation will do with their inherited legacies.
Although these personal testimonies are first presented separately, eventu-
ally the show will bond them together. In the new assemblage, the stories of
those who were not directly touched by violence have a crucial role to play:
they help to grasp how the resonances of trauma can be processed collectively,
beyond those usually considered as the ‘real victims’. Maybe this has been the
most spectacular discovery of Arias’s production.
Without ‘pedigree’
Lola Arias was born in 1976, the year of the military coup in Argentina. She has
no relatives who ‘disappeared’. To some extent, her work has run outside the
narratives of blood. This ‘illegitimate’ background attracted global media attention
as soon as the show began touring major festivals in Europe and Latin America.
During an interview conducted in Buenos Aires in 2010, Arias told me:
Most journalists kept on asking ‘why you?’, as if one must have a sort of
inherited right to talk about this issue, as if only the children of the victims
were allowed to talk. I may not have had any tragic story in my family,
but I am also touched because was born in 1976, and all my childhood was
marked by the dictatorship.'*
I could recognise myself in the director’s words. Indeed, that international reac-
tion did nothing but repeat the long-term local tradition which largely stipu-
14 ‘Los periodistas me preguntaban por qué vos?, como si uno tuviera que tener un derecho
heredado para hablar de esto, como si los unicos que tuvieran derecho a decir y a opinar fueran
los hijos de las victimas. Me toca porque naci en 1976; toda mi infancia esta marcada por eso’ (my
translation). My interview with Lola Arias took place on 10 January 2010 in Buenos Aires. Unless
otherwise indicated, the quotations in this chapter are taken from this interview.

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 11]
lated that only those related by blood to the missing have the right to engage
with the dictatorship’s legacies. Still, 1 was surprised to hear that Arias’s produc-
tion also raised suspicions among her colleagues:
When I started working on this show, the artists of my generation looked
down on me. They said that I was going to do something shit and that I was
going to repeat the same old discourse. But I wanted to tell different stories.
That is why there are not only children of the disappeared in the play.'°
Against predictions, Arias’s piece removed all conventional markers. The
show does not speak from the side of the victims, but from those who appar-
ently have not been affected by violence. Pervasive screens, live alteration of
images and vibrant solos corrapt previous forms of documentary theatre. For
the first time an ‘outsider’, a young woman with no pedigree — as H.I.J.O.S.
activists would say — comes forward to intervene in the battle of memory. From
this non-normative perspective, Mi vida después stands for a politics of mourn-
ing in which the experience of being affected is not limited to familial borders
but open to more expanded affiliations. In order to do so, it displays a fantastic
performance of costumes and blood; a generational machine which solicits a
differential engagement with technology and humour.
The death of the father
To see this generational machine in action, I would like to focus on a specific
scene. Carla Crespo recalls the death of her father, a guerrilla activist who reached
a high position within the Ejército Revolucionario del Pueblo (ERP). Dressed
as a female guerrilla, she says to the audience:
I’ve heard so many versions of how my dad died that it’s as if he died several
times, or as if he never died. If my dad’s life were a film, I’d like to play his
stunt double.
Empowered by her father’s costumes, Carla becomes the leader of a guerrilla
group. She uses a loudspeaker to instruct the rest of the actors who run, jump
and crawl around the stage, re-enacting the atmosphere of a clandestine training
camp during the 1970s. ‘Death number one,’ yells Carla: ‘When I’m six, my
mum told me my dad died in a car accident.’ Straight away, the team of actors
breaks file to make a structure from chairs; they turn on a radio and sit inside
15 ‘De entrada, cuando dije que queria trabajar sobre este tema, todos los artistas de mi gen-
eracion me dijeron que no iba a poder, que iba a hacer una mierda, que iba a repetir el mismo
viejo discurso de siempre. Pero yo queria contar otras historias, por eso no hay solo hijos de de-
saparecidos en la obra’ (my translation).

iL? CECILIA SOSA
chatting loudly, as if they were travelling in a fictional car. Seconds later, the
music stops and all heads drop down. ‘Death number two,’ shouts Carla:
When I’m 14, during a family gathering my grandma says right in front of
me that my dad died in 1975, at the Battle of Monte Chingolo, in a clash
between the ERP and the military.
As if it were a fantastic sequence from an action movie, the actors perform that
clash using their fingers as guns and falling down to the ground. Despite the
ostensibly childish proceeding, the remake depicts the spirit of sacrifice usu-
ally attributed to those armed groups, subtly exaggerated as if coming from a
bitter cloud of irony. The exchange of roles among the actors proposes mischie-
vous touches across time: the son of the priest kills the leftist activist whereas
the daughter of the policeman plays the part of the traitor. The pace of the se-
quence leaves little time for breath. ‘Death number three,’ continues Carla:
When I am 20, I read a letter that the party sent to my mum, saying that all
those wounded at Chingolo had been taken prisoner and shot three days later.
The militant performers walk together to the back of the stage and, as if sud-
denly hit by invisible bullets, they all fall down over the pile of clothes. Carla
looks to the audience and says that those who were killed during that episode
had their hands cut.off so they could be identified. However, the high tempera-
tures of the summer of 1975 made the hands decompose and the bodies could
not finally be recognised. “That is why my mother and J are still alive,’ says the
actor. The scene finishes with the crew sketching a chalk mass grave on the
floor, recreating the cemetery where Carla’s father may have been buried along-
side 50 other bodies with no hands. ‘Two years ago I did a DNA test to find out
whether my dad is buried there. | am still waiting for the results,’ she says. In
fact, when the piece was re-staged at a little venue in Buenos Aires in 2011,
these lines had to be amended. By that time Carla had received the DNA results
that confirmed that her father was in that common grave. On discovering the
variance in the script, | was shocked. It seemed to me that the piece had acquired
a new life: it had gained the capacity to change alongside the stories performed
on stage. In those terms, Mi vida después subverted the boundaries between
documentary and fiction, the private and the collective. In doing so, the collec-
tive dissection of the past managed to propose an experience of transmission
that contests familial narratives.
