Not only the family
The previous scene offers a fruitful case to explore further the mechanisms at
stake in the ‘post-memorial’ structure of transmission. In fact, Hirsch argues

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 113
that within descendants’ recollections the frontiers between truth and fiction
tend to blur. In this respect, it is clear that the way in which Arias’s production
engages with the parental past is not related to a regime of ‘truth’ but rather to
an affective and even fantastic ethics and aesthetics of remembrance, which
seems to confirm and even enhance the show’s post-memorial inscription.
Moreover, within the piece the fragmentary stories transmitted by the family
acquire the form of enigmatic fairy tales. Nonetheless, Hirsch also argues that
this mode of transmission can endorse a strategy through which the post-gen-
eration asserts ‘its own victimization alongside that of the parents’.'° By contrast,
I argue that Arias’s production challenges this assumption. It ultimately shows
how a generation manages to deal with overwhelming memories to move on
from family narratives. Performance appears here as the potential engagement
for producing an alternative ftfture.
Although Carla’s testimony initially draws upon the stories inherited from
her family, this bloodline framework is undermined on stage. At some point,
the actor playing her ghost-father on stage appears to be enacting a fantasy
emerging from the performance itself. Moreover, this is not simply an actor
giving an account of a painful episode but a whole team collaborating to build
her story, and being affected by its vibrations and resonances. The group is not
simply representing the actor’s ambivalence but exposing it in all its contradic-
tion and difference. Thus, the final performance does not assert ‘victimisation’
but rather shows how the idea of being affected can be staged beyond bloodline
ties. Nonetheless, if Arias’s production manages this, it is not through the single
force of the individual stories. Rather, it is because of the collective framework
on which the performance relies and within which it takes place.
While subverting the boundaries between documentary and fiction, the indi-
vidual and the collective, Carla’s sequence also contests the idea of testimony
as the expression of an inner and private self. If the relatives’ associations in
Argentina have tended to frame the idea of testimony as excruciating facts that
inform legal trials, Mi vida después proposes alternative dialogues among tes-
timony and performance. Arias’s production illuminates how first-person accounts
of traumatic events can differ from the mere exposure of sorrow or private ac-
counts of suffering. Rather, the possibility of ‘giving an account of one self’, to
use Butler’s expression, emerges as a ritualistic performance that can be rehearsed,
iterated and finally cross-dressed beyond bloodline inscriptions.'’ On stage,
personal testimonies emerge as a chorus of voices, costumes and flesh that evoke
an experience of communality. Thus, Mi vida después offers an alternative re-
sponse to the duty of memory, one that erupts and multiplies the possibilities of
the bodies on and off the stage.
16 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 108.
17 Butler, Giving an Account of Oneself.

114 CECILIA SOSA
A kiss from the past
To explore this ‘eruption’, | would like to focus on another scene; that of the remak-
ing of a kiss. The protagonist is Liza Casullo. She was almost born in a lift in
Mexico City in 1981. Her father, Nicolas Casullo, a recognised left-wing intel-
lectual, unexpectedly died during rehearsals for the play in October 2008. On stage,
Liza confesses that after her father’s death she thought of leaving the show, although
she finally stayed. She explains to the audience that her parents were forced into
exile before the military took power: the Argentine Anticommunist Alliance (AAA)
was pursuing them. While Liza speaks, two of the actors, Blas and Carla, play the
roles of her parents. The rest of the cast operates a camera and the audience can
follow the remade scene projected on to a big screen at the back of the stage. Liza
gives the instructions: ‘Close-up of my mum’s profile. In the background, my dad,
out of focus with a book in his hand.’ Blas says: ‘We have to leave the country.’
Carla: ‘Why?’ Blas: ‘Someone sent me a death threat.’ Liza instructs: ‘My mum
blinks.’ On the big screen, Carla blinks. Blas says: ‘Will you marry me?’ After an
edgy silence, Liza gives the final instruction: ‘My parents kiss for seven and a half
minutes.’ The actors meet in a slow motion kiss, which is extended, enlarged and
subtly faded, as if it were part of a cheesy television soap.
In interview, the actors in Buenos Aires all agreed they had fun performing
this scene, especially those in charge of the kiss. More than a ‘joke’, the sequence
helps to address further how the transmission of trauma can go beyond bloodline
narratives. Moreover, it shows how the experience of being undone by loss
might help to build alternative affiliations for the future. In fact, one of the most
striking features of Arias’s show is the collective way in which each story is
performed on stage. The actors not only enact their own lives and those of their
parents, but also those of the rest of the team. This operation is stressed in this
particular sequence when the actors have to exchange roles to recreate a mar-
riage proposal that took place in the 1970s. That seemingly blissful moment is
brought back through a pantomimic choreography, which seems to defy any
romantic atmosphere. History is written backwards. The actor who commands
the scene is the daughter of the bride and groom, not even born at the moment
oft he event. If emotions belong to an irretrievable instant always located in the
past, this twisted variant of affection comes to be satirically performed three
decades later. Arguably, the remade kiss stands as a bad copy. Nonetheless, the
transition that goes from one kiss to another reveals the playful and critical space
that the younger generation found to deal with their overwhelming stories.
Furthermore, these bodies touching each other, operating through each other,
eventually settle on a ‘politics of touch’ where the body on stage, as Erin Man-
ning says, “is always more than one’.'* This collective assemblage that underpins
18 Erin Manning, ‘The Collectivity of aL ife’, Body and Society, 16.1 (2010), 117-27 (p. 123).

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 115
the scene finally exceeds familial lines of transmission, bringing to the foreground
new generational desires. Furthermore, that mischievous kiss can also be con-
ceived of as a response to the sense of bereavement evoked by Liza’s father’s
death. It introduces a joyful moment that undermines the feeling of loss that
remains in the background. In this sense, it is a kiss that touches across times.
In so doing, the remade kiss laughs in the face of death and mocks the ongoing
familialism, staging the uncanny pleasures of being plural in grief.
Importantly, this affective structure of transmission not only involves the bodies
of the actors, but also and fundamentally the bodies of the audience. While laugh-
ing in the darkness in front of the overacted kiss, the audience acknowledges the
circulation of affects that emerges from the exchange of costumes and roles on
stage. This radiation circulates on and off while assessing the tricks of time and
space enhanced by the estrangement of theatre. As Nicholas Ridout suggests,
theatre can be conceived as a ‘vibratorium’, a threshold space were the affects
experienced on stage can also resonate beyond it, in a kind of radiation that circu-
lates back and forth from the audience.'? Drawing upon Ridout’s insight, I suggest
that the vibrations emanated from a kiss draw a form of intimacy that quivers in
the bodily encounter with the audience. In so doing, Arias’s production brings into
being a form of ‘fleeting community’.” In this sense, the performance of a kiss
allows both actors and public to share a non-normative experience of grief, one
that contests victimising narratives by presenting a new idea of being together
after loss. In this process, both loss and joy become available to the audience.
Don’t cry for me, Argentina
When Mi vida después was re-staged in Buenos Aries in 2011, Susana Villalba,
a respected poet and theatre critic, wrote a harsh review of the show. She accused
the piece of bearing a tone of ‘light comedy’ that lessened and ultimately trivi-
alised the reception of the traumatic past.*' She also queried the prevailing fa-
milial inscription of the stories, suggesting a lack of criticism of the parental
figures. The accusation incited a quarrel within the group in which two main
positions were finally delineated. While one of the actors whose father had been
assassinated by the military published an angry response to the journalist,
Arias argued that there was no need to respond since the piece ‘spoke for itself.”
19 Ridout, ‘Welcome to the Vibratorium’, p. 222.
20 Ridout, ‘Welcome to the Vibratorium’, p. 231.
21 Susana Villalba, ‘El discurso del vencedor’, Revista N, Clarin, 12 February 2011.
22 | had access to this internal discussion since my review of the show was published along-
side Villalba’s article. For this reason, I was included in the email exchange between the cast and
friends commenting on both. See Cecilia Sosa, ‘En el nombre del padre’, Revista N, Clarin, 12
February 2011, http://www.revistaenie.clarin.com/escenarios/teatro/nombre-padre-hi-
jo 0 428357361.html [accessed 23 December 2011].

116 CECILIA SOSA
To my eyes, the internal discussion was convoluted and even disheartening: the
hierarchies of blood that I was trying to contest through my project seemed to
be confirmed. Once again, the ones who felt they had the ‘right’ to respond were
those usually considered the ‘true’ victims of violence. Although the tensions
remained, the episode was fortunately sorted within the group.
Nonetheless, drawing upon this episode, I would like to consider in what
expanded terms Arias’s performance ‘spoke for itself’, to use the director’s
expression. In so doing, I also attempt to offer a belated response to Villalba’s
review of the piece. My argument here is not related to the script or the kind
of testimonies delivered by the actors in relation to their parents. Rather, I
would like to draw attention to a certain constitution of the bodies on stage
that remains in excess of the practices of the speaking subject. In order to do
so, it is productive to consider the ‘non-verbal, non-conscious dimensions of
experience’ that Arias’s performance brings into play. These dimensions have
been the focus of attention of scholars working on the expanding field of af-
fect, since they help them to re-engage with questions of memory, attention
and perception, as Lisa Blackman and Couze Venn comment in a recent dis-
cussion on the future of affect studies published in Body and Society.” Draw-
ing on this, I suggest that, rather than a universe of uniform stories, what
Arias’s show brings to the forefront is an ‘experiential choreography’, a form
of co-enactment and co-evolution of the bodies on stage, to use Blackman and
Venn’s expressions. Moreover, | argue that this collective assemblage is
mostly attached to a particular use of technology and a certain appeal to humour
that Arias’s show foregrounds. Both elements play a distinctive role in this
production, reinforcing each other and crafting a generational platform to
recall the traumatic past. Despite the harsh and even disturbing content of
most of the testimonies, Arias’s show never loses its singular playful mood.
This affective tone clearly differs from the solemn productions that deal with
similar issues, mainly those staged by Teatro por la identidad.** As the direc-
tor acknowledges, this was in fact her aim from start:
I didn’t want to make a play of people crying. It would have been demagogic
and I didn’t want a melancholic play at all. I didn’t want to repeat what was
already there within the human rights associations. For me, it was important
that the play could have irony and the humour of our generation.”°
23" Lisa Blackman and Couze Venn, ‘Affect’, Body and Society, 16.1 (2010), 7-28 (p. 8).
24 Blackman and Venn, ‘Affect’, p. 10.
25 In particular, I refer to the kind of productions staged by Teatro por la identidad (Theatre
for Identity). As mentioned in the Introduction, since 2001 this group of theatre-makers has stood
as the ‘artistic branch’ of the Abuelas of the Plaza de Mayo, bringing together actors, directors and
choreographers.
26 ‘No queria hacer una obra de gente llorando. Hubiera sido demagégico y yo no queria una
obra melancolica. No queria repetir lo que ya existe en las organizaciones de derechos humanos.

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY Ny
As | learned later, at the explicit instruction of the director, the actors were not
allowed to cry on stage. Throughout the show, passionate musical tandems,
hectic fights over clothes or exuberant dances tend to replace tears. This is
particularly clear when, finishing the reading of the last letter that her father sent
to her mother, Carla says: ‘My dad died four months before I was born. He was
26. When I turned 27, I thought: “Now I’m older than my dad.” The uncanny
disturbance of lifetime narratives that permeates the sequence — and, by exten-
sion, Argentina’s aftermath of violence — is stressed by a furious drum solo that
the actor plays at the front of the stage.”” As Carla commented during our inter-
view, the scene did not become easier with repetition:
I can say without difficulty that my father’s hands were cut off but when
I have to say that he died“at 26, I always feel my voice breaking. Mi vida
después has been different from all my previous work. I have to push my
feelings down instead of evoking them.”®
Arias’s production transforms this disruption of biological narratives into
its key atmosphere. The time machine of costumes enhances this dislocation
of reproductive accounts of kinship. Moreover, it reminds the audience that
time does not necessarily move ‘steadily forward’, but rather can experience
unpredictable enfolding and unfolding, as Carolyn Dinshaw states in a discus-
sion on queer temporality.’ In these terms, the same forceful instruction that
prevented the actors from making visible their emotions on stage finally cre-
ated a hushed sense of the collective, which defined the show at a deeper
level. This contested combination of affects managed to create a certain attun-
ing of the bodies on stage.
Moreover, this singular affective tonality foregrounded by Arias’s production
emerges entwined within certain operations of technology, which also craft a
generational platform to invest and unsettle the past. The recurrent use of cam-
eras, screens and live alteration of images work as main scenic resources that
corrupt traditional forms of documentary theatre. These devices create an au-
tonomous sense of time that, far from a frivolous game, works as a form of
memory prosthetic through which the younger generation reclaim an active role
Para mi era muy importante que la obra pudiera tener la ironia y el humor de nuestra generacién’
(my translation).
27 A similarly dislocated temporality was detected in the Madres’ claim that ‘Our children
gave birth to us’ and also Quieto’s photographic series, which proposes an uncanny encounter
between forever young parents and mature offspring.
28 ‘Puedo decir sin problemas que a mi papa le cortaron las manos, pero cuando tengo que
decir que murié a los 26 siempre siento que se me quiebra la voz. Esta experiencia fue totalmente
distinta a mis trabajos anteeriores. Tengo que contener toda emoci6n en lugar de evocarla’ (my
translation). i
29 Dinshaw, Edelman and Ferguson, ‘Theorizing Queer Temporalities’, p. 185.

118 CECILIA SOSA
in the production of their own selves and others. The particular use of technol-
ogy also sheds light on the material conditions of existence that belong exclu-
sively to this generation of actors, which were not thinkable during their parents’
youth. This entanglement of humour and technology finally staged the conditions
of possibility of the performance itself. Even as each sequence focuses on one
actor’s personal story, the audience can follow the entire troupe manipulating
pictures, borrowing bodies to enact others’ memories, and ultimately blurring
the boundaries between one and another. Thereby, the audience witnesses not
singular stories, but a collective assemblage that resonates through the actors in
an affective experience of body-to-body transmission. This collective machine
of affects underpins the show and suggests a novel exchange between indi-
vidual loss and public trauma. This collective assemblage of bodies exceeds
familial transmission, bringing to the fore new generational desires. In this
‘non-verbal’ sense, the piece also ‘speaks for itself’.
I do not wish to suggest that this generational platform to invest the past was
a ‘natural’ response on the part of the descendants. Rather, it was the ‘artifice’
that Arias’s production strove to create, which finally went beyond intentions.
The show brought to the stage a form of sociality that can be read in tune with
Patricia Clough’s argument in the issue of Body and Society devoted to affect
studies. In the afterword to this issue, Clough contends that the very idea of
‘sociality’ should be thought of as ‘a matter of affective transmission across
bodies in a mechanical assemblage with technology and technical arrangements’.*°
Drawing on this, I suggest that Arias’s production had the rare ability to highlight
the intimate connection among humour, subjectivity and technology which
permeates Argentina’s post-dictatorship. In so doing, it managed to rethink the
relationship ofa ffect and trauma by deploying a time machine of memory, which
brought to light a creative response to injury coming from the future. To this
generational platform Villalba’s review finally reacted.
Pictures into flesh
I would like to address a slight transgression that has been operating within
my analysis, one that can be productive for reassessing the concept of post-
memory in the terms in which Hirsch first formulated it. Although Hirsch
concedes that the structure of post-memory helps to explain in what ways less
affected participants become engaged with trauma, she also warns that this
affective form of transmission risks falling back on the familiar.*! Still, Hirsch
has become increasingly interested in thinking of other modes of transmission
that go beyond the family. In her latest work, post-memory becomes a critical
30 Patricia Clough, ‘Afterword: The Future of Affect Studies’, Body and Society, 16.1 (2010),
222-30 (p. 225).
31 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 108.

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 119
tool to explore embodied forms of transmission of trauma and living experi-
ence that exceed bloodline ties and become available to other contemporaries.
This is what she calls ‘affiliative postmemory’. In my own project, I have
been interested in forms of transmission that can move away from the bio-
logical normativity that has constrained the politics of memory in contempo-
rary Argentina. In this section, | would like to explore whether Hirsch’s early
dependence on photography as a medium oft ransmission might help to explain
why she ultimately attaches post-memory to the tropes of the family. These
critical reassessments on post-memory could be useful to address the particu-
lar fascination for the visual that has been part of the memory struggles in the
country, where pictures have been privileged sources in the construction of
the official archive of memory.
In her well-known essay ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, Hirsch argues
that the regime of photography works as ‘a uniquely powerful medium for the
transmission of events that remain unimaginable’.** For her, photography not
only allows the viewer to see but also to touch the past. She writes that family
photos tend to become ‘screens’ that resemble ‘spectres reanimating their dead
subjects with indexical and iconic force’.* It is important to bear in mind that
Hirsch’s concept of post-memory draws on Roland Barthes’s famous concept
of the ‘punctum’, whose first example develops the trope of maternal loss. In
fact, the fantastic line of light that suddenly links the viewer to an image begins
for Barthes in front of a picture of a woman wearing a necklace that reminds
him of the one that his mother used to wear.*> Moreover, in Camera Lucida,
Barthes describes the photographic punctum as a projected ‘umbilical cord’.*°
I suggest that this bloodline inscription remains elusively present in Hirsch’s
formulation of post-memory. Although it might be expanded and projected
among less implicated viewers, it is always ready to ‘fall back’ to the familiar,
as the scholar herself recognises.
By contrast, my reading of Arias’s production explores what happens when
photography loses its autonomy as genre and becomes part of the embodied
face-to-face encounter proposed by theatre. I argue that in this shared space and
time, performance can become a productive medium for the circulation of trau-
matic memories that, unlike photography, can contribute to breaking with fa-
milial languages. In this context, Mi vida después offers an exceptional counter-
case to challenge the familial foundation of the notion of post-memory, and
32 See Marianne Hirsch, The Generation of Postmemory. Writing and Visual Culture after
the Holocaust (New York: Columbia University Press, 2012), p. 36.
33 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 108.
34 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 116.
35 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 123.
WwW6 Roland Barthes, Camera Lucida: Reflections on Photography, trans. Richard Howard (New
York: Hill and Wang, 1981), p. 80.

120 CECILIA SOSA
assess the limits of the concept, at least in its first formulations. The case seems
to be particularly appropriate since Arias’s production also set out from pictures,
the main medium of Hirsch’s formulations. This is how the director acknowl-
edged this fact:
The show started by looking at family pictures together and thinking of
the enigmas that everyone has about one’s own childhood. These enigmas
become stuck to these images.*”
After that initial moment of exploration, pictures are put into flesh on stage.
The performers draw upon these pictures to create a novel assemblage in which
childhood images become contrasted, subverted and interrogated in their mo-
ments of awkwardness. The audience can follow, for instance, the actors
scratching the faces of Vanina’s relatives on a gigantic screen, adding artificial
moustaches to signal their credentials as policemen. A red arrow also disturbs
the picture of Liza’s mother, eight months pregnant, to denounce a pack of
cigarettes lying next to her while she sunbathes at the edge of a swimming
pool. These seemingly minor interventions shed light on critical dissections of
family albums on the part of the descendants. Projected on to screens, the im-
ages become part of an expanded theatrical forum. In so doing, Mi vida después
undermines photography as a pre-established autonomous genre. The episodes
that finally reach the stage seem to emerge from an abject wardrobe, as if the
unconscious of photography were forced to reveal the long-term secrets and
oscillating textures silently contained within childhood images. Thus, those
pictures are infused with urgent and contemporary demands which entangle
multiple temporalities on stage.
As many scholars have noticed, since the Madres began to use pictures of
their missing children, the relatives’ associations but also diverse activists and
artists have followed this tradition.** As addressed in Chapter 2, Diana Taylor
frames this visual presentation of the victims as a form of ‘DNA of performance’,
a public paradigm in which the scientific and the performative become knotted.”
Family albums and ID images are part of the official repertoire of memory. Even
independent artists remained faithful to this tradition. To some extent, loss and
37 ‘El proceso de armado del show comenz6 mirando todas esas fotografias juntos y reflex-
ionando sobre los enigmas que cada uno tiene en relacion a su infancia. Muchos de esos enigmas
estan pegado a esas imagenes’ (my translation).
38 See Ana Longoni, ‘Fotos y siluetas: dos estrategias en la representacion de los desapare-
cidos’, in Los desaparecidos en la Argentina. Memorias, representaciones e ideas (1983-2008),
ed. Emilio Crenzel (Buenos Aires: Biblos, 2010), pp. 43-65. An English version translated by
Yaiza Herandez can be found in Afterall (Autumn/Winter 2010), http://www.afterall.org/journal/
issue.25/photographs-and-silhouettes-visual-politics-in-the-human-rights-movement-of-argentina
(pp. 1-7) [accessed 10 August 2011].
39 Taylor, The Archive and the Repertoire, p. 175.

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 121
lineage appeared to be tied in Argentina’s post-dictatorship by a similar ‘um-
bilical cord’ to those that haunt Barthes’s punctum and Hirsch’s considerations
of post-memory. Within the last few years, however, this bloodline tradition has
begun to be contested. My project addresses this transition. Moreover, I suggest
that Mi vida después brings this normative repertoire on stage to propose a col-
lective dissection of its authority. While proposing a contamination among
genres, family albums are dethroned as ‘unique’ repertoires of loss. On stage,
pictures have to share the power of evidence with fictional sections that include
dreams or fantasies in which the actors imagine their own deaths. In this sense,
Arias’s show queers the material evidence to provide a singular response to
trauma. The DNA of performance is countersigned on stage.
I suggest that this operation proposes a critical framework to engage with
post-memorial meditations. While putting the pictures into flesh, Mi vida después
opens the structure of transmission beyond the borders of the family. In this
displaced environment, the ‘punctum’ softens its bloodline foundation to provide
sharp piercing on both sides of the stage. Thus, the pictures that were once part
of innocent childhood albums can also experience ominous turns. I shall provide
anew example to illustrate this.
The baby-bath
Vanina shows a picture that is projected on to a big screen. It was taken in 1978,
at the height of the dictatorship. It shows a smiling middle-aged woman giving
a bath to a newborn baby in the living room of an ordinary home. Standing next
to the woman, a girl stares at the scene. For some reason she looks like a spy,
as if she were witnessing a spectacle in which she had no part to play. While the
audience examines the picture, Vanina explains:
This is me when I was three, watching my mum bathe my brother. In the photo
you can see that I’m happy but confused, I don’t quite understand where my
brother came from, because I don’t remember seeing my mum pregnant.
More than three decades later, the ostensibly innocent picture comes back on
stage. Like a fleeting Benjaminian recollection, the scene of the baby-bath
flashes back to reveal the secrets that were not known at the moment that it
was taken. Now that Vanina knows that this baby is not her biological broth-
er, the poignancy of the image is highlighted. It shows how the resonances of
military violence permeated everyday lives, while linking the intimate with
the public and proposing a backwards connection with the net of complicity
which pervaded Falco’s home. The baby-bath picture also reconfigures the
actor’s relationship to her own childhood, when she used to be “dad’s favour-
ite’. It becomes the touchstone of a previous life that finished at the end of
2003, when her alleged brother ran the DNA test that confirmed that he was

122 CECILIA SOSA
the son of an activist couple. The baby in the picture now bears his original
name: Juan Cabandié. He is the same young man who stood next to President
Kirchner during ESMA’s civil recovery in 2004: ‘I am my birth parents,’
Cabandié proclaimed that evening.
The scene reveals the extent to which a single photo can be conceived as
a document in which evidence is at the same time hidden and revealed. It
shows, as Benjamin would say, the extent to which ‘nothing is lost for history’.*°
The picture of the baby-bath carries an image of the past that is still looking
for redemption. This demand is iterated each time that Vanina brings it back
on stage, as if the pervasive ‘here and now’ oft heatre could work as a progres-
sive chain of judgement days. In this respect, Arias’s performance offers a
fascinating example of the relation of theatre to the world. In fact, the show
eventually helped to change the story. When Cabandié discovered that his
identity had been falsified, he took his abductor to court. Despite previous
legislation preventing descendants from testifying against their parents, a judge
considered that Vanina’s part in Mi vida después was enough of a precedent.
In December 2009 she testified against her father.*' Following her testimony,
on 18 May 2011 Luis Falco was sentenced to 18 years’ imprisonment, the
harshest punishment that had ever been given to an appropriator.* Ironically,
the intervention of the three-year-old girl in the picture was instrumental in
Falco’s imprisonment. Thus, Arias’s production not only brought real lives on
stage, it also contributed to the forging of an alternative future. The case stands
as a Striking example of the ways in which performance can influence the law
or promote social and political change.
From first-hand witnesses to ‘accidental tourists’
Drawing upon the same picture of the baby-bath, I would like to consider a
heated discussion that underpins current debates in Argentina, involving both
scholars and activists. It mainly concerns whether Hirsch’s meditations on post-
memory and second generations can be translated from the Holocaust’s descend-
ants to the aftermath of mass violence in contemporary Argentina, and even
more broadly. This problem becomes particularly pressing since some local
descendants resist being considered as ‘second generations’, claiming to qual-
ify as victims themselves. In fact, a recent initiative is attempting to incorporate
40 Benjamin, ‘Theses on the Philosophy of History’, p. 246.
41 This judicial decision, which was taken on 23 December 2009, was not only crucial for
Falco’s case but also enabled other abducted children to present cases against their parents. See
http://www.paginal2.com.ar/diario/suplementos/soy/1-1283-2010-03-21.html [accessed 10
August 2011].
42 Victoria Ginzberg, ‘Con la condena mas alta para un apropiador’, Pagina 12, 18 May 2011,
http://www. pagina 2.com.ar/diario/elpais/1-168409-2011-05-18.html [accessed 11 October 2011].

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY 123
the category of nifio desaparecido-detenido (‘disappeared-arrested child’)
within the current legal framework.* As the image of three-year-old Vanina
reveals, some of those who are now in their thirties were actual witnesses of
those traumatic events. Although their recollections might be fragmentary, many
of these young adults were present at the moment of their parents’ kidnapping,
were kidnapped with them or were born in captivity at clandestine detention
centres. In all these cases, the categories of ‘second generation’ or ‘second wit-
nesses’ do not seem to be completely accurate.
Even so, I contend that the concept of post-memory remains politically rel-
evant to address the memory debates that are currently taking place in Argen-
tina. This not only refers to the ‘imaginative investment’ and the creative ways
in which the younger generation have managed to present their visions of the
past. Rather, as Jens Andermann suggests, post-memory becomes especially
productive when exploring the ‘witnessing and empathic adoption of the trauma
of the first generation by subsequent ones’.** In the Argentine context, this per-
spective can make vital contributions to an understanding of how the experience
of trauma can be adopted in the context of spectatorship, and made available to
others, while remaining critical of familial narratives. This process not only
concerns first-hand witnesses, but any accidental spectator who engages, for
instance, with the uncanny vibrations of an old picture that takes over the stage.
In those occasions, the affect constituted on the basis of performance can frame
collective emotions and subjectivities. The fleeting form of intimacy construct-
ed between actors and spectators shows how post-memory can also work as an
intra-generational mechanism for the adoption of trauma, once the ‘real’ of
performance emerges. Certainly, the ‘real’ refers here less to the ‘true stories’
presented by the actors than to the collective event created by the performance
itself. Spectators may not have been directly affected by violence, but they can
still adopt those stories and fill them with their own personal experiences. In
those cases, both inter- and intra-generational forms of transmission become
stressed as a form of commonality, which contributes to the ongoing process of
‘working through’ the experience of mourning.”
Through others’ eyes
To test the ways in which theatre can work as the unexpected space for the
adoption of trauma in the present, I would like to refer to an episode that took
43 The leader of this initiative is Angela Urondo, a writer and one of the main referent
within the descendants. See Victoria Ginzberg, ‘Por primera vez el estado me esta devolviendo
algo’, Pagina 12,9 October 2011, http://w|w2 .wco.m.apr/adiagriio/nelpaai s/1-178549-2011-10-09.
html [accessed 12 October 2011].
44 Andermann, ‘Returning to the Site of Horror’, p. 81.
45 This is also the path that Hirsch’s latest work has taken.

124 CECILIA SOSA
place when a survivor from a detention centre attended a performance of Mi
vida después. One evening, the director and crew discovered that a man who
had lost his wife and a son during the military regime was seated in the audience.
This is how Arias recalled that night:
I was in a panic. I was afraid that the man would feel that the piece was
irreverent and could get hurt. After the show, he told me that, for the first
time, he was able to laugh about what had happened to him. ‘I could laugh
and I could see the story through your eyes,’ he said. This was the best thing
he could ever have said to me.*°
The episode offers an incidental case study to examine how a direct victim could
fruitfully engage with the particular view of a director who does not have a
direct connection with the missing. It addresses a mysterious encounter that took
place at that threshold area between the stage and the audience, where roles can
be mischievously reversed.*” Drawing upon the survivor’s statement, I suggest
that Arias’s production offered him the opportunity to see, live and feel through
‘others’ eyes’. By this I do not mean that the show, or any theatrical performance,
can assist in the process of individual healing. I only contend that this case re-
veals how theatre may expand the capacity to see and fee/ from the perspective
of others. In this sense, Mi vida después managed to bridge traditional barriers
between those usually considered as victims and those who apparently were
not. To address this point further, |w ould like to draw attention to Joe Kelleher’s
exploration of theatre and politics, where he draws from Bertolt Brecht’s
‘method’ to argue that the very condition of theatre, of ‘any theatre at all’, is to
become ‘estranged’.** Kelleher writes:
[theatre] is a place too, we tell our friend, where every action that is
performed appears planned out or scripted in advance, at least to an extent.
This produces a strange effect, we say, in that the people who are coming
and going across the space — let’s call them the actors — seem to have all
the freedom in the world to do whatever they like, even the freedom not
to do anything at all. But at the same time they seem constrained, as if all
their choices are somehow being made for them somewhere else, and as
if every move they make is basically a renewed attempt to deal with this
peculiar situation.”
46 ‘Esa noche estaba en panico. Tenia miedo que pudiera sentir que la obra era irreverente y
que pudiera salir herido. Cuando termino la funcion, vino a verme y me dijo que por primera vez
en su vida se habia podido reir de que le habia pasado. “Me pude reir y ver la historia a través de
tus ojos”, dijo. Fue lo mejor que pudo haberme dicho’ (my translation).
47 Jacques Ranciére, The Emancipated Spectator (London: Verso, 2009), p. 13.
48 Joe Kelleher, Theatre and Politics (Basingstoke: Palgrave, 2009), p. 61.
49 Kelleher, Theatre and Politics, pp. 61-62.

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY Ves
Mi vida después stresses the ‘estrangement’ inevitably involved in theatre.
It does so partly through the incorporation of distancing techniques in which
contemporary times appear ‘as though they were historical’, as Brecht would
contend, therefore acting as constant reminders of the artificiality involved
in performance. Yet, in this case, the V-effect becomes even more compelling.
This is mainly because the people we see ‘coming and going across the space’,
in Kelleher’s words, do nothing but act their own lives: they are actors acting
themselves. This does not mean that feelings of constraint disappear. Rather,
the performers still need to adjust to a script that has been written somewhere
else. They might have been selected because of the common condition of
being professional actors, but they are still subject to rules, the rules of re-
making their own lives and also the lives of others. They have been dressed
and scripted, they have been fold to act as little as possible. They have been
instructed to perform their own selves in a neutral tone, as if there were no
emotions involved; they have been told not to cry. Keeping their names on
stage, the actors directly address an audience that bears witness in the dark-
ness. The artificiality of drama seems to have been removed. Apparently, the
only thing there ‘to be seen’ is true stories. It is precisely under these condi-
tions that the ‘estrangement’ of theatre becomes highlighted. This does not
simply occur on stage. By contrast, it constitutes the very mode of address
of the audience, and is also what gives theatre the potential to embody a liv-
ing community as the very means of political emancipation. Yet Ranciére
also complains about the fetish of ‘audience participation’ and ‘interactive’
performances, popular tropes within the so-called ‘Post-dramatic Theatre’,
which tend to blur boundaries, confuse roles or disrupt conventional arrange-
ments but where, nevertheless, ‘what the spectator must see is what the direc-
tor makes her see’. For Ranciére true emancipation of the spectator only
begins when the implicit hierarchies of what comes to be seen are shaken.
As he writes, ‘emancipation begins when we challenge the opposition between
viewing and acting [...], when we understand that viewing is also an action
that confirms or transforms this distribution of positions’.°*' That is why, for
Ranciére, the spectator also acts; she participates and refashions the perfor-
mance in her own way, as if completing a poem, or even dreaming in an-
other stage. This separation while ‘being together’ is ‘the unique intellectual
adventure that makes her similar to all the rest in as much as this adventure
is not like any other’.*
Still, this argument can be taken a step further. While considering the kind
of illusion or disillusion that takes place in front of ‘performers who might not
be acting at all’, Kelleher argues that the form of estrangement mobilised by
50 Ranciére, The Emancipated Spectator, p. 14 (Ranciére’s emphasis).
51 Ranciére, The Emancipated Spectator, p. 13.
52 Ranciére, The Emancipated Spectator, p. \7.

126 CECILIA SOSA
theatre should be conceived as ‘a way oft hinking about the possibility of a
politics — at least in theatre- of non-relation’.* In this sense, he contends that
theatre brings to the foreground the possibility of learning something about
ourselves ‘as for the first time’.®* With this framework in mind, I would like to
go back to the case oft he spectator who had lost his family during the dictator-
ship. As mentioned before, the survivor acknowledged having seen ‘through
others’ eyes’ during Arias’s performance. | shall suggest that he also learned
something about himself ‘as if for the first time’.
Arias confessed that she didn’t know in advance how the former captive
would react to the show and ultimately what he would ‘see’ was going on
before his eyes, on stage. That is why the director was so stressed. As revealed
later, what actually happened there was a form of knowledge that emerged at
the time of the performance, while the survivor was watching a younger gen-
eration remaking stunt episodes of their parents’ youths. It was not the trans-
mission oft he director’s knowledge to the spectator, but rather ‘the third thing
that is owned by no one’.® At that moment, the individual intelligence oft he
survivor as spectator made it possible for him to introduce an irreducible
distance from his own experience, to put it in Ranciere’s terms. That was when
the possibility of emancipation arose, and also the unique ‘adventure’ that
took place for him that night.
The survivor confessed later that he laughed during the show. In some pecu-
liar way, he was also entertained. Far from any triviality, Kelleher argues that
in theatre, to ‘entertain’ means to offer ‘hospitality to someone, to make them
your guests, to hold them in your welcome in the way you might hold an idea
in your mind’.** Indeed, Mi vida después provided this ex-captive with the pos-
sibility of engaging with his own trauma as if for ‘the first time’. He was ‘held’
and ‘welcomed’ in a different narrative of grief. This process was primarily
political since it also enabled him to see from the perspective of others, those
who were not directly affected by violence. While pushing further the connec-
tions between theatre and politics, Kelleher argues:
What would appear to be at stake for both politics and theatre are the
very activities of showing and saying through which some are made
visible who would otherwise have ‘no business’ being seen and others
get to speak who would not, in ‘normal’ circumstances, count as speaking
beings.*”
53 Kelleher, Theatre and Politics, p. 64 (Kelleher’s emphasis).
54 Kelleher, Theatre and Politics, p. 65.
55 Ranciére, The Emancipated Spectator, p. 15.
56 Kelleher, Theatre and Politics, p. 65.
57 Kelleher, Theatre and Politics, p. 68.

THE ATTIRE OF (POST-) MEMORY PA
In these terms, I wish to make the case that Arias’s piece is mainly dedicated
to entertaining strangers, those who have been traditionally excluded from
discourses of mourning and who in ‘normal circumstances’ would not become
involved or even count as ‘speaking beings’. For the first time, Atias’s produc-
tion plays the part of those who have not experienced the rights of remember-
ing in post-dictatorial Argentina. In this political context, the performance
develops a way of ‘showing and saying’ for those who seemingly have ‘no
business’ within the local experience ofl oss. Also in this sense, Arias’s piece
‘speaks for itself’, and it does so on behalf of those who seemingly do not
have the ‘right’ to speak, those who lack pedigree and, thereby, do not have
the most ‘interesting’ roles within the current discourses of memory. Thus, Mi
vida después shows how seemingly underestimated subjects of politics can
have surprising things to say, if they are allowed to step into ‘main’ roles. They
can then also ‘entertain’ others and be ‘entertained’ by others. They can hold
and host their audiences within non-normative discourses that intersect, twist
and displace conventional acts of mourning. In this movement, Arias’s produc-
tion appeared as a prominent political space where alternative social relations
could be created and re-enacted in unforeseen ways. In doing so, Mi vida
después provided a new hospitality, a non-biological narrative of grief that
could be shared and transmitted among those who had not been directly touched
by violence. Moreover, as the experience of the survivor witnessed, this pro-
cess of ‘estrangement’ could also offer a blissful surprise even to those usu-
ally considered to be direct victims.
Kelleher suggests that theatre’s job could be to imagine ‘other, paradoxi-
cal worlds’.** By displacing normative discourses governed by the ‘wounded
family’, Mi vida después also worked in that direction. It helped to consider
the productivity of post-memory as a form of adoption of trauma, one that is
not necessarily confinéd to second generations but is open to all kinds of
witnesses, waiting to be entertained while sitting together in the darkness.
This situation stresses the estrangement between politics and performance
out of theatre, and beyond theatre, which fosters opportunities for building
more inclusive political communities. Mi vida después not only made visible
how strangers could exchange roles, costumes and emotions in broader sce-
narios; it worked as a reminder that theatre will always be attached to the
possibility of building alternative worlds, which come to interrupt ‘what-
ever is taken for granted’.”’
58 Kelleher, Theatre and Politics, p. 72.
59 Kelleher, Theatre and Politics, p. 72.

128 CECILIA SOSA
Close
On 30 September 2011 Mi vida después had its last national performance at La
Carpinteria. The small venue located in Abasto, a newly gentrified district of
Buenos Aires, was crowded. After three years the show had become an uneasy
self-reflective object, which not only mutated alongside the actors involved in
it, but also created new emergences. On the night of the last performance the
atmosphere was hectic. Although most of the audience had already seen the
show, the collective intensity was reminiscent of a gig. By then Luis Falco,
Vanina’s father, had been convicted and imprisoned for 18 years. That night also
marked the debut of Ismael, Mariano’s second son. The cheerful presence of
the three-year-old child playing next to his brother further enhanced the pre-
cipitated feeling of time that circulated throughout the venue. Ismael’s figure
appeared before me as a fantastic ‘emergence’, even a self-creation of the show
itself. To some extent, that last performance was the end of an era, the final stage
of a journey in which the actors might have gained adult credentials.
60 In December 2011 Arias’s production was presented at the Theatre des Abbesses in Paris.

Kinship, Loss and Political Heritage:
Los topos and Kirchner’s Death
In this final chapter, I put forward an analysis of Los topos (‘The Moles’, 2008),
a novella by the Argentine author Félix Bruzzone, both of whose parents were
murdered during the dictatorship. Although the biography of the author seems
to continue the tradition that stipulates that only those who were ‘directly af-
fected’ by violence are entitled to the rights of remembering, I will make the case
that Los topos works as a counter-performance to the idea of the ‘wounded fam-
ily’ as the only victim of the military violence. More than this, Los topos provides
a queer, insurgent and ironic version of the performance of blood, one that sug-
gests a more fluid entanglement among kinship, loss and political heritage. My
proposal, then, is to read Bruzzone’s novella alongside the radicalisation of the
struggles around the past which have emerged in the country since Cristina
Fernandez de Kirchner took office in 2007. In this context, Bruzzone’s book can
be conceived as an enhanced response to the black humour that permeated
H.1.J.O.S.’ organisation during the mid-1990s, at the same time that it assesses
the contradictions and limitations of a politics of mourning when it becomes
officialised. Although Los topos was published in 2008, I ultimately suggest that
Bruzzone’s fiction not only prefigured the affective environment that surrounded
Néstor Kirchner’s death in October 2010, but also envisaged the expanded lan-
guage of kinship that is currently in play in contemporary Argentina.
Critical distance, a ‘killjoy’ obsession
Bruzzone’s parents were both activists in the guerrilla group Ljército Revolucion-
ario del Pueblo. After their kidnapping, Bruzzone grew up with his grandparents in
the suburbs of Buenos Aires. Although the author was never part of H.I.J.O.S., his
work has focused obsessively on the drama of the children of the disappeared. In
fact, during an interview that I conducted with him in 2009, he argued:
I was about to join H.I.J.O.S. but in the end I thought that I did not need to.
I am not interested in a vindictive perspective. Literature does not work like
that. Literature needs to address different kinds of problems.'
1 ‘Estuve a punto de entrar a H.I.J.O.S. pero al final pensé que no lo necesitaba. No me in-

130 CECILIA SOSA
Indeed, Bruzzone has cultivated a strict personal discipline to remain faithful
to this independent artistic perspective. He lives in a suburb of Buenos Aires
with his wife and son, and cleans swimming pools in a wealthy neighbourhood
for a living. On a daily basis, he follows this experience of personal detachment
through a blog entitled ‘The one who cleans swimming pools.’* In this way,
Bruzzone has managed to keep a ‘healthy’ distance from conventional debates
in the field of memory. Despite this apparent isolation, his recognition as a
writer has expanded internationally. In 2010 he was invited as a guest of honour
to attend the Frankfurt Book Fair where he received the Anna Seghers’ Award.
In 2010 his first book, 76 (2007), an autobiographical collection ofs hort stories,
and Los topos were translated into French and German.’
In the following pages, I will contend that the wild spirit that underscores
Bruzzone’s book endorses a surprising turn within the narratives of grief. Draw-
ing on Sara Ahmed’s critique of normative forms of happiness, Los topos can
be read as a killjoy narrative of the ‘happy scripts’ supported by the relatives’
associations, a provocative response that comes from its most deviant son.*
Moreover, the author could be conceived as a double agent within the narrative
oft he victims, in particular with regard to the black slang developed by H.1.J.O.S.
Broadly, Los topos sheds light on those guilty pleasures that remained mostly
confined to the select circle of the ‘orphans’. As the main character argues, ‘even
the purest organisations can be spied on from the inside’.* Drawing on this
ambiguous position, I propose to analyse the novella as a sharp and excruciating
caricature of the wretched characters of the ‘wounded family’.
Making fun of the ‘wounded family’
As with the rest of Bruzzone’s fictions, Los topos draws from the author’s per-
sonal experience. The central character has also lost both parents during the
repression and lives with his grandparents in the suburbs of Buenos Aires. He
does not seem to identify with the figure of the ‘son’ and follows with scepticism
his grandmother’s anxieties around his parents’ disappearance. The elder ma-
triarch, named ‘Lela’, is somehow convinced that her missing daughter gave
birth to a second child while she was held captive at ESMA. When her husband
teresa la perspectiva revindicativa. La literatura no funciona de esa manera. Necesita dar cuenta
de problemas diferentes’ (my translation). My interview with Félix Bruzzone took place on 21
April 2009 in Buenos Aires. Unless otherwise indicated, the quotations included in this chapter
come from this interview.
2 ‘El que limpia las piletas’. See http://www.barrefondo.blogspot.com/ [accessed 31 January
2011].
3 See http://www. fu-berlin.de/en/presse/fup/2010/fup_10_348/index.html [accessed 20
January 2011].
4 Ahmed, The Promise of Happiness, p. 59.
5 ‘Hasta las organizaciones mas puras pueden ser espiadas desde adentro’; Félix Bruzzone,
Los topos (Buenos Aires: Mondadori, 2008), p. 65 (my translation).

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE Neil
dies, Lela moves opposite the detention centre to undertake a daily search for
her supposed second grandchild. An odd sense of humour pervades this pre-
liminary scene. Lela’s character (her name reads as ‘silly’ in Spanish) seems to
be endorsing an irreverent recitation of Abuelas’ activism and the group’s search
for the babies stolen during the dictatorship. Considering that the novella seems
to take place during the late 1990s, H.I.J.O.S.’ ‘golden years’, Lela’s shouts in
front of ESMA could appear as a sort of eccentric and untimely performance.
By that time, the abducted children were already in their twenties and not
likely to be at the former detention centre, which in 2004 was declared a ‘space
of memory’ and is progressively becoming a ‘Human Rights City’.
Eventually, the protagonist starts dating Romina, a sweet young woman who
joins H.I.J.O.S. as a devoted gesture of commitment towards the narrator’s
traumatic past. The woman endlessly tries to talk him into following her in this
decision, contending that among other descendants he will find the companion-
ship he needs. Her insistence does not convince him. The protagonist has strong
feelings against the escraches, the celebrated form of activism developed by the
descendants during the 1990s:
The escraches, for instance, were for me a form of revenge, law taken into
one’s own hands, something that interests me very much but perhaps because
of cowardice, or idiocy, or intelligence, I’ve been unable to put it into practice.
Sometimes | thought of borrowing Lela’s car, selling it, buying a Falcon [the
type of car that was used by the military while conducting the kidnappings],
and gathering some friends to go out kidnapping military personnel.°
As analysed in Chapter 2, H.I.J.O.S.’ public interventions, targeting the military
repressors in their own locations, worked as a gesture of moral condemnation
in the absence of legal justice. Los topos introduces an ironic version of this
form of activism: the escraches are reduced to a mere impulse of revenge, which
is deliberately turned back on the perpetrators. The transgression is flagrant. The
operation counts as a rich example of ‘subversive iteration’, in the terms devel-
oped by Judith Butler who argues that ‘it is precisely the repetition of the play
that establishes as well the instability of the very category that it constitutes’.’
Bruzzone’s deviant recitation of the escraches is all the more poignant since it
is envisaged through the same means that the military forces adopted during the
dictatorship, the Falcon, the green car that remains the most obvious symbol of
state repression.
6 ‘Eso de los escraches, por ejemplo, eran para mi una forma de revancha 0 de justicia por
mano propia, algo muy de mi interés pero que por cobardia, 0 idiotez, 0 inteligencia, nunca concre-
taba. A veces hasta pensaba en pedirle a Lela los papeles del auto [...] venderlo, comprar un Falcon
y salir con mis amigos a secuestrar militares’ (Bruzzone, Los topos, p. 17, my translation).
7 Judith Butler, ‘Imitation and Gender Insubordination’, in /nside/Out, ed. Diana Fuss (New
York and London: Routledge, 1991), pp. 13-31 (p. 18).

132 CECILIA SOSA
This subversive operation can be also witnessed in the way in which the
narrator condemns his girlfriend’s decision to join H.1.J.0.S. Not only does
he argue that there were no real victims in Romina’s family, but also he con-
tends that they did not even know what ‘the issue of the disappeared was
about’.* In so doing, Los topos sardonically acknowledges the hierarchy of
blood championed by the descendants and addresses with cruel irony the
question of who has the right to remember in Argentina. The provocation goes
even further. Romina’s feelings of empathy with the descendants make the
protagonist reflect with scorn:
I don’t know how things are between her and her mother, but the first thing
that struck me was that this lady should not like to see her daughter becoming
an activist in an organisation for people without parents.’
The book is fully embedded in this malicious mood. As a cynical H.I.J.O.S.’
double agent, the protagonist highlights the apparent contradiction of being part
of the group without bearing the status of a victim. Certainly, the question of
whether or not to accept those with no victims in their families — the so-called
‘open population’ — resulted in the group splitting in 1999. Thus, Los topos
laughs at the idea of ‘pedigree’ that regulated the group’s internal hierarchies.
For instance, echoing and displacing one of the ongoing jokes of the group, the
narrator suggests that the right moment for joining H.I.J.O.S. is close to Moth-
ers’ Day, to provide an appropriate commemoration for the missing parent.!°
The recursive black humour that was once part of H.I.J.O.S. is transformed into
Los topos’ corrosive method, which Bruzzone satirically turns back on its
‘creators’. Thus, the novella not only provides a powerful critique of the de-
scendants’ rituals but also of any secure narrative championed by the relatives.
For instance, while referring to a young woman member of H.I.J.0.S. whose
aunt was kidnapped, the protagonist suggests: ‘It would have been great for her
to join Romina, so as they could both found SOBRINOS [Nephews and Nieces],
NUERAS [Daughters-in-Law].’'' By proposing new branches within the genea-
logical tree of victims, Los topos teases the relatives’ obsession with kinship
titles. In so doing, it disturbs the feelings of property and the fantasy of unity
that lies at the core of the family of relatives. Bruzzone’s fiction pokes fun at
this bloodline cast of survivors and portrays it as a caricaturised ‘self-help club’
8 Bruzzone, Los topos, p. 16 (my translation).
9 ‘No sé cémo estan las relaciones entre ella y su madre, pero lo primero que se me ocur-
ri6 fue que a la sefiora la militacia en H.1.J.0.S. no debia gustarle, que no tenia por qué pade-
cer que su hija militara en una organizacion de personas sin padres’ (Bruzzone, Los topos, p.
21, my translation).
10 Bruzzone, Los topos, p. 21.
!1_ “Hubiera sido bueno que se juntara con Romna y fundaran SOBRINOS, NUERAS, no sé’
(Bruzzone, Los topos, p. 18, my translation, capitals in the original).

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 133
of victims. Following on from this, Los topos proposes an expanded version of
the ‘wounded family’, which should, from now on, not only include mothers,
grandmothers, children and siblings of the disappeared, but also nieces and
nephews, and, why not, daughters- and mothers-in-law. In sum, an expanded
collection ofv icarious relatives.
Against official grieving
In December 2007 Cristina Fernandez de Kirchner took office as the new
president. Her administration witnessed a different stage in the revision of the
past, which included a flirtation with the revolutionary rhetoric of the 1970s. In
this context, many former members of H.I.J.O.S. were called to work for the
government bureaucracy. Mosfly hired as human rights experts, former activists
now in their mid-thirties were placed in the role of ‘double agents’: they became
public servants of a state that officialised the demands of memory and justice.'”
In the face of this ‘institutional’ time of mourning, Los topos shows its sar-
donic teeth. As if teasing the mundane contingencies of being part of the state,
the novella looks backwards to examine H.I.J.O.S.’ radical years. For instance,
the book picks up the discussion around the reparation funds offered by the
democratic governments from 1994. As I explored in Chapter 2, the use of this
controversial money has been a pervasive source of discomfort and jokes
within the organisation. Still, Bruzzone pushes these boundaries further. He
depicts his characters using the reparative money to pay for abortions, sex,
travel to exotic beaches, or to fund eccentric research such as the development
of cigarettes that can be smoked in the rain. Los topos also mocks the descend-
ants’ passionate vindication of their parental activism. In so doing, the book
manages to envisage the extent to which this belligerent spirit was relaunched
during Fernandez de Kirchner’s administration, mainly through La Campora,
the youngest and most enthusiastic branch of the official party, which gained a
new centrality during the period.
As a fine caricaturist of the group, Bruzzone not only teases H.I.J.0.S.’ secret
rituals but also magnifies and makes visible its moments of failure and contra-
diction. If humour enabled H.I.J.O.S. to reverse individual experiences of shame,
Los topos shows how humour also worked in the group to confirm the preroga-
tives of the victims. By contrast, Los topos calls the entire frame into question:
it offers a non-biological response to the descendants’ drama, which reverses
the legitimacy of blood. In the following sections, | would like to show how
this new sequence of the comical is enhanced by a queer turn.
12 This new team was strongly connected and was greeted by the media as the ‘under-35s’.
See Nicolas Llantos, ‘Los sub-35’, Pagina 12, 9 January 2011, http://www.pagina12.com.ar/diario/
elpais/1-160147-2011-01-09.html [accessed 23 December 2012].

134 CECILIA SOSA
In the name of the (queer) son
At an early stage in the plot, the relationship between the protagonist and
Romina comes to an end. The protagonist mourns his loss by looking for sex in
the red light district of Buenos Aires. There he meets Maira, a transvestite sex
worker.'? The encounter eventually leads to ‘the greatest and more beautiful
love’.'* This romantic twist in the story of the descendant addresses the figure
oft he transvestite, which, following the decriminalisation of sex work in 1998,
was at the centre of Buenos Aires’ anxieties. The cross-dressing sex parades in
the red light district, first located in Palermo, a trendy area of the city, made
visible as never before forms of non-normative sexuality.'° In a context in which
the very definition of citizenry was at stake, Los topos plays its most defiant
card: it suggests that Maira, the protagonist’s new girlfriend, could also be his
biological sibling. As uncanny as it sounds, the narrator suggests that his brand-
new sexual partner could also be his missing brother who was stolen from his
mother after she gave birth at ESMA. Indeed, Maira might be the latchkey kid
that granny Lela was looking for from the beginning.
When | interviewed Bruzzone in April 2009, I was curious about the inclusion
of Maira in his novella. This character fascinated me, not only because she was
the first queer figure to appear in the author’s work, but also, and more impor-
tantly, because to my knowledge it was the first time that a queer reference was
explicitly applied to address the dictatorship’s heritage.
In the mind of my main character there is this situation about his potential
brother who might have been born at ESMA. Maira appears like a fantasy at
the time he is breaking up with his girlfriend. | think he becomes attracted to
Maira because he sees in these girls a problem of identity similar to the one he
has. He sees in the transvestite world almost a cliché of people with identity
problems. This is the sort of identification that the main character establishes.
When he engages with Maira in a more affective way his own problems and
suspicions are fostered, and eventually he becomes convinced that she could
13 As Leticia Sabsay notes, in the Argentine context, ‘transvestite’ refers either to transsexu-
al or transgender people — passing from male to female. Although within the local culture the
expression works as a pejorative term, it has also been appropriated by trans communities. The
term ‘transgender’ begins to be incorporated in Argentina from 2000. See Leticia Sabsay, ‘The
Limits of Democracy. Transgender Sex Work and Citizenship’, Cultural Studies, 25.2 (2011),
213-29 (n. 4).
14 “BI mas grande y hermoso amor’ (Bruzzone, Los topos, p. 34, my translation).
'5 In 2004, following furious responses from the neighbours, a series of judicial reformulations
confined the sex workers’ activity to another red light district located this time in the main green
urban space of the city. As Leticia Sabsay argues, ‘the regulation of sexual desire implied several
consequences for the definition of the public space itself and, at the same time, it implied the sta-
bilisation of the parameters through which citizenry and its rights to use urban space could be de-
termined’. For further discussion on this issue, see Sabsay, ‘The Limits of Democracy’, p. 215.

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 135
be his brother. However, I never thought of this story as a way oft alking about
tranvestites. Rather, I thought of it as a means to talk about the disappeared.' °
In the context of my project, Bruzzone’s words were revealing. The queer turn
that underpinned his story was related less to a sexual role, or even to a gender
disposition, than to a methodological turn deployed to subvert a problem. It was
not that Maira’s character had no value in and of herself, but rather that the
value of queerness worked as the very strategy to challenge the legacy left by
the dictatorship. To some extent, | empathise with the author’s position: my own
critical framework draws from this argument. In this vein, I suggest that the
figure of Maira can offer a new perspective to rethink a politics of mourning,
especially since the Kirchnerist administrations have appropriated the position
of the victims as their own vicarious political lineage. Her character can be
perceived as a subversive recitation of the figure of the son, a novel operation
to unseat the trajectory of the offspring of the disappeared.
I shall develop this argument carefully. On the one hand, by proposing a love
story between a transvestite sex worker and a son of the disappeared, Bruzzone’s
novella suggests an exploration of the contested subjectivities constituted in re-
sponse to the dictatorship’s violence in tune with the trans communities recently
revealed as part of the city. Maira’s character brings together the anxieties that
surround the figure of the transsexual and the opacity of the figure of the ab-
ducted son. They are both injured and repudiated characters. They have both been
victimised and recently they have also been politically vindicated. Moreover,
Bruzzone’s novella suggests that both figures could be biological siblings. While
inviting us to read the descendants’ drama alongside non-normative sexual filia-
tions, Los topos also touches on the problem of falsified identities. The drama of
growing up under fake names and sharing home-spaces with their appropriators
(who are, further, usually accomplices in their parents’ murder) already installs a
queer notion of kinship within the offspring of the missing. Maira’s character
reframes the traces of shame that inform the descendants’ biographies through the
feelings of queer unhappiness enacted by the figure of the transsexual worker. In,
this vein, Los topos reads the conflict of contested heritages while envisioning
alternative communities within the realm of kinship. Thus, it offers a unique op-
portunity to examine how the biological normativity supported by human rights
narratives failed to address the extended affective anxieties raised by grief.
16 ‘En la cabeza de mi personaje esta la situacion del supuesto hermano que podria haber
nacido en ESMA. Maira aparece como una fantasia al estar rompiendo con su novia. Creo que se
siente atraido por ella porque ve en estas chicas un problema de identidad como tiene él. Encuen-
tra en el mundo travesti un cliché de los problemas de identidad. Ese es el tipo de identificacién
que establece. Cuando desarrolla un vinculo mas afectivo termina creyendo que es su hermano.
Pero nunca pensé esta historia como algo para hablar de travestismos si no para hablar de desa-
parecidos’ (my translation).

136 CECILIA SOSA
On the other hand, Maira’s figure also offers a fruitful perspective to dis-
mantle the ongoing image of the descendants, so often asphyxiated beneath
the duty of memory during the Kirchnerist period. When mourning in Argen-
tina appears to be institutionalised, Los topos shows how the effects of trauma
cannot be reduced to trials. As already explored, from 2006 those responsible
for human rights violations were brought back to court. In these trials, H.I.J.0.S.,
for the first time, took the role of prosecutor. Nevertheless, compared with the
effervescence of the 1990s, the number of activists decreased by 75 per cent
in 2010. Somehow, Maira’s figure seems to be grieving H.I.J.O.S.’ golden
years. Her character brings to light an alternative constellation of desires. She
emerges as an impertinent response to the attempts to sanctify the figure of
the descendants. When former members ‘transitioned’ into public servants, or
became enthusiastic Kirchnerist supporters, the novella guffaws with delight
and mocks the moral arrangements prescribed by the so-called most progres-
sive human rights politics since the return of democracy. By reinstalling the
tension in relation to the heritage of the past, Bruzzone brings to light an-
other conception of politics.
Queering the repertoire
To ground this argument further, I would like to refer to a thought-provoking
comparison provided by Bruzzone while referring to his previous book, 76, a
collection of stories which also focuses on the descendants’ biographies and
draws from 1976, the year of his birth.
In my stories, the children of the disappeared have these abandoned buses
in their heads, which used to work but no longer do. They are looking for
something that is prior to any claim. They seek an origin, an identity, they
need to know who their parents were, but that prevents them from building
new families.'”
As the passage shows, Bruzzone reads the imaginary of the descendants as in-
trinsically melancholic, incapable of getting over their loss. The image oft hose
sinister buses that the author used to stare at in a depot near his neighbourhood
seems to be echoing the missing parents. From his comparison the ‘disappeared’
emerge as if they were pervasively kept in a sort of vegetative state, kept alive
only through a respirator. This confrontational position detects a sort of leth-
argy encrypted within H.1.J.O.S.; a stubbornness that also informs the Madres’
'7 ‘En mis historias, en las cabezas de mis personajes hay colectivos abandonados, que en
algun momento funcionaron pero ahora ya no. Estan buscando algo que es previo a todo, buscan
un origen, una identidad, necesitan saber quiénes fueron sus padres pero eso les impide armar otras
familias’ (my translation).

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 1s7
circling around a square. As defiant as it sounds, Bruzzone proposes a compul-
sive ending of melancholic ruminations around parental figures as the condition
for building new affective communities. By contrast, this is how the author
addressed his own broken lineage:
My parents turned out to be a lot of people: my grandma, my uncles and
aunts, my cousins, my friends; people that appear in life [...] One is always
searching for strange filiations. Perhaps something similar happens in
literature. '8
Thus, Los topos captures the atmosphere of expanded kinship that circulates
around the author’s personal and intellectual environment. Nonetheless, Bruz-
zone’s fiction also addresses the ambivalence that haunts the horizon of victims.
Maira’s figure also speaks about a process of mourning that is not easy to get
over. To some extent, her character echoes the figure of the drag, which in But-
ler’s perspective ‘allegorizes heterosexual melancholy’.'? In this vein, Maira’s
transsexual persona also points towards the melancholic constitution of the
‘wounded family’ and those other forms of attachment that remain ungrieved
within the bloodline normativity that it prescribes. Her obscure hopes and desires
unsettle and ‘transvestise’ the heteronormative image of the family of relatives.
Thus, Maira appears as the troublemaker at the table of the victims, the killjoy
of their moments of comfort. After all, she is also the one for whom there are
no clear affiliations. She is the one who can be depicted as the ‘non-relative’ in
the family of victims. To some extent, Maira embodies the notion of queerness,
not only as a character but also as a method. In this vein, her figure suggests
that alternative forms of mourning are also possible.
Becoming other
Halfway through, the plot of Los topos speeds up. Maira has been the victim of
an operation similar to the kidnappings that took place during the dictatorship.
Her place has been raided and there are no traces of her. The transvestite girl-
friend (and perhaps the narrator’s abducted sibling) becomes a ‘neo-disappeared’,
an intriguing condition for the democratic period, which seems to recall the fate
of Julio Lopez, a retired bricklayer who disappeared after testifying at the trial
against the perpetrator Miguel Etchecolatz in September 2006. In order to cope
with the new loss, the protagonist moves to the south, where he becomes involved
18 ‘Mis padres terminaron siendo muchas personas: mi abuela, mis tios y tias, mis primos,
mis amigos; gente que aparecié en mi vida ... Uno esta siempre buscando filiaciones raras. Algo
parecido pasa en la literatura’ (my translation). See Quintin, ‘Uno a uno. Suefio con medusas, de
Félix Bruzzone’, http://www.lalectoraprovisoria.com.ar/?p=2550 [accessed 21 February 201 iN}.
19 Butler, Bodies that Matter, p. 235.

138 CECILIA SOSA
with E/ Aleman (‘The German’), a conspicuous character who is responsible
for the murder of a frightening number of homosexuals.
After Maira’s disappearance, the protagonist undergoes a process of plastic
surgery and also embraces a transgender identity. The process of metamorpho-
sis experienced by the main character seems to pursue the impossible aim of
occupying the place of his neo-disappeared girlfriend. Her figure of sibling—
lover—alter ego does nothing but expand.the displaced images that the queer
son develops before a perverse mirror, which entwines the multiple echoes of
his—her own self. As embodying the worst of fantasies, the end of the novella
finds the narrator romantically involved with E/ Aleman, the serial killer who
might have murdered Maira and perhaps also his parents. Furthermore, this
relentless impulse of contagion reaches the figure of the killer, who also em-
braces a transgender identity and eventually becomes depicted as the Aleman-
Papa (‘German-Father’).”°
Within Bruzzone’s drama of inverted filiations the experience of a broken
generation is transformed into an astonishing, scornful and terrifying family
performance. Los topos’ final moment seems to suggest that past and present
share a common destiny, as if Maira, the missing parents and the father-perpe-
trator had grotesquely become one. In a poignant parody of Stockholm syndrome,
the narrator is not only in love with his potential killer but is also captured by a
past that exceeds his own story. Once again, the fantasy that seems at stake here
is that of having one’s own experiences ‘evacuated’ by a previous generation,
an issue that, drawing on Marianne Hirsch’s work on second generations of
survivors, I have considered in relation to the Argentine descendants.”'! To some
extent, the narrator seems to be chained to his father’s fate, ultimately condemned
to re-enact a frightening version of his parent’s story.
How to make sense of this abject ending? What kind of disturbing arrange-
ments can be established between the figure of the son, a transgender neo-dis-
appeared and a serial killer, which iterates the spectre of a missing father? Far
from any kind of whim, I would suggest that Bruzzone’s fiction deploys a dis-
placed technology of memory that can be extremely fruitful in challenging the
normative constitution of grief prescribed by the relatives. This argument needs
to be addressed in different stages. First, | contend that the progressive mutation
of Los topos’ characters shows how grief inevitably implies a process of becom-
ing other. In her reflection on grief in the wake of 9/11’s anxieties, Butler writes:
‘Perhaps mourning has to do with agreeing to undergo a transformation (perhaps
one should say submitting to a transformation) the full result of which cannot
be known in advance.’” Los topos takes this transformation to a fantastic ex-
treme. Through the successive forms of gender transitioning experienced by its
20 My emphasis.
21 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 107.
22 Butler, Precarious Life, p. 21 (Butler’s emphasis).

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 139
characters, Bruzzone’s fiction queers the fantasy of purity that lies at the core
of the ‘wounded family’. In so doing, it shows how the resonances of grief can-
not be mastered in advance. In fact, Los topos’ never-ending parody contests
not only any familiar coherence but also any regime of genealogical truth that
pretends to be secure.
Still, this argument can be taken further. In her research on affects and queer
subjectivities, Lisa Blackman argues that certain forms of affective becoming
can be grasped as ‘queer performancing’, stressing the production of new sub-
jectivities in the wake of trauma.” As local re-enactment of ‘queer peformanc-
ing’, Los topos provides an original framework to explore the emergence of
alternative affective configurations in the aftermath of violence. Moreover, it
invites readers to negotiate unconventional forms of intimacy emerging from
the experience of grief throughtraces of biographies similarly marked by shame
and trauma. While suggesting unconventional intersections between perpetrators
and victims, descendants and transvestites, Los topos displaces the normativity
of blood. It shows how the process of becoming involved in grief can assist not
only extreme but also joyful opportunities.
Defamation of kinship and trans-heroes
Provocatively enough, Bruzzone’s fiction develops an alternative sense of hu-
mour for a new stage of Argentina’s grief. For instance, the narrator confesses
that he likes to portray himself and Maira as Batman and Robin. The playful
evocation of the popular superhero couple seems to be uttering the contested
cases of the abducted siblings separated by violence. Yet the queer innuendo
that Bruzzone evokes for the cult-comic legendary duo works to enhance the
defamation of the law of kinship, performed by the displaced lineage of the
disappeared descendants. In this way, Bruzzone mocks the traditional dis-
courses of ‘happy family reunion’ proposed by the Abwel/as in their search for
the missing grandchildren. Moreover, through this camp turn in the descendant’s
drama Bruzzone anticipates the case of the Nobles addressed earlier. Although
the resistance of the siblings to the DNA test only gained major media attention
in 2010 (after Bruzzone’s book was published), Felipe and Marcela Noble can
also be thought of as a displaced version of Batman and Robin, the twinning of
the hero and the wretch who struggle to keep their genetic codes away from the
vampire-like instincts of a nation that craves blood. Mocking any vestige of
pedigree, Los topos addresses the limits of the ‘wounded family’ and the impos-
sibility of reaching its full ideals of purity. Joyfully, the very syntax of this
lineage becomes frantic, corrupted and ultimately transvestite.
