23 Lisa Blackman, ‘Affect, Performance and Queer Subjectivities’, Cultural Studies, 25.2
(2011), 183-99 (p. 195).

140 CECILIA SOSA
The successive forms of transitioning depicted by Bruzzone offer a new
response to the broken lineage enacted by the abducted son. Los topos inflates
and choreographs the traumatised lineage of the descendants through a series
of intersections among gender transgressions and political conversions that
have no end. During different and contested political periods, the figure of the
son has become progressively damaged—broken—abducted—stolen—converted—
vindicated in Argentina. Moreover, these mutations could be read alongside
the central status acquired by the relatives during Fernandez de Kirchner’s
administration. In suggesting a parallel between the descendants and a series
of trans superhero characters, Bruzzone’s fiction liberates the descendants
from their fatal destiny. It suggests that the figure of the son is not rigid and
fixed forever but, rather, open to constant mutations and processes of becom-
ing. Thus, the descendants are no longer constrained by the duty of remem-
brance but free to reign under other guises. The inscription of the descendants’
drama alongside feelings of kinship that go beyond the limits regulated by
law could also be addressed as a process of ‘queer performancing’, to use
Blackman’s expression. It ultimately stresses the production of alternative
subjectivities in the wake of trauma.
Recurring melancholia or the broken spell?
In the last scene of the book, the readers find £7 Aleman and the queer son trans-
formed into a transsexual couple who share a romantic venue near a gorgeous
Patagonian lake. Even so, the past seems to be just around the corner: ‘When-
ever you are ready, we can go out to search for Maira, you just let me know and
that’s it.’** In this muddled temporality, the search for Maira is then inevitable.
Somehow, the readers are back at the beginning, as if they were listening to the
grandmother Lela’s crazy shouting in front of ESMA. The last line of the story
finds the protagonist captured by a new stillness: ‘To be truthful, right now, with
this cold, there is not much more to do.’* Instead of considering this closing
scene as tragedy, I suggest that it captures a hidden form of the comical, one that
according to Bergson follows the shape of a ‘roulette’.”° The narrator’s quietness
highlights how the work of mourning involves for the descendants dealing with
entangled temporalities that often do not adjust. Indeed, for the offspring of
Argentine trauma, past, present and future can appear to be intimately bound, as
if the past were never completely buried. Thus, Los topos’ cyclical ending
stages the precise moment in which mourning enters into a limbo-zone, in which
24 ‘Cuando vos quieras salimos a buscar a Maira, vos me decis y listo’ (Bruzzone, Los topos,
p. 189, my translation).
25 ‘La verdad que ahora, con este frio, no hay mucho mas para hacer’ (Bruzzone, Los topos,
p. 189, my translation).
26 Bergson, Laughter, p. 24.

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 14]
there seems to be no future. In its dark and bitter mood, Los topos’ last scene
apparently reverses the author’s spoken command to get over melancholia while
suggesting that the stubborn pace of Argentina’s mourning might be also its secret
power, as obscure, sticky and resistant as a mole. Drawing upon this, | contend
that Los topos’ final scene stages this critical scenario in order to break the spell.
In so doing, it manages to liberate an alternative space in the present.
In order to demonstrate this, we need to go back to the Patagonian landscape
where the narrator-son and the German-father are having their romantic meal.
Then we should listen carefully to the protagonist’s rambling thoughts:
What Maira wanted, what Mum wanted, and also Dad in his way, was a new
world, a world anew, a new man, blissful men because of the arrival and the
promise of return, the whtte sisters, the purest daughters of the snow, of the
most intense coldness that becomes a night of love.?’
This frantic, poetic and hallucinatory monologue seems to propose at least one
horrifying possibility: that the descendants may want exactly the same as their
missing parents wanted. By staging this extreme version, Los topos not only
contests the obsession with the past, but also the ‘happy’ scenes of reconciliation
championed by some of the relatives’ associations. In fact, the evocation of these
‘purest daughters of the snow’ seems to deploy the idea of pedigree that circu-
lated through the seemingly ‘pure’ lineage of descendants. The last whispers of
the queer son somehow prefigure the furious response delivered by the relatives
during the Schoklender scandal: undoubtedly, in the view of the selective rela-
tives, a parent-killer has neither the ‘right blood’ nor the ‘purity’ to wear the
Madres’ white scarves.
Los topos seems to do nothing but iterate the biological normativity of the
‘wounded family’. Nevertheless, I suggest reading it as exactly the opposite.
Moreover, Bruzzone’s fiction enables me to sharpen my response to Diana
Taylor’s idea of the ‘DNA of performance’. In Taylor’s perspective, Argentina
post-dictatorship is embedded in a self-same repetitive paradigm of public
presentation based on a bloodline connection of biological kinship.* In my own
analysis, Los topos queers this framework to offer a different answer. While
Taylor conceives the idea of transmission as attached to sameness and repetition
of biological kinship, Bruzzone’s fiction enables the reader to rethink kinship
as a form of transmission through difference. In so doing, Los topos also reveals
alternative subjectivities that have emerged from grief. These forms of ‘queer
27 ‘Lo que Maria queria, lo que queria mama, y papa a su manera, mundo nuevo, nuevo
mundo, hombre nuevo, hombres felices por la Ilegada, y la promeso de rotorno, de la hermanas
blancas, las mas puras, las hijas de la nieve, del frio mas intenso que se vuelve noche de amor’
(Bruzzone, Los topos, p. 185, my translation).
28 Taylor, The Archive and the Repertoire, p. 175.

142 CECILIA SOSA
becoming’, to borrow again Blackman’s expression, do not correspond to the
biological normativity prescribed by the official discourse, but suggest a novel
combination of affect and desires which disfigures the bloodline normativity.
Within Los topos’ twisted lineage, there is no origin to be recovered but rather
endless distortions that have no secure law.
Let us take this argument a step further. While reworking performativity as
a form of citationality, Butler notes that to ‘cite’ the law is to produce it differ-
ently and finally to displace it.’ In parallel terms, Los topos ‘cites’ the biologi-
cal normativity of the ‘wounded family’ to show how the possibility of forging
a different future will always come from resources that are inevitably impure.*°
In so doing, it creates unforeseen possibilities for non-biological encounters,
which suggest twisted coalitions of trans-characters across time. Indeed, the
final image of dads, mums and the ‘pure’ daughters of the snow stages a self-
fashioned community of queer affections. Waiting for the arrival of the ‘new
world’, this queer family mimes and displaces the performance of DNA, while
transforming it into a last big joke. Hence, Los topos’ fatal ending exhibits the
worst-case scenario in order to reverse the threat. It does what the human rights
narratives and the discourses of the relatives cannot do: it demonstrates how
humour can queer traditional repertoires of memory that are still at stake in
contemporary Argentina. Against Taylor’s predictions, Bruzzone’s fiction il-
lustrates how young generations can always manage to find their spaces in the
present. Via its dark laughter, Los topos ultimately shows the extent to which
the legitimacy of blood cannot be taken for granted. The novella emerges as a
startling response to a period of institutionalisation of grief. In enabling the
readers to taste the flavour of the non-normative, other forms of communities
might become palatable, and to some extent, also enjoyable.
A new grief: Kirchner’s death
Despite the fact that Los topos was published in 2008, I suggest that the book
foresaw how disputes around varying versions of the past would produce dra-
matic intersections in subsequent years. During Fernandez de Kirchner’s first
term of office, Argentine society experienced a split into seemingly irreconcil-
able factions that persists today. Although the process began in late 2008,
mainly following the official decision to implement an export tax on agricul-
tural goods, by late 2010 this had led to a major political conflict.*' The dispute
29 Butler, Bodies that Matter, p. 15.
30 Butler, Bodies that Matter, p. 241.
31 In fact, during the passing of Law 125, the vice-president, Julio Cleto Cobos, voted against
the official proposal, splitting the government into two irreconcilable sections. Nonetheless, as
Brenda Werth argues, this economic conflict had its beginnings in the eighteenth century. See
Werth, Theatre, Performance, and Memory Politics, p. 205.

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 143
was articulated between two main belligerents: the Kirchnerist government and
El Campo (The Farm). If the tension was a classic, it also came to be read in
terms of the heritage of dictatorship.
In September 2010 I arrived in Buenos Aires to give a presentation at an
international conference on the politics of memory that was scheduled to take
place at the former ESMA.* I was surprised by the strong sense of polarisation
that contorted the public sphere. There seemed to be no space in-between, nor
room for critique or any other mediation within binaries that had become com-
pletely hegemonic. Even my most sceptical friends and colleagues felt compelled
to take sides, as if the only possible positions were to be for or against the
Kirchnerist government. Eventually, the opposition came to be articulated in
Clarin, the national newspaper and the most powerful media voice in the coun-
try.** The political arena was c6nceived in terms of a war. By the end of October
2010 the spiral of violence had its final expression in a trade union clash in
which Mariano Ferreyra, a 23-year-old student and member of the Partido
Obrero (Workers’ Party), was murdered during a demonstration in support of
rail employees made redundant.** His death was conceived as the first political
bereavement of the Kirchnerist period. The resonances were vast. Still, just one
week later, another major and unexpected event completely changed the politi-
cal scenario; the former President Kirchner died of a heart attack. The shocking
news worked as a test to calibrate the feelings of kinship that had emerged from
loss. | would like to read this period of national bereavement in tune with the
progressive chain of queer metamorphosis anticipated by Bruzzone’s novella.
‘Orphans once again’
27 October 2010 was an unusual day. It was declared a bank holiday since the
National Population Census was taking place, and people were obliged to stay
at home. By 10 a.m. the news of Kirchner’s death was being broadcast across
the media. While detractors furtively celebrated indoors, by sunset thousands
of supporters had gathered at the Plaza de Mayo. They cried in the streets and
brought flowers and home-made souvenirs for his memorial. Thousands of
youngsters with apparently no previous political background positioned them-
selves front and centre to pay homage to the leader who had made them “dis-
32 The III Seminario Internacional Politicas de la Memoria ‘Recordando a Walter Benjamin:
Justicia, Historia y Verdad. Escrituras de la Memoria’ was originally scheduled for 28-30 October
2010 at the ‘Haroldo Conti’ cultural centre. It finally took place from 30 October to 1 November
because of the official period of national mourning.
33 After 2010 the media group led by Clarin became a main political actor.
34 Laura Vales, ‘Morir por apoyar a trabajadores despedidos’, Pagina 12, 21 October 2010,
http://www.paginal 2.com.ar/diario/elpais/1-155429-2010-10-21 htm] [accessed 12 November 2011].

144 CECILIA SOSA
cover the pleasure ofpo litics’, as they expressed it.*° It was also the first public
incursion of La Campora. A multitude of youngsters had taken over the Plaza
de Mayo to say goodbye to their father. As it was argued, nobody could tell the
strength of this bond until the moment of death.*°
For the memorial service, Cristina Fernandez de Kirchner stood for hours
next to her husband’s coffin, which was set up in the Casa Rosada. The govern-
ment building remained open for the vigil, and a multitude queued the whole
night to offer condolences to the president. ‘Be strong, Cristina’ was the insist-
ent plea.*’? During the communal and highly theatrical procession, the widow
received the support of hundreds of mourners who had become, somehow, an
extended family in grief. The relatives’ associations occupied the foreground of
the demonstrations. Estela Carlotto, the leader of Abuelas, and Hebe de Bonafini
were the first to console the widow. The Madres brought their scarves to cover
the grave. ‘He was also our son,’ they said evoking Kirchner’s inaugural speech
in which he presented himself as the ‘son’ of the Madres and the Abuelas of
Plaza de Mayo. Bonafini wept in front of the coffin. ‘Don’t cry Hebe, don’t cry,
we need to be strong,’ urged the president embracing the Madre. ‘Orphans once
again,’ bewailed H.I.J.O.S. ‘He was our second father,’ proclaimed young
Kirchnerists.** The media suspended all ordinary transmissions. Juan Cabandié,
one of the abducted children and personal protégé of the deceased leader, and
also an upcoming leader of La Campora, cried on the radio, incapable of pro-
nouncing a word.” His weeping spoke of this helpless feeling of orphandom
that circulated within broad sectors of Argentine society.
| would like to suggest that the events precipitated by this new grief showed
the extent to which the notion of kinship in play in Argentina no longer cor-
responded to a traditional family. The choreographed ceremony of mourning
was empowered by a sense of affective support, which was not at all norma-
tive. It was not the idea of a terrorised Argentine family targeted by the mili-
35 Julieta Nassau and Lujan Scarpinealli, ‘Los jévenes K conquistaron la plaza’, La Nacién,
21 October 2011, http://www.lanacion.com.ar/1319685-los-jovenes-k-conquistaron-la-plaza [ac-
cessed 12 November 2011].
36 This is also in tune with Laura Di Marco’s argument in La Campora. Historia secreta de
los herederos de Néstor y Cristina Kirchner (Buenos Aires: Sudamericana), p. 23.
37 “iFuerza Cristina!’ (my translation). The slogan became the /eitmotif of the funeral, as
reported by the international media. See Soledad Gallego-Diaz and Alejandro Rebossio, ‘Argen-
tina se funde en el abrazo a Kirchner’, E/ Pais, 28 October 2010, http://www.elpais.com/articulo/
internacional/Argentina/funde/abrazo/Kirchner/elpepuint/20101028elpepuint_6/Tes [accessed 12
November 2011]
38 ‘El también era nuestro hijo’, ‘No llores, no llores, tenemos que ser fuertes’, ‘Nos queda-
mos huérfanos otra vez’, ‘El era nuestro segundo padre’ (my translation). See Tiempo Argentino,
29 October 2010, http://tiempo.infonews.com/notas/cristina-no-llores-hebe-no-llores-que-hay-
que-ser-fuerte [accessed 12 November 2011].
39 As stated in the Introduction, Cabandié was one of the speakers at the recovery of ESMA
in March 2004.

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 145
tary regime, nor a simple re-staging of the ‘wounded family’ championed by
the relatives. Rather, it was a self-fashioned and self-created and, to some
extent, also joyful community in mourning, which made visible the uncon-
ventional affiliations built as a response to grief. The multitude that was at
stake during those days could be perceived as an expanded family in loss, one
that enacted a non-biological conception of kinship, which had broadly perme-
ated at least one important sector of civil society. This alternative political
lineage circulated through Argentine society through different unarticulated
forms. Kirchner’s death made it visible. It confirmed a queer displacement
between family, loss and political heritage. This displacement of kinship was
clandestinely announced by Los topos in 2008.
During those days of sorrow, Kirchner’s famous statement in his inaugural
speech resonated again and again: ‘I am the son of the Madres and Abuelas of
Plaza de Mayo.’ In the wake of death, that slogan acquired an expanded politi-
cal significance. Indeed, the appropriation of the role of the injured son by the
former leader displaced the uniqueness usually attached to the figure of the
mother. Instead, the position of the son was opened to an expanded series of
political and affective iterations, since, arguably, we can all be daughters and
sons. I would like to suggest that this displacement of the route of the descend-
ants was prefigured by Maira’s character. Drawing on a twisted lineage of the
descendants’ drama, Bruzzone’s book had already invited its readers to imagine
the non-conventional forms of attachment emerging out of a common loss. In
its dark, extreme language, Los topos offered the opportunity to envision the
reconfiguration of an affective community, which ultimately suggested an al-
ternative conception of politics attached to non-biological modes of love, support
and care. Eventually, La Campora would appear as the most flagrant (and
eventually threatening) development of the period.
Black light theatre: the mother-widow, the neo-disappeared father and
their heirs
In this last section, I would like to explore a progression within the non-normative
system of kinship that features in the post-Kirchner era. At the beginning of this
period, a new political alliance was constituted. As the local scholar Leon Ro-
zitchner suggests, this lineage draws upon female figures such as the Madres and
Abuelas, leaving vacant the position of the ‘Terrible Father’. In fact, immedi-
ately after Kirchner’s death, doubts proliferated about whether his widow would
be able to command a country gripped by internal tensions and recurrent griev-
ances. The main doubts concerned whether the president could deal with the many
40 Leon Rozitchner, ‘Un nuevo modelo de pareja politica’, Pagina 12, 10 November 2010,
http://www. pagina 2.com.ar/diario/elpais/subnotas/1 56626-50240-2010-11-10.html [accessed 25
November 2011].

146 CECILIA SOSA
contesting factions inside the Peronist movement that she had inherited. While
fears abounded, her supporters played out the role of a new breed of orphans, the
camporistas. The proto-formation of the movement was announced by a former
H.1.J.O.S. activist, who posted on his personal blog: ‘There is Cristina, the
Mother who will continue, who will resist. First are the Mothers. Now that the
father is dead, Cristina has to raise all her children alone, who love her so much.”*!
During those days of bereavement a process of transference took place. A non-
normative political lineage nurtured the power of the mother-widow while enhanc-
ing the potentialities of a new cast of heirs. I suggest that Fernandez de Kirchner
managed to rebuild her legitimacy, drawing from the figure of these other Madres,
the women who did not want to relinquish their own losses and came to the gov-
ernment building to offer their scarves to the dead man and their support to the
widow in power. In this displaced family in power, I argue, Kirchner, the deceased
leader, was transformed into a ‘neo-disappeared’, almost like Maira.
In fact, during the days that followed Kirchner’s death, Fernandez de Kirch-
ner did not hesitate to make visible her sorrow. Only one week after his death,
she featured in a compelling 5-minute video for the nation. Dressed in an elegant
mourning outfit — which she wore for two years — she looked empowered by
pain.” She discouraged political adversaries by insisting that losing her husband
was not the most ‘difficult? moment of her life, but the most ‘painful’.** The
president went further: ‘This is the loss of a partner of 35 years, a life partner,
a partner of struggle, of ideals. A part of myself vanished with him.“ She fin-
ished the speech abruptly, broken by tears. Although the discourse could have
been recorded again, it was known that Fernandez de Kirchner preferred not to.
In some obscure way, her bereavement emerged as a sign of authority. In this
process, the president managed to transfer her loss to the audience, seeking a
national operation of healing.
Twenty days after the former leader’s death, the widow was back in public.
This time she referred to her husband as ‘the strongest and the best among all
of us’.*° The narrative was remarkable: she used the same vocabulary that the
41 ‘Ahi esta Cristina, la madre, para seguir, para resistir. Primero a las madres. Porque
muerto el padre, ahora Cristina tiene que criar sola a todos sus H.1.J.0.S. que tanto la aman’ (my
translation).
42 The president only abandoned her mourning outfits in October 2013, when she resumed
her role after a surgical intervention that kept her out of the administration for a month.
43 Nicolas Llantos, ‘No es el momento mas dificil es el momento mas doloroso’, Pagina 12,
2 November 2010, http://www.pagina12.com.ar/diario/elpais/1-156137-2010-11-02.html [accessed
24 January 2011].
44 ‘Es la pérdida de quien fue mi compafiero durante 35 aitos, compafiero de vida, de lucha,
de ideales. Una parte mia se fue con él’ (my translation). Pagina 12, 2 November 2010, http://
www.paginal2.com.ar/diario/elpais/1-156137-2010-11-02.html [accessed 24 January 2011].
45 ‘El era el mas fuerte y mejor que todos nosotros’ (my translation), 17 November 2010,
http://www.cadena3.com/contenido/2010/11/16/65855.asp [accessed 24 January 2011].

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 147
relatives use to pay tribute to the ‘disappeared’. In fact, the slogan ofth e miss-
ing as the ‘best ones’ largely informs the national repertoire of loss. The same
legitimising images were iterated as part of an expanded operation of transfer-
ence. In her next intervention, the president dedicated her speech to the young-
sters who took part in the memorial demonstrations:
Let me thank especially the tens of thousands of youngsters who sang and
demonstrated with pain and joy, chanting for him, for their motherland. |
want to tell all these young people that, on each of their faces, I saw his
face as I had seen it for the first time. There was his face, exactly. And allow
me to tell these youngsters that they are luckier than he was when he was
young, because they live in a much better country, a country that did not
abandon them, a country that did not condemn them, nor persecute them. On
the contrary, a country that summoned them, a country that loves them, and
needs them, a country that we will still build together.*°
The quality of the manoeuvre was exceptional. The elusive transpositions evoked
by the presidential speech suggested a spectral contagion across generations. In
the faces of those youngsters who flooded the Plaza de Mayo on the day of the
leader’s death, Fernandez de Kirchner saw ‘his face’. The image of the missing
circulating back and forth contributed to narrow the distance between the de-
ceased leader and the new generations of supporters. Somehow, La Campora
had a visionary blessing. In fact, this entanglement of faces, evoked by the
president, could be thought as the counter-token of the faceless assemblage
constituted between the disappeared and the unnoticed servants in Martel’s film,
as addressed in Chapter 3. To some extent they are both self-fashioned and ul-
timately affective communities constituted ‘across times’, to use Dinshaw’s
expression.*” Somehow those thousands missing from the dictatorship also in-
habited these other youngsters ‘luckier than he was’, who came to bid Kirchner
farewell. I would argue, therefore, that the liturgy of the ‘wounded family’
empowered the presidential figure with a renewed aura of authority. The trans-
ference was endorsed. Although over the following years this process eventu-
ally found its most public face in La Campora, it also transcended it. A survey

de é/, exacto. Y decirles a esos j6venes que tienen mucha mas suerte que cuando é/ era joven,

148 CECILIA SOSA
published a week after the former leader’s death revealed that Fernandez de
Kirchner’s strongest support was among those under 40.** Less than one year
later, on 23 October 2011 she was re-elected as president by 54 per cent of the
votes. For the celebration she was surrounded by her son Maximo’s friends, /os
camporistas.”
I would like to take this argument a step further. This process of transmission
managed to bring two experiences of mourning together: a collective loss at-
tached to the military repression and the isolated and premature death of a po-
litical leader. Despite the obvious distance between these losses, the interventions
of the widow-president infused them with a similar affective atmosphere. It
worked as if the new grief demanded the vigour of the previous one. Via its
legitimising impulse, the president asked for redemption, not only of her loss
but also of her government. In this process of transference, the death of the
former leader endorsed a new heroic narrative. If in life Néstor Kirchner had
adopted the role of the ‘son’, after his death he became a ‘neo-disappeared’.
Almost deified, he has started being recalled as ‘EL’ (‘HE’).*° Estela Carlotto,
leader of Abuelas, best acknowledged this affective transmission: ‘HE is already
with the 30,000 [disappeared],’ she said.*!
In this expanded family of loss, Fernandez de Kirchner adopted her public
image of mother-widow. By the end of January 2011 this malleable series of
mutations, ultimately sanctified by a maternal spirit, was made explicit. Facing
the last months of her first term of office, the president claimed:
Like all mothers I will take care of the manifesto initiated in 2003 as if it
were a baby [...]. It is a creature that will turn eight years old, who has lost
her father but still has a mother who will fight tooth and nail to keep her
safe.”
In this way, the president infused the role of the mother with new life. The young
branch of camporistas was fundamental to this. In fact, this displaced perfor-
mance of kinship was already in play in Bruzzone’s novella. Los topos offered

=0521 [accessed 22 December 2011].
49 This is what the journalist Laura Di Marco recalls in her book. See La Caémpora, p. 24.

KINSHIP, LOSS AND POLITICAL HERITAGE 149
a queer account of the drama of the descendant, a trans-parody where all famil-
ial positions were subverted. I would suggest that the expansion of kinship that
came to light after Kirchner’s death reminds us of how the past can become a
‘vibrant source for community building’ in the present, as the queer scholar
Dinshaw well acknowledges.* The sense of belonging to this political com-
munity does not respond to biological inscriptions, but rather to the fantasies of
a nation empowered by grief. Since Kirchner’s death, loss has emerged as the
affective experience that illuminates non-biological forms of political bonding
that become transmitted throughout different sectors of Argentine society. The
image of an expanded family in loss contributed to cherishing an expanded idea
of ‘we’. This lineage is the one that has furtively governed the country. This
series of non-aligned acts of mourning has mimed and displaced the plethora
of family arrangements established by the relatives. In so doing, it has also
queered conventional repertoires of memory.
During the last few years, Argentina’s democracy has been infused with a
renewed legion of ‘sons’ and ‘daughters’. At the beginning of 2012 the
president had to undergo thyroid surgery, with the possibility that cancer might
be present, though in fact it was not.** During the run-up to the day on which
the surgery was scheduled, 4 January, a group of young activists promoted a
public campaign for blood donation. ‘Our blood for Cristina’ was the slogan.*°
The campaign, originally attributed to La Campora, remained mostly isolated
within a branch of young Peronist activists from the north of Buenos Aires.
Nevertheless, the narrative was crucial. I suggest that the initiative worked as
a splendid image of the process of conversion that was already taking place,
another form of secular Eucharist perhaps even more explicit than those cel-
ebrated during the cooking lessons at ESMA. By offering blood to the moth-
er-president, a legion of young Argentines had the unique opportunity of
joining the expanded political community in mourning. No ‘blue’ blood was
required. In return, the ‘descendants’ — regardless of age, class or pedigree —
became blessed and ultimately purified. The transference of blood conse-
crated the new orphans into the official lineage.
In this sense, the post-Kirchner era has suggested a re-domiciliation of Ar-
gentina’s mourning. It has illustrated the transference of a lineage. The univocal
relation between victims and family roles has been displaced. By subverting the
biological inscription of heritage, ownership of the experience of loss was re-
moved from being the exclusive preserve of the relatives. I would suggest that
