principal/index.html [accessed 8 January 2012].
55 See ‘La Campora lanzé la consigna “La sangre por Cristina”’, La Nacion, 31 December
2011, http://www. lanacion.com.ar/143 7049-la-campora-lanzo-la-consigna-la-sangre-por-cristina
[accessed 1 January 2012].

150 CECILIA SOSA
this was precisely the process of conversion envisaged by Los topos. In the
current period, ‘the force of blood’, as claimed by the young Campora activists,
has become a key ingredient of politics.°° The happy narrative of the ‘wounded
family’ lies like a corpse on stage. The performance of DNA has been joyfully
infiltrated, transvestised, queered. To some extent, the figure of the Kirchnerist
couple has worked as progressive spies; they have been infiltrators or ‘double
agents’ within the narrative of the victims. In an unintended way, and much as
in Bruzzone’s fiction, this expanded model of political family created a room
for non-conventional forms of connection. Listening carefully, it is possible to
hear laughter coming from the background. The moles are giggling again. They
welcome new ‘orphans’ to join in this queer lineage ofl oss.
56 ‘La fuerza de la sangre’ (my translation).

Conclusion
The Recovery of the House
This book opened with the story of ah ouse. So too does it end. This story comes
in different acts, which enfold one another.
P
Act 1: A house under arrest
In Archive Fever: A Freudian Impression, Jacques Derrida argues that the first
figure of an archive is topological. It is the violence of a power, a lineage, a place,
a domicile: ‘It is thus, in this domiciliation, in this house arrest, that archives take
place.’' In the aftermath of Argentina’s dictatorship, the relatives of the victims
have commanded the house of mourning. To some extent, they have kept this
house under arrest. They have been the ones who had the force to command the
legitimacy of remembering. As such, they have been the guardians of the pro-
cesses of mourning. This right has been animated by the power of blood. This
book, however, has sought to show how the domiciliation of this archive has been
displaced. In this sense, it has encompassed a movement of transference of the
experience of loss, one that goes from the relatives to the broader Argentine so-
ciety. Non-biological feelings of kinship have re-occupied the house.
In order to illustrate this process of re-occupation, this book has delineated
a non-normative archive. It is an archive of unconventional acts of mourning.
It does not rely exclusively on blood but on bonds that have been forged through
acts of coming up against violence. My aim has been to explore those forms of
social belonging that have emerged from loss. They are plural ties, which trans-
pose conventional settings and propose a common condition of survival. This
archive gathers different experiences together across different generations.
Chapter 1 used the Madres’ statement ‘Our children gave birth to us’ as the
departure point for considering a non-biological lineage that reversed genea-
logical bonds. Chapter 2 explored how the black humour that informed H.I.J.O.S.
worked as a form of affective reparation in the face of loss. Chapter 3 proposed
a dialogue between Los Rubios, Mand La mujer sin cabeza, showing how these
disparate films have displaced the cult of the victim that defined the earlier
‘cinema of the disappeared’. Chapter 4 showed how the cooking sessions that
1 Derrida, Archive Fever, p. 2 (Derrida’s emphasis).

la CECILIA SOSA
took place at ESMA suggest a conversion of the site of death through food
gatherings. Chapter 5 explored Lola Arias’s Mi vida después as an inter-gener-
ational artefact for the transmission of trauma on- and off-stage. Chapter 6
considered Félix Bruzzone’s novella Los topos as a prophetic exploration of the
language of kinship that has governed the country since Kirchner’s death. The
materials of this archive share a non-normative impulse. They shed light on a
new sense of ‘being together’ that has emerged in the wake of violence. They
also show how the aftermath of violence has not only produced pain but also
new forms of pleasure. Ultimately, this book illuminates how grief has offered
space to a new community.
In the following pages, I would like to explore some oft he encounters, dia-
logues and tensions that emerge among the subjects and materials involved in
this unconventional archive of mourning. I will also examine a curious incident
that took place when the law allowing same-sex marriage was about to be passed
in Argentina. Then, I will draw on some strands of biographies marked by
trauma to show how the concept of performance that informs this archive has
enabled me to understand the relation between personal lives and social forms
of becoming. I will conclude by offering a comment on Monument to Escape
(2001, Dennis Oppenheim), an installation based at the Parque de la Memoria
in Buenos Aires. I will make the case that Oppenheim’s piece helps to conceive
how the house, which was placed ‘under arrest’ during the post-dictatorship
period, has been expanded to host alternative forms of support and care.
Act 2: Queer dialogues, unexpected encounters
This book has proposed a novel approach to examining Argentina’s aftermath
of violence. In so doing, it has brought together the fields of kinship, queer
theory and performance studies. To some extent, the rephrased conception of
kinship I have proposed throughout these pages draws from Judith Butler. As
she writes:
If we understand kinship as a set of practices that institutes relationships
of various kinds, which negotiate the reproduction of life and the demands
of death, then kinship practices will be those that emerge to address
fundamental forms of human dependency, which may include birth, child
rearing, relations of emotional dependency and support, generational ties,
illness, dying, and death.
Indeed, the aftermath of Argentina’s experience of violence has witnessed the
emergence of alternative forms of support, dependency and care for addressing
the ubiquitous demands of loss in the restored democratic context. These mo-
2 Butler, Giving an Account of Oneself, pp. 102-3.

CONCLUSION 33
dalities of social belonging have staged forms oft ies that exceeded blood. More
than this, | have contended that they can be thought of as ‘queer bonds’. A
recent discussion on this issue published in GLO enables me to frame further
the intersection of fields that is at stake in this book. Joshua Weiner and Damon
Young address queer bonds as those forms of ties that appear under diverse
conditions of negation and constraint, which query autonomous definitions of
the subject They also argue that queer bonds do not occur ‘in spite of but
because of some force of negation, in which it is precisely negativity that or-
ganizes scenes of togetherness’. Indeed, in these chapters, | have explored the
scene that emerged in Argentina as a response to the military violence and the
forceful reworking of kinship imposed by its brutal intervention in the intimate-
public sphere. While conceiving those attachments emerging from loss as ‘queer
bonds’, I suggest an expansion of the field of kinship studies to address a
constellation of new intimacies from a locally situated response to trauma.
Moreover, this book studies these alternative attachments under the frame of
performance studies to explore their potentiality for allowing new forms of
social becoming. These modes of social bonding are not necessarily homo-
sexual, but rather conceive queerness ‘as a way of being-with and a mode of
intimacy’, as Weiner and Young state.°
Thus, by critically engaging with the work of different queer scholars — But-
ler, David Eng and Sara Ahmed, among others — this book examines the forms
of attachment that have existed around, outside of, or in the interstices of what
I have referred to as the ‘wounded family’; that is, the net of associations con-
formed by the relatives of those missing, who have tended to appear as the only
victims of violence. In this process, I have insisted on queerness as a form of
critique against the bloodline normativity. Far from dismissing the pain of those
directly affected by violence, I have been curious to explore other modes of
grief that touch on less’ obvious zones of injury. While looking for new spaces
(and faces) around and beyond bloodline attachments, I have shown how seem-
ingly less implicated witnesses can also adopt and partake in loss. In this respect,
my approach has been concerned with reorienting the modes of critique towards
a more inclusive politics of mourning, one in which a new ‘with-ness’ could be
available to wider sectors of Argentine society.
This book also shows how violence has allowed forms of cohabitation that
otherwise might not have been possible. Throughout these pages, precariousness
has been the condition of passionate encounters. Against conventional frame-
works, this study has focused on pleasure. I have asked what attachments bind
a society coming from loss and how these ties are constituted and transmitted
across different generations. In so doing, I have sought to show how the realms
3 Weiner and Young, ‘Queer Bonds’, p. 223.
4 Weiner and Young, ‘Queer Bonds’, p. 236.
5 Weiner and Young, ‘Queer Bonds’, p. 237.

154 CECILIA SOSA
of the family and kinship are inescapably political and also the threshold space
where unconventional pleasures could be inscribed.
The chapters of this book can be conceived as a series of queer family encoun-
ters. Drawing on Ahmed’s work on tables as main kinship objects, my project has
attempted to queer the table of Argentine victims.° In this sense, | have welcomed
new guests to the table, even those who might look ‘oblique’, ‘strange’ or ‘out of
place’ within the traditional net of victims.’ For instance, | have presented the
cooking sessions taking place at ESMA as a recreation of family dinners around
an unconventional table located at a former detention camp. I have also shown how
Bruzzone’s fiction suggests an expansion of the table of the ‘wounded family’ to
include nephews, cousins and daughters-in-law, and, ultimately, all those who, like
myself, may assume grief as a personal lived experience.
The perspective of performance studies has allowed me to examine pro-
cesses of identification, imagination and projection in relation to traumatic pasts
among less proximate witnesses.* While accounts of the past have traditionally
emerged from the side of the ‘injured’, my archive has sought to make room for
alternative voices. The subjects that speak through these pages have different
backgrounds and stories. For instance, I have considered how the theatrical
production created by Lola Arias, who has no victims within her family, offers
a playful stage for the adoption of trauma in the context of spectatorship. Even
when considering narratives appearing from the side of the ‘injured’, I have
focused on materials that shed light on unconventional acts of mourning. For
instance, the analysis of Los topos shows how a son of the disappeared could
become a ‘double agent’ to portray the series of mutations experienced by the
descendants in the face of grief.
This project not only suggests a performative reworking of kinship beyond
bloodline borders, it also relies on a broad conception of performance. In this
sense, I have proposed an encounter with different actors, on- and off-stage.
Vulnerability has not here been a position inhabited by the victims; it has been
rather the very condition of spectatorship. On this basis, the possibility of shar-
ing in grief has been expanded to ‘foreign’ witnesses who have an extra op-
portunity to adopt trauma in trials, theatres, cinematic experiences, cooking
sessions and other sites of public intimacy in which the traumatic past returns
to be addressed collectively.
Overlooked by many studies on ‘post-memory’, humour has been of central
importance here. This book has shown how younger generations explore legacies
of trauma and grief not only without renouncing their own languages, but also
by building new ones. Drawing on the dark slang created by H.1.J.O.S., I have
explored how different approaches and dimensions of the comical have ex-
6 Ahmed, ‘Orientations’, p. 570.
Ahmed, ‘Orientations’, p. 570.
8 Hirsch, ‘The Generation of Post-memory’, p. 113.

CONCLUSION LSS)
panded the vocabularies available for loss. In this sense, humour has also con-
tributed to the building of an ew sense of togetherness. Nonetheless, it is worth
noticing that my involvement with post-dictatorship Argentina is not intended to
be exclusive. Rather, I suggest that the collection ofe xperiences gathered in this
archive may be productive to explore wider forms of transmission of trauma in
other landscapes dealing with disparate traumatic pasts. After all, this archive
has no masters, though it might have multiple gatekeepers.°
Act 3: Tensions, progressions, inversions
The materials included in this book not only suggest encounters, but also ten-
sions, progressions and inversions. One of the main displacements identified
within these pages has been related to the enhanced performance of blood
witnessed during the Kirchnerist era. In this sense, I have attempted to provide
a response to Diana Taylor’s idea of the ‘DNA of performance’. Although her
concept has been particularly useful in exploring the many ways in which blood
has remained at the heart of this period, I have also considered how and why it
fails to account for the expansion of kinship that has occurred in Argentina
post-2003. Abuelas’ search for the abducted children has relied on DNA tests,
which have been exceptionally helpful in reuniting families dismembered by
violence.'° Nonetheless, far from a scientific genetic fingerprint encoding the
unique characteristics of individual subjects, in contemporary Argentina blood
has become a malleable political tie that has enabled different sorts of mutations
and transitioning. This enhanced politicisation of blood can be illustrated through
a brief example. Like many other recovered grandchildren, Alejandro Sand-
oval, whose story was explored in the Introduction, has become a passionate
supporter of the current government. His website bears a suggestive title: ADN
Nacional y Popular (‘National and Popular DNA’).'' The slogan shows how the
performance of blood has become divorced from a simple biological basis to
emerge as a key political platform. In this respect, the case of the Nobles has
been paradigmatic. Although in July 2011 the results of DNA tests showed that
the siblings are unlikely to be descendants of the disappeared (their samples did
not match the files gathered at the National Genetic Bank), the nuances of their
blood have been part of a heated discussion, proliferating in broad sectors of
Argentine society. Ironically, their DNA became ‘a popular and national’ concern
and the subject of a soap opera in many ways. In this sense, I suggest that Tay-
lor’s performance of DNA has been queered.
9 [am grateful to Catherine Silverstone, my former PhD supervisor at Queen Mary, Univer-
sity of London, for suggesting this progression.
10 In November 2013 Abuelas managed to recover grandchild number 109.
11 Alejandro Sandoval, ‘ADN Nacional y Popular’, http://adnnacionalypopbluolgaspro.t .
com/2011/10/batalla-cultural-peronistas-somos-todos.htm|?spref=tw [accessed 12 October 2011].

156 CECILIA SOSA
Moreover, the cases studied here have demonstrated how the experience of
mourning proposes the reassembling of alternative communities which go far
beyond bloodline ties. To some extent, each chapter stresses different aspects,
folds and possibilities oft his community building. The crew wearing the blonde
wigs at the end of Los Rubios stands for an extended non-biological family in
mourning. This sense of togetherness reappears in the expanded subjectivity
built by Arias’s performance, in which the team of actors cross-dresses the
parental figures to create new characters for the future. As a counter-token, Los
topos teases the fantasy of purity that remains at the core of the “wounded
family’ through an organic assemblage of parents and perpetrators. As already
observed, by staging the worst-case scenario, Bruzzone’s fiction also counter-
signs the performance of blood.
Yet the feelings of kinship explored within this archive do not follow a lin-
ear temporality. This book is also an invitation to move back and forth through
the vibrating resonances left by the experience of grief. Many figures within
this archive address this connection across time: time machines, cross-dressing,
songs, pictures, films, testimonies; they are all forms of rehearsing trauma in
the present. They also speak about the non-normative forms of pleasure that
have emerged through experiences of loss. In so doing, they have infused the
future with new hopes and desires. Within this expanded sense of time, a dif-
ferent affective horizon can be envisaged. My project has attempted to give an
account of these potentialities.
Act 4: Queremos mamy apap a (‘We want Mum and Dad’)
On 15 July 2010 same-sex marriage became legal in Argentina: the National Con-
gress established equal rights to marry for all couples, regardless of their sexual
orientation or gender identity. The new law also gave the same rights to adopt
children. Argentina thus became the first Latin American country to legislate on
this issue. In the days leading up to the passing of the law, different demonstrations
took place in front of the Congress. The first united the detractors, who were led
by Jorge Bergoglio, then Archbishop of Buenos Aires and from March 2013
elected as pope. On that occasion Bergoglio described same-sex marriage as an
initiative ‘inspired by the Devil’. The future pope called for a ‘war for God’ to
safeguard against “serious injury against the family’.'? Following on from this, on
13 July thousands of Catholic, evangelist and conservative groups, dressed in or-
ange, lit candles and prayed against what they deemed an atrocity. The demonstra-
tors defended their repudiation with one main argument: they contended that
children ought to have both parental figures at home as the condition of a healthy
12 In the original the Archbishop Beroglio referred to ‘a movida del demonio’ and called for
“Guerra de Dios’. See Horacio Verbitsky, ‘La Inquisicion’, Pagina 12, 11 July 2010, http://www.
paginal 2.com.ar/diario/elpais/1-149246-2010-07-11.html [accessed 25 November 2011].

CONCLUSION Ney,
environment. This claim eventually led to the adoption of a unified slogan, which
was iterated through songs, flags and postcards: Queremos mamd y papa (‘We want
Mum and Dad’). The seemingly naive tag-line not only introduced a disturbing
regressive conception of the family into the public sphere, but also confirmed how
conservative demands often tend to be enunciated in the name of the child. This is
something that the queer scholar Lee Edelman addresses as part of his critique of
what he calls a “futuristic reproductive politics’, one that aims at safeguarding the
reproduction of heteronormative narratives." Against the advocacy of the securing
figure of the child, Edelman claims a ‘nonteleological negativity’, one that can
break both normative times and politics, and may have no future."
I suggest that a curious example of this negativity could be witnessed in
Congress Square the day after the above demonstration. During a cold winter
night, a new wave of demonStrators waited for the approval of the same-sex
marriage law until the early hours of the following morning. As the chain of
hurrahs, embraces and kisses spilled across the multitude, one image remained
stuck in my mind. Among hundreds of demonstrators, I could spot some H.I.J.O.S.
activists. The organisation of the children of the disappeared had come to sup-
port the equal marriage law and brought a peculiar flag: ‘We want Mum and
Dad’ was written on it. The slogan was surprising. It sounded like a joke, a dark
joke. By addressing their orphaned condition through a humorous twist, H.I.J.O.S.
showed how a seemingly naive appellation of the ‘normal’ family hid a dark
and disfigured face. The flag echoed the conservative demand raised by the
detractors but this time to subvert it. Violence had marked the entire society,
even at its most unexpected borders.
H.I.J.O.S.’ flag performed a form of ‘nonteological negativity’, as advocated
by Edelman. The playful and dark slogan uttered by the descendants effec-
tively pointed towards a crucial displacement. It showed how the discussion of
equal marriage took place in a society in which traditional kinship ties had been
broken by violence. As much as the descendants could no longer recover their
missing parents, ‘the Mum-and-Dad family’ demanded by the conservatives
was no longer viable. At a deeper level, the flag also denounced how, in the
Church’s eyes, only some families seemed to be worthy of life. Ultimately, their
flag proposed a subtle process of transference. Since ‘the Mum-and-Dad fam-
ily’ is no longer the normative unit, to some extent we were all sons and daugh-
ters of the disappeared that night. Traditional life-narratives had been displaced.
While calling for the impossible, H.I.J.0.S. showed how the scene of the ‘nor-
mal’ family did not fit the affective ties currently in play in Argentina. The
heritage left by the dictatorship had re-entered the realm of the social while
proposing an unconventional exchange between sexuality and politics. In this
e
13 Lee Edelman in Dinshaw, Edelman and Ferguson, ‘Theorizing Queer Temporalities’, p. 181.
14 Dinshaw, Edelman and Ferguson, ‘Theorizing Queer Temporalities’, p. 195.

158 CECILIA SOSA
way, the flag demonstrated the extent to which questions around equal marriage
could not be isolated to local, or even global, LGBT groups, but rather had to
be discussed in the context of ac ountry emerging from loss. The flag waved by
H.1.J.O.S. also suggested an unforeseen encounter between public forms of
intimacy and the heritage of violence."
Still, H.I.J.O.S.’ surprising intervention in the field of sexual politics was
enhanced by another fact. Once again, by stressing their orphaned condition,
the descendants depicted themselves as the victims; they performed the role of
the wretched. Through their dark statement, H.I.J.O.S.’ members made fun of
themselves. In so doing, they confirmed how laughter worked as a form of
political empowerment. Moreover, the ‘Mum-and-Dad’ flag also acknowledged
and displaced the main tension that had been encrypted within the group since
the mid-1990s. If by this time dark humour worked as a main platform for
survival, in an surreptitious manner it also tended to confirm the ‘privileging’
of the victims. During that early period, the descendants were the only ones who
could laugh about it. By contrast, their intervention at the same-sex marriage
demonstration showed the extent to which something had changed. This time
the descendants were not the only ones authorised to laugh about their ‘orphaned’
condition. Rather, they transferred this power to all those in the streets, making
laughter available to others. In this sense, their flag could be conceived of as a
gift to the wider society. Thus, the ‘Mum-and-Dad’ flag also endorsed the pro-
cess of transference at stake in contemporary Argentina."
As I explored in Chapter 6, Los topos also contributed to break this biologi-
cal normativity. While bringing new vocabularies to think about the queer en-
gagements emerging from loss, Bruzzone’s novella also proposed a change of
attire and opened alternative spaces for laughter. Moreover, the fiction created
by an ‘orphan’ provided the audience with new figures of sexual citizenship that
help to address more fluid arrangements between heritage and loss. If not all
body transformation counts as a disruption of normativity, the gender transitions
experienced by the characters in Los topos can be read as an attempt to displace
the power of blood. Moreover, the breast enhancements gained by the queer son
metonymically contest the figure of the Madres as the exclusive female heroines
of the aftermath of violence. In this way, Bruzzone brings to light new trans-
heroes for the aftermath of violence.
There is a picture that is illustrative oft his. It is almost an installation. Prob-
ably as part of a marketing strategy, a copy of Bruzzone’s book was placed in
front of ESMA, the former detention camp where the protagonist’s sibling, a
transvestite prostitute, might have been born. (Figure 5, p. 79) The book leans
'S As Carrie Hamilton helped me notice, this queer cross-fertilisation of non-heteronormative
and memory politics was also confirmed by the presence of transgender rights groups during the
demonstration on the ‘National Day of Memory’ on 24 March in 2011, 2012 and 2013.
'6 Tam grateful to Carrie Hamilton for her invaluable contribution in developing this argument.

CONCLUSION 159
against the fence of the former detention camp. From the cover, the protagonist
stares at his readers. He has a wicked figure and a stubborn gaze. It looks as if
he were trying to seduce (and corrupt) both readers and the spectres that come
from behind the fence of the former detention camp. The queer son inhabits a
strange temporality, one that seems to ‘touch’ across time. He has dressed up
for the occasion: he wears an elegant detective suit, which reminds me of the
old coat that the filmmaker Prividera wore to uncover the fate of his missing
mother. The protagonist seems to be inviting the readers to join in this trip. Thus,
Los topos invents a new sense of humour for post-dictatorship Argentina, one
that displays sardonic teeth to spitefully mock narratives of victimisation. This
variant sense of the comical demonstrates how the experience of loss can be
travestied by laughter. Like H.I.J.O.S.’ flag, Los topos’ trans-heroes show how
global debates concerning sexual politics adopt new (and queer) attires as soon
as they intersect with the Argentine lineage of loss. The power of blood has also
been queered. It shows the extent to which the aftermath of violence has opened
space for new forms of belonging and becoming. This inter-sectional approach
is also the one that I have tried to delineate throughout the pages of this book.
Act 5: Families of choice
This book has proposed an insertion of intimate stories within larger public
histories. Following Lauren Berlant’s argument that ‘the autobiographical is not
the personal’, I have shown how the fragile material that informs private lives
can also emerge as a performative zone of encounter for broader publics." In this
sense, the cases that inform this archive demonstrate how reworking personal
stories from a performance studies’ perspective can shed light on a collective
process of transmission. This collection encompasses the interviews conducted
with H.I.J.O.S., the stoties of injury that inform the documentaries explored in
Chapter 3, the testimonies that were part of Mi vida después and also the twisted
autobiography proposed by Los topos. In all their disparate forms, these materi-
als show how different forms of biography can also craft and make available
non-normative acts of mourning.
My project has also called attention to the modes of affection that have
emerged outside conventional forms of intimacy. Again, throughout the six
chapters, various modes of these assemblages have been imagined. From the
closed community enacted by H.I.J.O.S. in the 1990s to the contested and ulti-
mately futile couple formed by the Madre Hebe de Bonafini and her ‘adopted
son’ Sergio Schoklender, this project has introduced, in disparate circumstanc-
es and for limited periods of time, different forms of queer bonds and, ulti-
mately, ‘families of choice’.
e
17 Lauren Berlant, The Female Complaint (Durham, NC: Duke University Press, 2008), p. vil.

160 CECILIA SOSA
A particular story has underlain this archive at different stages, mainly
during the analysis of Lola Arias’s production Mi vida después. More than
an individual story, it is the story of two siblings and their appropriator-father:
Vanina Falco, daughter of the police officer Luis Falco, and her abducted
non-biological brother, Juan Cabandié. I suggest that the bond constituted
between these siblings not only exposes how much, in Butler’s terms, bodies
are always given over to others, but also shows how loss and precariousness
are always allocated differentially.'* For these siblings, Luis Falco played
different but ultimately also overlapping roles; he was an intimate repressor.
Still, their strategies for dealing with this exposure to risk have been diverse.
As | argued in Chapter 5, Vanina’s life experienced a dramatic turn when she
discovered that her father was also the abductor of her supposed biological
brother. ‘When I knew about Juan my reaction was worse than when I had
to confront my father about my sexual decision,’ she stated in a interview.”
Nevertheless, the siblings’ bond was strengthened by these circumstances.
To some extent, their attachment was constituted despite blood, or rather,
coming up against blood. Both siblings experienced a transition in front of
Falco’s figure: Cabandié had to rebuild his life as the abducted son and
Vanina her life as the ‘deviant’ daughter.*°
By Mi vida después’ last performance, Falco was already judged. Follow-
ing Vanina’s testimony, on 18 May 2011 he was sentenced to 18 years’ im-
prisonment, the harshest punishment that had ever been handed down to an
appropriator.*' ‘The saddest thing is that he’s still my dad,’ said the actor on
stage.” Vanina’s case introduces a tension that cannot easily be avoided. While
exploring how bodies come into being, Butler contends that ‘this obtrusive
alterity against which the body finds itself is surely linked to that primary
dependency which is before and against our will’.** Vanina defines herself in
tension with the primary repudiated figure of her father. Biological kinship
appears to her as something that she cannot get rid of and that, to some extent,
leaves her helpless and exposed to violence. Against this bond, she comes into
being. Her decision to tell her story, first in front of an audience in a theatrical
venue and then in court, can be conceived as an act of freedom, critique and
18 Judith Butler, ‘Remarks on “Queer Bonds”, GLQ: A Journal of Lesbian and Gay Studies,
17.2-3 (2011), 381-7 (p. 382).
19 ‘Cuando se desaté lo de Juan tuve una reaccién epidérmica peor que cuando tuve que
enfrentar a mi padre por lo de mi decision sexual’ (my translation). See Paula Giménez, ‘A la iz-
quierda del Padre’, Pagina 12, 19 March 2010, http://www.paginal 2.com.ar/diario/suplementos/
soy/1-1283-2010-03-21.html [accessed 3 December 2011].
20 For an expanded analysis of Vanina Falco’s relationship with her father, see Sosa, ‘Queer-
ing Kinship’.
21 Ginzberg, ‘Con la condena mas alta para un apropiador’.
22 Arias, My Life After, n.p.
23 Butler, ‘Remarks on “Queer Bonds”, p. 384.

CONCLUSION 161
responsibility. It also refers to the uncertain feeling of being injured and put
at risk by violence. Her case provides a rich example of how, in Butler’s terms,
the possibility of ‘giving an account of one self is always a form of putting
oneself at risk in order to confront power.” Still, Vanina’s repudiated relation
with her father also animates a sense of duty. Her decision to bear testimony
against him shaped her conditions of survival. As Butler writes, ‘If survival
depends not so much on the policing of a boundary but on recognizing how
we are bound up with others, then this means we have to conceptualize the
body in the field of politics.’* In the context of the bloodline normativity that
has marked Argentina’s aftermath of violence, Vanina’s testimony can be
conceived as a non-normative act of grief. It witnesses her transformation
before loss. In a parallel way, my project has also attempted to shed light on
those queer bonds that condition new forms of sociality and survival.
Towards the end of Mi vida después, the actors sit on a big sofa. Vanina is
in the middle. In her hands she has the records of the trial that her brother ini-
tiated against her father: the forged ID, the forged birth certificate, DNA
analysis, the police file and legal statements. In this gripping context, the seem-
ingly irrelevant fact of the actors sitting together on the same couch came to
my attention. They actors were all very close to each other. In an uncanny way
the choreographic disposition of the scene reminded me of a family picture.
Still, that ‘picture’ did not correspond to any traditional album. The crew en-
acted a spectral community that helped to conceive a broader idea of being
affected by violence. By stressing the ‘estrangement’ of performing their own
stories, the actors welcomed the audience in a narrative of mourning that
hosted unexpected affiliations emerging from loss.
More than a restrictive community of relatives, the reworking of kinship
that I have proposed within these pages takes the form of an impossible com-
munity. This community follows a queer historical impulse; it gathers past and
present, loss and pleasure. This assemblage of flesh, costumes and time not
only acts as Vanina’s condition of survival but also that of all those who might
feel touched by the uneasy winds of the past, and allows them to mobilise
unexpected encounters. Through Arias’s production, Vanina’s experience of
injury was also offered to the audience. To some extent, her experience of be-
ing hurt emerged as the path that constituted a new ‘us’ in the context of spec-
tatorship. This ‘us’ ultimately refers to all those second-hand witnesses who
could share and be transformed by trauma in the space of performance. More
broadly, this book foregrounds affective communication and identification as
being of central importance in processes of community building.
°
24 Butler, Giving an Account of Oneself, p. 23.
25 Butler, ‘Remarks on “Queer Bonds”’, p. 385.

162 CECILIA SOSA
Act 6: Expanded kinship and non-normative forms of happiness
Still, Vanina’s case also establishes an important distinction that helps me to
frame further my approach to the ‘happy narratives’ staged by the ‘wounded
family’. In her critique of normative forms of happiness, Ahmed notices that
these modes of gratification seem to involve ‘a narrative of assimilation in the
specific sense of becoming like’. In a similar way, the biological narratives
supported by the relatives of the missing have also relied on a form of as-
similation. For instance, for the Abuelas, the idea of happiness follows the
promised future in which the missing grandchildren will be recovered. By
contrast, I would suggest that Vanina and her non-biological brother’s pos-
sibilities of joy do not follow a straight but a queer line. Their experience of
injury provided them with an alternative script, different from the ‘wounded
family’. This narrative emerges not as condemnation, but rather as an affective
life-world beyond the dictates of blood.
The cases explored in this book also suggest a model of happiness which
does not follow a straight line, but rather an oblique or twisted form of wil-
fulness. They propose a self-created foundling narrative that illuminates
unconventional forms of love and hope. Recently, both queer and kinship
studies have been interested in identifying alternative kinship narratives,
which ‘are not organized by the desire for reproduction, or the desire to be
like other families’, as Ahmed frames it.*’ This project also contributes in
that direction. To be precise, most of the cases gathered in this archive sug-
gest a reworking of kinship that follows a different path from ‘being like’.
Indeed, they are stories constituted before or against blood, which suggest
new attachments built from disorientation and loss. From their unexpected
circumstances they point towards non-biological communities that can help
to stage a new sense of being together.
Thus, the stories included in this archive embody a disobedient energy that
also pursues unconventional forms of gratification and pleasure. They speak
about “queer forms of relationality’ that rethink kinship as a form of friendship,
as Lisa Blackman suggests in her important research on affects and performance.”8
As she also argues, these different forms of non-normative relationality are
‘central to a politics of re-invention’.” In a similar way, this book also attempts
to reinvent a politics of mourning for Argentine society. For that reason, I have
been interested in forms of dealing with trauma that do not put the past behind,
but rather allow ‘queer forms of becoming’ in the face of grief, to borrow again
26 Ahmed, The Promise of Happiness, p. 112.
27 Ahmed, The Promise of Happiness, p. 114.
28 Blackman, ‘Affect, Performance and Queer Subjectivities’, p. 183.
29 Blackman, ‘Affect, Performance and Queer Subjectivities’, p. 188.

CONCLUSION 163
Blackman’s expression.’ In this sense, the form of ‘queerness’ that animates
my project has been a critical methodology for exploring the creative process
of ‘working through’ the scars left by trauma; one which can also shed light on
alternative futures.
Throughout this book, I have looked for alternative forms of sociality that
may exist outside, around and beyond the engagements proposed by the ‘wound-
ed family’. In this process, I have relied on the force of queerness, both as
method and non-normative form of attachment. Maybe like Los topos at a fic-
tional level, the case of the non-biological siblings brings both paths together.
In so doing, it contributes to tie the materials that inform my archive. These
lives that come to be bound to each other under enforced conditions may also
bring a sense of reparation in the face of loss.
The cases included here show how the past is not rigid, but malleable and
textured, written from the affective investments of the present, almost like
clothes. They bring to the foreground a language dressed by loss. This language
names the insupportable but also the expanded ties to deal with it. It ultimately
exposes how much bodies depend on others as the condition for their own sur-
vival. I suggest that this language of loss has been put in circulation, and is now
available to share. My archive witnesses a similar attempt. It aims at providing
an alternative language for loss, one that could frame the conditions of an ex-
panded social survival, even among less implicated beholders.
Act 7: A true match
In Argentina the struggles of memory and heritage have risked being ‘purified’
in terms of blood. In this book I have tried to contest this narrative through a
queer approach. In so doing, I have proposed a deterritorialisation of the traces
of trauma from the realms of the biological family. In fact, the cases included
in my archive constitute an attempt to demonstrate the emergence of alternative
conditions of belonging. They show how the responsibility of remembering has
moved to the ‘open society’. Still, this rephrased domiciliation of grief also risks
being misunderstood. Affective reparation does not occur on an individual
level, but as a collective impulse that seeks the adoption of trauma in the present.
While exploring the recent power of a ‘queer liberalism’ in the US, David Eng
argues that ‘unlike political theories of reparation, which seek to write history
into a definitive past, psychic reparation does not delineate such a finite process,
but rather a process of working through’.* ! In the context of my project, ‘affec-
tive reparation’ accounts for the opportunities of ‘working though’ the local
process of grief as an expanded form of kinship. In this sense, the cases in-
e
30 Blackman, ‘Affect, Performance and Queer Subjectivities’, p. 193.
31 Eng, The Feeling of Kinship, p. 196 (Eng’s emphasis).

164 CECILIA SOSA
cluded in my archive show the various ways in which different modes ofa ffec-
tive reparation are already taking place. In fact, this process of ‘working through’
the heritages left by the dictatorship has staged a rephrased form of sociality,
which also rethinks melancholia.
This idea of reparation delineates the grounds of ethics as not relying on
individual subjects but on a new collective. Indeed, the materials included in
my archive show how individual stories coming back from loss are always con-
nected and dependent on others. In this sense, it is worth recalling the experience
of the survivor of a detention centre who found himself touched by Arias’s
performance. His laughter demonstrates how being exposed to others can bring
surprising encounters. While it may be impossible to know in advance how
bodies can be affected by pain or injury, the cases presented in this book have
shown how the fact of being twisted and turned by violence might also bring
hope for the future and suggest unforeseen responses to histories that are nev-
ertheless unfinished. In fact, these cases also show that it is possible to move
away from pure states of injury.
In an interview for the BBC, Vanina Falco said that the figure of her father
reminded her of a scar that she has after a childhood accident.** She also men-
tioned that she learned how to put make-up on it. Vanina’s wound will remain,
even as a drained scar. Some make-up might still be needed. This make-up can
be conceived as a new texture, a sort of new outfit, another layer in the costume
of grief. It literally enacts a way of ‘working through’ the effects of trauma, one
that shows that affective reparation is possible. This ‘true match’ between mourn-
ing and the unconventional pleasures emerged from loss has also been at the
foreground here.
Act 8: Epilogue
In Buenos Aires, next to the rio de la Plata, the Parque de la Memoria com-
memorates the victims of the dictatorship. There is a monument carrying the
names of the missing, and also a park of sculptures. They were selected in an
open international competition, which included 665 artistic projects coming
from 44 different countries. Although the project to build the park was approved
in 1998, it was only inaugurated in 2007. As in the case of ESMA, the park
faced years of resistance, delays and struggles among the authorities of the city
and the nation and internal splits among human rights activists and relatives.*?
32, See BBC Mundo, 25 March 2010, http://www.bbc.co.uk/mundo/america_lati-
na/2010/02/100126_mandamientos_honraras_padres_mz.shtml [accessed 7 November 2010].
33 These disputes mainly concerned whether the riverbanks could be the ‘proper’ site of
commemoration for the disappeared, and the fact that the names included on the monument are
far fewer than 30,000, the iconic number that the relatives have flagged since the 1980s. In addi-
tion, one sector of the Madres rejected the monument by arguing that it might appear as an un-

CONCLUSION 165
While the official purpose of the park is to keep memory alive for future
generations, Vikki Bell and Mario Di Paolantonio suggest that it sustains an
experience of the ‘impossible’. They argue that whereas the massive monument
with the names of the missing stands as an ‘open wound’, by. contrast the
sculptures ‘refute the logics of equivalence and reconciliation, evoking instead
the unsettled questions that still linger in the present’.** Drawing on this, I sug-
gest that these installations embody an affective appellation for the audience.
Rather than simply representing loss, perilously located in front ofa river-tomb,
they offer an opportunity for the adoption of trauma in the present. In so doing,
they show how the work of mourning is always negotiated in the encounter
with their beholders.
Among the dozen sculptures that were finally placed in the Parque de la
Memoria, the final act of my project emerges in the encounter with Dennis
Oppenheim’s Monumento al escape (2001), one of the first installations to be
located at the park. This artwork could be conceived as the story of my archive.
It is also the story of a house that has been put ‘under arrest’. Oppenheim’s
piece comprises three huge boxes of steel that have been placed one on top of
the other. It composes a home-space built of cell blocks, lights and large
openings.* A strange radiance illuminates the blocks of concrete when the
light passes through the roofs built of red acrylic. The effect is uncanny. It
looks as if the split house were trying to find a new balance on those unstable
grounds (Figure 6, p. 80).
While exploring Oppenheim’s piece, Bell and Di Paolantonio suggest that
the ambiguity of shape and colour, and the oscillation between comfort and
confining, are addressed by the title: Monument to Escape. ‘A monument to
escape from whom?, one might ask, since so many did not escape; but perhaps
this is the monument one has to escape, the prison of the past from which the
society must escape.’**'I would like to propose another a story for Oppenheim’s
piece, one different from escape. This house addresses the forceful changes
that the dictatorship operated in the realm of kinship. By turning the home-
space upside down, Monumento al escape manages to expose how family ties
have been vividly transfigured during the post-dictatorship period. The build-
ing that stands in the park does not adjust. It looks strange, not entirely safe.
This house has been disoriented, and yet it has not collapsed. Another balance
has been reinvented.
wanted cemetery for unburied bodies. For further details on this, see Vikki Bell and Mario Di
Paolantonio, ‘The Haunted Nomos: Activist-Artists and the (Im)possible Politics of Memory in
Transitional Argentina’, Cultural Politics, 5.2 (2009), 149-79.
34 Bell and Di Paolantonio, ‘The Haunted Nomos’, p. 62.
35 See http://parquedelamemoria.org.ar/parque-ing/nominados/dennisoppen.htm [accessed 2
December 2010].
36 Bell and Di Paolantonio, “The Haunted Nomos’, p. 62.

166 CECILIA SOSA
More than offering space to something that is already there, Monumento al
escape creates an uneasy, edgy demand in my presence. Its awkward shape ap-
pears before me bearing a sort of magical power. Standing in front of these
overlapped boxes, touching its rough concrete, I reckon that Oppenheim’s piece
works as an ‘affective architecture’, the material enactment of those alternative
ties emerging from grief. The twisted installation offers asylum, a transitional
home for those non-normative feelings of kinship that emerged in the face of
violence, which have not yet been fully articulated. The house has been opened.
It embraces expanded possibilities. It holds new forms of intimacy.
Moreover, these non-biological engagements unseat the figure of the ‘rela-
tives’ as the only victims oft he dictatorship. In dialogue with Derrida’s Archive
Fever, | suggest that Monumento al escape shows how a new domiciliation of
the archive of mourning was possible. The building hosts another lineage, one
that is not commanded by blood. While bearing witness to a new form of to-
getherness, this home-space transposes the law of the archive. It proposes the
recovery of a house which used to be ‘under arrest’.
The river works as the stage. It announces other coasts. Located in that pub-
lic space, the house has been recovered for more residents. The house is inhab-
ited by spectres; they are faceless, coming both from the past and the future.
They do not propose reconciliation. They do not adjust to the duty of memory.
They speak about loss, grief and love. They claim an ‘impossible’ politics of
mourning, one that can offer hospitality to different communities across time.
The house is both a shelter and an orphanage. It bears witness to those indi-
rectly affected by violence. Thus, Oppenheim’s piece shows how the house of
mourning has been occupied by an expanded sense of care. This awkward,
uncomfortable house also hosts my project. It gives shelter to the materials that
inform my archive. They are stories built through the rehearsal of trauma, both
in theatre and in life. A new story can always emerge at the juncture of grief. It
is also my house now.
I step inside. There are no guardians at the entrance. There are no doors to
lock. There is a vacant space. I can hear a mournful dog barking. There are
clothes everywhere. They are mostly old but there are also new outfits. There
are blonde wigs on top of a pile of fabric, ready to be picked out. The table is
laid. There is some extra crockery waiting for unexpected guests. New genera-
tions of orphans can come inside ... and maybe laugh.

Afterword
While presenting my work at a conference in Cambridge at the beginning of
2012, I was accused of being an ‘infiltrator’.' ‘Who are you to challenge the
experiences of those who suffered if there were no victims in your family?’ The
accusation came from a daughter of disappeared parents who was in the audi-
ence. The young woman went on to say that my intervention reminded her of a
short story written by Julio Cortazar in which an old lady spends her days at-
tending vigils she has not been invited to.” ‘All the more in Argentina’s case,’
my accuser said, ‘when there are no bodies to be mourned.’ To some extent, she
was right. This book proposes a non-normative reading of Argentina’s post-
dictatorship to explore the unconventional forms of intimacy that have emerged
in the aftermath of violence. In this search, my perspective has always been that
of a ‘deviant daughter’ with no credentials whatsoever; an ‘infiltrator’ of the
bloodline narratives of grief.
My argument does not mean to be a mere justification for ‘infiltrators’ to
engage with painful and complex processes they have not directly experi-
enced. After all, we are all strangers to our ‘objects’ and somehow always
de-centred by them. Yet since that conference in 2012, the process of circu-
lation and dissemination of Argentina’s experience of loss has not stopped
countersigning itself. To some extent, scripts have changed. Nowadays there
are more and more ‘infiltrators’. These enfolded processes of countersigna-
ture have not only involved the true ‘orphans’. Rather, other voices have
emerged. In many ways, a shared sense of ownership towards the traumatic
past is being kindled.
There have been political reasons for this. As I have argued throughout this
book, in the first decade of the twenty-first century a new scenario emerged
in Argentina. President Néstor Kirchner’s inaugural speech in 2003 acted as
1 The conference took place on 13-14 January 2012 and was entitled *Visualising Violence:
Art, Memory and Dictatorship in Latin America’. I presented a preliminary version of the last
chapter of this book, mainly my work on Los Topos, Bruzzone’s novella.
2 The short story in question is ‘Los buenos servicios’ (The Good Services), which is in-
cluded in Las armas secretas (1959). In fact, in Cortazar’s story the old widow is hired to attend
the vigil of a man, who she eventually discovers she had known beforehand. With this discovery
her sorrow becomes real rather than just performed.

168 CECILIA SOSA
the official launching oft his alternative lineage: ‘We are the sons and daugh-
ters of the Mothers and Grandmothers of Plaza de Mayo.’ Self-invested as the
‘son’, the president showed how the heritages of loss were not restricted to a
biological family of victims, but rather could be adopted by more ‘illegitimate’
witnesses. Within my research I have read this displacement as a form of
queering of the bloodline normativity of the ‘wounded family’. This ex-
panded — and somehow queer — version of kinship has been one of the sur-
reptitious promises of the period, iterated and reinforced during Cristina
Fernandez de Kirchner’s administration.
The past decade has also witnessed the emergence a new lineage of activists:
La Campora. Created by Maximo Kirchner, the son of the presidential couple,
in 2003, the youngest branch of the official party gained political relevance
after Néstor Kirchner’s death in 2010. The young militants proposed a form of
heritage via political conversion. President Cristina embraced them as the new
orphans and also as an impure cast of heirs. For a time the mother-widow and
her new cast of children enacted a ‘happy’ family in mourning. The comforting
and reassuring figure of the dead president — transfigured into ‘EL’ (‘HE’) and
the styled image of Néstornauta — remained always in the background.’ Nowa-
days, however, this reloaded version of the wounded family seems to be facing
discredit. Although many Campora activists still retain key roles in state com-
panies, government departments, ministries and Congress, their public support
has been considerably undermined. This has also affected the reputation of Juan
Cabandié, Kirchner’s favourite ‘adopted’ son, who has been accused of obtain-
ing advantages from his ‘orphaned’ condition.* The ‘privileges’ and also the
boundaries of invoking the ‘disappeared-parents card’ came to be increasingly
disputed during 2013.
Following the mid-term elections in October 2013, I realised that many of
the discussions explored within these pages are likely to change. With her
party defeated in key districts, Fernandez de Kirchner’s dream of being re-
elected in 2015 for a third term seems to have come to an end. A renewed faction
of the Peronists, led by Sergio Massa, Cristina’s former cabinet chief, gained
44 per cent of the vote. His figure represents a market-friendly economy, argu-
ably less interested in memory issues. Right-wing candidates leave even less
3 La Campora has used as one of its main symbols the figure of Néstornauta, a stylised
image of the protagonist of E/ Eternatuta (1957-59), the classic comic by Oesterheld and Solano
Lopez, with his face replaced by that of Néstor Kirchner.
4 Although the episode took place at the beginning of the year, it was only made public two
wecks before the mid-term elections in October 2013. A video portrayed the abducted grandchild
and current legislator of the City of Buenos Aires engaged in a dispute with a female police of-
ficer. At one point during the quarrel, Cabandié brought up his status as being the son of disap-
peared parents to escape a ticket. Broadcast by the opposition media, the video went viral. The
arguably minor episode resulted in Cabandié’s public political dishonour, which also had a
negative impact on the elections.

AFTERWORD 169
space for hope. Yet following a month of mandatory rest after minor brain
surgery that had been performed two weeks before the elections, Cristina reap-
peared in November 2013 without the mourning outfit that she had worn for
three years and proclaimed a ‘deepening of the model’. In this vein, she reshuf-
fled her cabinet, giving the starring role as the Minister of Economy to the
Camporist Axel Kiciloff.
The Kirchnerist period has been called the ‘won decade’. Many accomplish-
ments have been achieved in the field of memory, as well as in social and
economic sectors, including the universal child allowance (a stipend for poor
families), same-sex marriage and gender transition laws, and the controversial
media law.° Although my perspective has been critical in relation to the nu-
ances of these processes, it has also been nurtured and embedded by them.
This book shows the intensity of this political cycle. During the final revisions
of the manuscript, I learned that I had to be faithful to its tone. Somehow,
these pages bring to light the flows, textures, affectivities and also the tem-
perature of the Kirchnerist period. After all, this book is a rebellious, and
perhaps infiltrated, child of the ‘won decade’. Hopefully, it will help to illu-
minate future forms of memory activism.
Lineages to-come
This book signposts interstitial political spaces where alternative lineages can
be created and re-enacted in unforeseen ways. As mentioned before, the experi-
ences gathered in this archive have no masters. Moreover, they have already
been revealed to have multiple gatekeepers. An upcoming artistic and intel-
lectual series points in this direction. Como enterrar a un padre desaparecido
(2012), a fictional memoir by Sebastian Hacher, exposes an appealing entangle-
ment between the figure of a daughter who seeks to efface her missing father
and a ‘non-affected’ author who adopts her voice.’ In Una muchacha muy bella,
a wonderful first-person novella, Julian Lopéz creates a mysteriously nuanced
love-story between a child and his — later-to-be-disappeared — mother.’ In both
cases, a vicarious adoption of the ‘identity of the child’ emerges as a political
(rather than merely literary) strategy of empowerment.
5 See ‘Cristina Kirchner: “Hay que seguir profundizando el modelo”’, La Nacion, 20 No-
vember 2113, http://www.lanacion.com.ar/1640091-cristina-kirchner-hay-que-seguir-profundi-
zando-el-modelo [accessed 1 December 2013].
6 On 28 October 2013, the day after the elections, the Supreme Court of Justice declared the
Broadcast Media Law constitutional. The decision came to be read as a win for the government,
which for more than four years had fought to restrict the power of media conglomerates. Eventu-
ally, Clarin, the main voice of opposition, was obliged to comply with the law.
7 Sebastian Hacher, Cémo enterrar a un padre desaparecido (Buenos Aires: Marea Edito-
rial, 2012).
8 Julian Lopez, Una muchacha muy bella (Buenos Aires: Eterna Cadencia, 2013).

170 CECILIA SOSA
While bringing to light new vocabularies and images to relate to suffering
and loss, the younger generation have also hosted wider audiences within non-
normative narratives that intersect, twist and displace conventional acts of
mourning. Sometimes relying on playful and ironic language, this production
has offered non-victimising accounts of trauma that have also confronted the
official duty of memory. Although the figure of the broken family has remained
insistently present within this series, traditional bloodline ties have re-emerged
strangely displaced, mocked and even subverted.’ For the new generation, fa-
milial inscriptions work as a form of countersignature and also as a mode of
transitioning into more expanded forms of kinship.
The use of a dark sense of humour has contributed to highlighting this process
of countersignature. If during the 1990s humour provided the descendants with
a form of collective comfort to mourn their missing parents, in the new period
the reverberating properties of joy propagated new lines of identification and
empathy among wider audiences. In Diario de una Princesa Montonera. 110%
verdad (2012), Mariana Eva Perez accomplished a novel twist to break the
unquestioned solemnities attached to the ‘little issue’ of the disappeared; ‘e/
temita’, as the author calls it. The ‘110% truth’ subtitle showcases the fictional
and playful surplus from which the experience of a young orphan who was
expelled from the Abuel/as organisation is contested. When I first interviewed
Perez in April 2009 she seemed to me sharply wounded and defensive. Since
the publication of her book, she has been increasingly recognised as a celebrat-
ed mind of the ‘post-memory’ generation. In the book, she sardonically thanks
her ‘orphaned’ condition for enabling her to be in the popular TV show A/mor-
zando con Mirtha Legrand, even though, as she writes, ‘by that time, being a
child of the disappeared lacked the current glamour’.' Drawing on this pro-
vocative mood, the book displays a self-deprecating sense of humour to decon-
struct the world of the ‘hijis’ (the children). Not only did she find there a per-
sonal tone for dealing with her own circumstances; her Diary has also allowed
the resonances of trauma to circulate among non-kin beholders. While teasing
the sense of ownership on the part of the descendants, the killjoy Princess also
queers the authority of the ‘blue blood’. In this movement, her Diary stands
both as a counter-act of mourning and also as a public path to healing.
9 In this vein, I have analysed Lola Arias’s performance Mi vida después and Bruzzone’s
autobiographical fiction Los topos. Yet this series also includes the exhibitions Familias O’Heridas
(2011), produced by Jorgelina Molina Planas, Ana Adjiman, Maria Guiffra and Victoria Grigera,
all daughters of the disappeared; Huachos |‘Orphans’| (2011), produced by an artistic branch of
H.1.J.0.S., who defined themselves as ‘orphans scientifically produced by state genocide acts’;
and also Filiacién [‘Filiations’ |( 2013), a photographic collection in which Lucila Quieto decon-
structed her father’s figure. The latter was shown at the ‘Haroldo Conti’ cultural centre located at
ESMA to mark the 2013 anniversary of the military coup.
10 Mariana Eva Perez, Diario de una Princesa Montonera. 110% verdad (Buenos Aires:
Capital Intelectual, 2012), p. 202.

AFTERWORD 171
The process of countersignature has also developed transnational ‘siblings’.
In early 2012 Lola Arias staged a Chilean version of Mi vida después." The
new show, entitled E/ afio que naci (‘The Year | was Born’), features a young
Chilean crew engaging with the traces of Augusto Pinochet’s dictatorship
(1973-90). The production has started to tour Latin America and Europe.
Rather than being a brand-new ‘franchise’, the transnational version inquires
how a theatrical artefact can eventually become an affective vehicle for
‘working through’ wider forms of transmission of trauma in disparate land-
scapes dealing with loss. Meanwhile, Mi vida después has also continued to
circulate overseas. As mentioned before, this piece has acquired the capac-
ity to change alongside the stories enacied on stage. When it was performed
at the Brighton Festival in May 2013, the ID picture of ab aby was projected
on to a big screen. The image showed Simon Lugones, the son of Carla Cre-
spo and Pablo Lugones. The couple met while rehearsing the piece in Buenos
Aires and have very different backgrounds. Carla is the daughter of guer-
rilla activists while Pablo is the twin son of bank employees, seemingly not
directly affected by violence. In October 2013 Simon was on stage, this time
in the flesh. At the last performance in Sao Paulo, Brazil, he played with the
prophetic turtle alongside the other children of the crew. In some way the
new performers can also be thought of as the blissful emergence of a non-
normative lineage.
A more fluid politics of memory
In 2013 Argentina celebrated thirty years of recovered democracy. That year
was also the 36th anniversary of the founding of the Abuelas de Plaza de Mayo.
So far, the group has managed to recover the identity of 110 missing children
of the disappeared. The organisation has recently launched a new publicity
broadcast to bring attention to the 400 adults, kidnapped as children, who still
live under falsified names. In the broadcast a woman holds a newborn baby on
a visit to a consultant. An older woman, presumably her mother, accompanies
them. The specialist wants to know the medical history of the child and also
that of the family. ‘Is this all important?’, complains the young woman while
exchanging odd glances with the older woman, who eventually is suspected of
being her illegal appropriator. ‘No le dejes a tus hijos la herencia de la duda.
Resolvé tu identidad ahora’ (‘Don’t leave your children a heritage of doubt.
Sort out your identity now’) is the final remark of the broadcast.’ The slogan
is consistent with the public strategy of the group. For Abuelas, the ‘right of
11 The new production draws on a workshop for Chilean artists held by Lola Arias during the
2011 staging of the festival ‘Santiago a Mil’, in which local actors began to construct a collective
album of their biographical stories.
12 See http://www.abuelas.org.ar/

LZ CECILIA SOSA
identity’ has always been the main fight. Bloodline ties appear there as the only
true affiliation. Therefore, the broadcast champions a conservative version of
the family, which tends to dismiss, or at least overlook, experiences of fertili-
sation and egg-donation, let alone other non-normative attachments which
endorse expanded forms of reproduction, love and care. Although the demands
of kidnapped children and those of same-sex couples cannot be strictly com-
pared, the way in which these tensions come to be imagined together will
probably configure the future of both fights. In fact, this queer cross-fertilisation
of non-heteronormative and memory politics has also been confirmed by the
increasing presence of LGBT groups during the demonstration on the ‘Na-
tional Day of Memory’ during the past few years. Indeed, alongside the expan-
sion of rights to traditionally marginalised communities, the potentialities of
these wide-ranging imbrications of spheres have been further deepened during
the current period.
New affectivities on stage
The institutionalisation of former sites of repression and political terror has
coincided from 2006 with the reopening of trials that prosecute the perpetra-
tors of human rights violations. This process encompasses broader debates
around affective reparation, justice and responsibility that go beyond immedi-
ate victims and survivors, and which prompt a sort of excess that cannot be
reduced to legal trials. Many of these effervescent processes resonate nowadays
within the so-called spaces of memory. They have become privileged land-
scapes in which to explore embodied forms of transmission of trauma.
At the beginning of 2013 another controversial episode cut across the
Argentine media. It was known that to celebrate the end of 2012, the Minis-
ter of Justice, Julio Alak, had hosted an asado (barbecue) at the former ESMA.
The party took place at H.I.J.O.S.’ headquarters, La casa de la militancia,
and it was attended by more than a thousand ministry employees. For many,
the celebration was perceived as an arrogant and over-confident gesture by
a government that had appropriated the banner oft he victims as its own nar-
rative. For some oft he relatives of the victims the event appeared especially
offensive, considering that the word asado — or better parrilla — was military
jargon to signify the cremation of prisoners’ corpses during the dictatorship.
In many senses, the official party was perceived as intolerable — or, as one
might say, indigestible.
Yet the episode arrived as the perfect opportunity to consider what kind of
practices of re-occupation former landscapes of torture and death might allow."
!3" [have developed this argument in an article published in the Argentine newspaper Pagina
12. See C. Sosa, “La mesa esta servida’, 6 January 2013, http://www.paginal2.com.ar/diario/el-
pais/1-211301-2013-01-06.html [accessed 1 May 2014].

AFTERWORD 73
Echoing Hebe de Bonafini’s cooking sessions that had taken place at ESMA a
couple of years ago, the asado interrogated the extent to which coming to-
gether around food might work as a public pathway for a collective digestion
of grief. In the context of radicalised divisions, the asado has appeared as fet-
ish and banal. I would still suggest that the spectrum of activities currently
taking place at former spaces of sorrow suggest a new conviviality in the af-
termath of violence. More broadly, spaces of memory have become experimen-
tal stages, sites of practice where different ways of ‘performing life’ in the
present can be tested, adopted and also rejected.
From the field of New Museology is has been argued that a successful pro-
cess of ‘curation’ should contribute to kindling a collective sense of ownership
towards traumatic pasts on the part of multiple communities.'* The intensities
flowing across spaces of memory in Argentina also encourage us to think in
that direction. This process of transmission does not only concern those ‘di-
rectly affected’ by military violence but also seemingly unrelated witnesses
who can also partake in loss. As paradoxical as it sounds, spaces of memory
are alive in the country. They have emerged as the amplified, hectic stages
where new affiliations can be formed.
While considering different images to illustrate the cover of this book, I
was confronted by an intervention that took place at the Parque de la Memo-
ria in Buenos Aires. In August 2013 design students at the University of
Buenos Aires built a series of mannequins using their own bodies as models.
The mannequins were positioned at the borders of the park, just facing the
river. The intervention worked as the official opening of the First National
Design Biennale and surreptitiously became a silent tribute to the disappeared.
Quite by accident, the mannequins offered a fleeting image of the returned.
Yet those who were made to return had also been transformed: they had col-
ourful outfits; they looked refreshed, almost new; collective but still driven
by singular needs and desires. Far from being mere substitutes for those van-
ished bodies, the mannequins brought a promise with them: an expanded form
of care on the part of a new generation.
Like the Siluetazo in 1983, the mannequins only stayed in the park for a day.
Screened by the media, however, they worked as a spark for wider audiences.
While looking at those strange dummies emerging from this landscape of loss,
I thought that somehow they managed to comprise the kind of argument I was
trying to put forward in this book: the extent to which the process of mourning
had managed to bring new corporalities, materialities and affectivities on to the
stage. No image seemed to be more powerful: dummies that were bright, sharp
and somehow alive. Their image now carries the promise of this book.
*
14 See Erica Lehrer, Cynthia E. Milton and Monica Eileen Patterson (eds), Curating Difficult
Knowledge: Violent Pasts in Public Spaces (New York: Palgrave Macmillan, 20M) pao

174 CECILIA SOSA
Issues of legitimacy, authority and ownership are still under discussion in
contemporary Argentina, maybe more than ever. The expansive reverberations
of grief have contributed to generating expanded links among new beholders.
There is a joy involved in this encounter, the surreptitious pleasure of being
together in the aftermath of loss.

Co-winner of the Inaugural AHGBI Sosa successfully redirects
Prize for Best Doctoral.Dissertation scholarship on studies
of memory, trauma, and
mourning by infusing
The aftermath of Argentina's last dictatorship
(1976-1983) has traditionally been associated existing dialogue with a
new theoretical approach
with narratives of suffering, which recall the loss
informed largely by queer
of the 30,000 civilians infamously known as the
studies ... a work of stunning
‘disappeared. When democracy was recovered,
originality. .. a beautifully
the unspoken rule was that only those related
by blood to the missing were entitled to ask for crafted book.
justice. This book both queries and queers this PROFESSOR BRENDA WERTH
bloodline normativity. Drawing on queer theory AMERICAN UNIVERSITY
and performance studies, it develops an alternative
framework for understanding the affective
transmission of trauma beyond traditional family
settings. To do so, it introduces an archive of
non-normative acts of mourning that runs across
different generations. Through the analysis of
a broad spectrum of performances — including
interviews, memoirs, cooking sessions, films, jokes,
theatrical productions and literature — the book
shows how the experience of loss has not only
produced a well-known imaginary of suffering
but also new forms of collective pleasure.
CECILIA SOSA received a PhD in Drama from
Queen Mary, University of London. She ts
currently post-doctoral research fellow at
School of Arts & Digital Industries, University
of East London.
